abstract_pt: 'As aplicações de microsserviços servem milhões de clientes todos os
  dias, muitas vezes executando sistemas críticos para o negócio em software containers
  e ambientes multi-tenancy. A utilização de microsserviços está a crescer rapidamente
  devido à agilidade do desenvolvimento e da distribuição, que é ainda mais facilitada
  pelos contentores, enquanto tecnologia de apoio, que são geridos por plataformas
  de orquestração, como o Kubernetes, que monitorizam, gerem e automatizam a maioria
  das tarefas. Estas ferramentas fornecem mecanismos de failover para aumentar a confiabilidade
  e a segurança das aplicações. No entanto, faltam estudos focados na eficácia de
  abordagens para lidar com falhas ou ataques. Assim, existem preocupações quanto
  à sua confiabilidade e segurança, o que constitui um fator de abrandamento da adoção.A
  deteção de intrusões é uma técnica para identificar actividades maliciosas numa
  máquina ou numa rede que tem sido utilizada para aumentar a segurança dos sistemas.
  O sucesso destes esforços torna-a uma abordagem promissora para aumentar a segurança
  dos microsserviços, mas atualmente enfrenta desafios significativos. Nomeadamente,
  o processamento eficaz de toda a informação produzida pelos serviços, o acompanhamento
  das constantes alterações e a manutenção de um funcionamento eficaz mesmo em ambientes
  tão dinâmicos. Portanto, é extremamente importante desenvolver técnicas que possam
  ser implementadas em cenários reais para manter as aplicações seguras.Este trabalho
  avança o estado da arte sobre a melhoria e avaliação da segurança para aplicações
  de microsserviços implementadas em ambientes escaláveis, elásticos e dinâmicos que
  estão em constante mudança para responder a requisitos e tecnologias plásticas.Dois
  estudos realizados neste contexto demonstram: i) a incapacidade dos atuais mecanismos
  de tolerância a falhas de uma plataforma popular de orquestração de containers em
  detetar falhas causadas por um conjunto diversificado de defeitos de software, o
  que compromete a confiabilidade das aplicações que as utilizam, e ii) a aplicabilidade
  e eficácia da deteção de intrusões com base em system calls que um container utiliza
  durante a sua execução. As conclusões mostraram a necessidade de trabalhar para
  melhorar as actuais abordagens de deteção e resposta a falhas em microsserviços
  e que a deteção de intrusões baseada em dados do sistema é uma técnica promissora
  para melhorar a segurança dos microsserviços.Um grupo de componentes foi desenvolvido
  para melhorar a eficácia de modelos de deteção utilizados em ambientes escaláveis,
  elásticos e em constante mudança. Um conjunto de técnicas permitiu aos modelos de
  deteção monitorizar as réplicas activas de um serviço como um grupo utilizando apenas
  um modelo de deteção, permitindo a sua utilização em vários ambientes operacionais.
  Além disso, propomos três técnicas que permitem obter o comportamento nuclear de
  um microsserviço em modelos de deteção de intrusões e focar nos padrões de comportamento
  base sem perder a eficácia de deteção, aumentando simultaneamente a probabilidade
  de reutilização do modelo em diferentes versões do mesmo microsserviço.Para representar
  a probabilidade de reutilização, propomos uma métrica que utiliza o código-fonte
  e dados recolhidos durante a execução do serviço para calcular a probabilidade de
  um modelo de deteção ser reutilizado para uma nova versão do serviço. Os resultados
  demonstram que as técnicas podem generalizar o conhecimento e melhorar a taxa de
  deteção em ambientes escaláveis e reduzir o tamanho dos modelos de deteção enquanto
  asseguram a sua reutilização em diferentes versões de microsserviços.Este trabalho
  também apresenta um benchmark que considera os desafios e as caraterísticas dos
  ambientes de microsserviços e fornece uma avaliação e comparação representativa
  de modelos de deteção de intrusões com várias configurações. O utilizador do benchmark
  pode usar um dataset de 900 horas com três tipos de dados (treino, teste, validação)
  que gerámos através duma metodologia que garante uma base de avaliação representativa
  e abrangente. Uma instância do benchmark foi criada para avaliar seis algoritmos
  em três casos de estudo, demonstrando a utilidade e a ampla aplicabilidade do benchmark,
  ao mesmo tempo que mostra que os algoritmos de ML têm geralmente um melhor desempenho
  em aplicações que produzem menos dados.O trabalho converge para a plataforma µSherlock,
  que fornece deteção de intrusões contínua e adaptativa que visa evoluir com aplicações
  de microsserviços e fornecer um nível constante de segurança, independentemente
  das alterações e atualizações. Com base na framework implementamos uma ferramenta
  para monitorização contínua de ponto-a-ponto de microsserviços em Kubernetes. O
  µSherlock calcula proativamente métricas para entender se os modelos de deteção
  estão desatualizados e devem ser retreinados. A validação e avaliação do µSherlock
  num cenário dinâmico mostram a sua eficiência e uma deteção eficaz.'
abstract_en: 'Microservices applications serve millions of customers every day, often
  running business-critical systems in software containers and multi-tenant environments.
  The adoption of microservices is growing rapidly due to the agility of development
  and deployment, which is further facilitated by containers, as a supporting technology,
  that are managed by orchestration platforms, such as Kubernetes, monitoring, managing,
  and automating most tasks. These tools provide failover mechanisms to increase the
  dependability and security of the applications. However, studies that focus on the
  effectiveness of such approaches in dealing with failures or attacks are missing.
  As a result, there are concerns about their dependability and security, which studies
  show is a slowing factor in adoption.Intrusion detection is a technique to identify
  malicious activity on a host or network that has been used to increase the security
  of many systems. The success of past efforts makes it a promising approach for enhancing
  the security of microservices, but it currently faces significant challenges. Namely,
  effectively processing all the information produced by the services, keeping up
  with constant changes and releases, and maintaining effective operation even in
  such dynamic environments. Therefore, it is extremely important to develop techniques
  that can be deployed in real-world scenarios to keep applications secure.This thesis
  advances the state-of-the-art on improving and evaluating security for microservice
  applications that are deployed in scalable, elastic, dynamic environment that are
  continuously changing to address plastic requirements and technologies.We present
  two studies that demonstrate: i) the inability of state-of-the-art fault-tolerance
  mechanisms from popular container orchestrator platform in detecting failures caused
  by a diverse set of software faults, which compromises the reliability and dependability
  of the applications that use them, and ii) the applicability and effectiveness of
  host intrusion detection based on the system calls a container uses during its execution.
  We conclude that work was needed to improve current approaches for detecting and
  responding to failures in microservices, and that host-based intrusion detection
  is a promising approach for improving the security of microservices.We propose components
  to improve the detection effectiveness of detection models used in scalable, elastic,
  and continuously changing environments. We devise a set of techniques that allow
  detection models to monitor all active service replicas as a group using a single
  detection model, allowing it to be used in multiple operational deployments. In
  addition, we propose three techniques that allow to obtain the core behavior of
  a microservice into intrusion detection models and focus on core behavior patterns
  without losing detection effectiveness, while increasing the likelihood of model
  reusability across different releases of the same microservice.To represent the
  likelihood of reuse, we propose a metric that uses source code and service runtime
  data to provide the likelihood that a detection model will be reused for a new version
  of the service. The results demonstrate that techniques can generalize knowledge
  and improve the detection rate in scalable environments as well as reduce the size
  of detection models and deliver reusable detection models for different microservice
  releases.This work also presents a benchmarking approach that considers the challenges
  and characteristics of microservices and provides a representative evaluation and
  comparison of intrusion detection models with configurable options. The benchmark
  user can utilize a 900-hour dataset with three types of data (training, testing,
  validation) that we generated following a well-defined methodology that ensures
  a representative and comprehensive evaluation base. We instantiate a concrete benchmark
  to evaluate six algorithms across three different case studies, demonstrating the
  usefulness and broad applicability of the benchmark while showing that ML algorithms
  generally perform better on testbeds that produce less data.The work converges on
  the µSherlock framework, which provides continuous and adaptive intrusion detection
  that aims to evolve with microservice applications and provide a constant level
  of security regardless of changes and updates. We implement a tool for end-to-end
  continuous monitoring of microservices in Kubernetes. µSherlock proactively computes
  metrics to understand if detection models are outdated and should be retrained.
  We validate and evaluate µSherlock in a dynamic scenario, and the evaluation results
  demonstrate its ability to perform efficiently and detect intrusions effectively.'

ix

Abstract
Microservices applications serve millions of customers every day, often running
business-critical systems in software containers and multi-tenant environments.
The adoption of microservices is growing rapidly due to the agility of develop-
ment and deployment, which is further facilitated by containers, as a supporting
technology, that are managed by orchestration platforms, such as Kubernetes,
monitoring, managing, and automating most tasks. These tools provide failover
mechanisms to increase the dependability and security of the applications. How-
ever, studies that focus on the effectiveness of such approaches in dealing with
failures or attacks are missing. As a result, there are concerns about their depend-
ability and security, which studies show is a slowing factor in adoption.
Intrusion detection is a technique to identify malicious activity on a host or net-
work that has been used to increase the security of many systems. The success of
past efforts makes it a promising approach for enhancing the security of microser-
vices, but it currently faces significant challenges. Namely, effectively processing
all the information produced by the services, keeping up with constant changes
and releases, and maintaining effective operation even in such dynamic environ-
ments. Therefore, it is extremely important to develop techniques that can be
deployed in real-world scenarios to keep applications secure.
This thesis advances the state-of-the-art on improving and evaluating security
for microservice applications that are deployed in scalable, elastic, dynamic
environment that are continuously changing to address plastic requirements
and technologies.
We present two studies that demonstrate: i) the inability of state-of-the-art fault-
tolerance mechanisms from popular container orchestrator platform in detecting
failures caused by a diverse set of software faults, which compromises the relia-
bility and dependability of the applications that use them, and ii) the applicability
and effectiveness of host intrusion detection based on the system calls a container
uses during its execution. We conclude that work was needed to improve current
approaches for detecting and responding to failures in microservices, and that
xi

host-based intrusion detection is a promising approach for improving the secu-
rity of microservices.
We propose components to improve the detection effectiveness of detection mod-
els used in scalable, elastic, and continuously changing environments. We devise
a set of techniques that allow detection models to monitor all active service repli-
cas as a group using a single detection model, allowing it to be used in multiple
operational deployments. In addition, we propose three techniques that allow to
obtain the core behavior of a microservice into intrusion detection models and
focus on core behavior patterns without losing detection effectiveness, while in-
creasing the likelihood of model reusability across different releases of the same
microservice.
To represent the likelihood of reuse, we propose a metric that uses source code
and service runtime data to provide the likelihood that a detection model will
be reused for a new version of the service. The results demonstrate that tech-
niques can generalize knowledge and improve the detection rate in scalable en-
vironments as well as reduce the size of detection models and deliver reusable
detection models for different microservice releases.
This work also presents a benchmarking approach that considers the challenges
and characteristics of microservices and provides a representative evaluation and
comparison of intrusion detection models with configurable options. The bench-
mark user can utilize a 900-hour dataset with three types of data (training, testing,
validation) that we generated following a well-defined methodology that ensures
a representative and comprehensive evaluation base. We instantiate a concrete
benchmark to evaluate six algorithms across three different case studies, demon-
strating the usefulness and broad applicability of the benchmark while showing
that ML algorithms generally perform better on testbeds that produce less data.
The work converges on theµSherlock framework, which provides continuous and
adaptive intrusion detection that aims to evolve with microservice applications
and provide a constant level of security regardless of changes and updates. We
implement a tool for end-to-end continuous monitoring of microservices in Ku-
bernetes. µSherlock proactively computes metrics to understand if detection mod-
els are outdated and should be retrained. We validate and evaluate µSherlock in
a dynamic scenario, and the evaluation results demonstrate its ability to perform
efficiently and detect intrusions effectively.
Keywords: Security, Microservices, Containers, Intrusion Detection, Intrusion
Tolerance
xii

Resumo
As aplicações de microsserviços servem milhões de clientes todos os dias, muitas
vezes executando sistemas críticos para o negócio em software containers e am-
bientes multi-tenancy. A utilização de microsserviços está a crescer rapidamente
devido à agilidade do desenvolvimento e da distribuição, que é ainda mais fa-
cilitada pelos contentores, enquanto tecnologia de apoio, que são geridos por
plataformas de orquestração, como o Kubernetes, que monitorizam, gerem e au-
tomatizam a maioria das tarefas. Estas ferramentas fornecem mecanismos de
failover para aumentar a confiabilidade e a segurança das aplicações. No en-
tanto, faltam estudos focados na eficácia de abordagens para lidar com falhas ou
ataques. Assim, existem preocupações quanto à sua confiabilidade e segurança,
o que constitui um fator de abrandamento da adoção.
A deteção de intrusões é uma técnica para identificar actividades maliciosas
numa máquina ou numa rede que tem sido utilizada para aumentar a segurança
dos sistemas. O sucesso destes esforços torna-a uma abordagem promissora para
aumentar a segurança dos microsserviços, mas atualmente enfrenta desafios
significativos. Nomeadamente, o processamento eficaz de toda a informação
produzida pelos serviços, o acompanhamento das constantes alterações e a
manutenção de um funcionamento eficaz mesmo em ambientes tão dinâmicos.
Portanto, é extremamente importante desenvolver técnicas que possam ser
implementadas em cenários reais para manter as aplicações seguras.
Este trabalho avança o estado da arte sobre a melhoria e avaliação da segurança
para aplicações de microsserviços implementadas em ambientes escaláveis,
elásticos e dinâmicos que estão em constante mudança para responder a
requisitos e tecnologias plásticas.
Dois estudos realizados neste contexto demonstram: i) a incapacidade dos atuais
mecanismos de tolerância a falhas de uma plataforma popular de orquestração de
containers em detetar falhas causadas por um conjunto diversificado de defeitos
de software, o que compromete a confiabilidade das aplicações que as utilizam, e
ii) a aplicabilidade e eficácia da deteção de intrusões com base emsystem calls que
xiii

um container utiliza durante a sua execução. As conclusões mostraram a neces-
sidade de trabalhar para melhorar as actuais abordagens de deteção e resposta
a falhas em microsserviços e que a deteção de intrusões baseada em dados do
sistema é uma técnica promissora para melhorar a segurança dos microsserviços.
Um grupo de componentes foi desenvolvido para melhorar a eficácia de modelos
de deteção utilizados em ambientes escaláveis, elásticos e em constante mudança.
Um conjunto de técnicas permitiu aos modelos de deteção monitorizar as répli-
cas activas de um serviço como um grupo utilizando apenas um modelo de de-
teção, permitindo a sua utilização em vários ambientes operacionais. Além disso,
propomos três técnicas que permitem obter o comportamento nuclear de um mi-
crosserviço em modelos de deteção de intrusões e focar nos padrões de compor-
tamento base sem perder a eficácia de deteção, aumentando simultaneamente a
probabilidade de reutilização do modelo em diferentes versões do mesmo mi-
crosserviço.
Para representar a probabilidade de reutilização, propomos uma métrica que uti-
liza o código-fonte e dados recolhidos durante a execução do serviço para cal-
cular a probabilidade de um modelo de deteção ser reutilizado para uma nova
versão do serviço. Os resultados demonstram que as técnicas podem generalizar
o conhecimento e melhorar a taxa de deteção em ambientes escaláveis e reduzir
o tamanho dos modelos de deteção enquanto asseguram a sua reutilização em
diferentes versões de microsserviços.
Este trabalho também apresenta um benchmark que considera os desafios e as
caraterísticas dos ambientes de microsserviços e fornece uma avaliação e com-
paração representativa de modelos de deteção de intrusões com várias configu-
rações. O utilizador do benchmark pode usar um dataset de 900 horas com três
tipos de dados (treino, teste, validação) que gerámos através duma metodolo-
gia que garante uma base de avaliação representativa e abrangente. Uma in-
stância do benchmark foi criada para avaliar seis algoritmos em três casos de es-
tudo, demonstrando a utilidade e a ampla aplicabilidade dobenchmark, ao mesmo
tempo que mostra que os algoritmos de ML têm geralmente um melhor desem-
penho em aplicações que produzem menos dados.
O trabalho converge para a plataforma µSherlock, que fornece deteção de in-
trusões contínua e adaptativa que visa evoluir com aplicações de microsserviços
e fornecer um nível constante de segurança, independentemente das alterações
e atualizações. Com base na framework implementamos uma ferramenta para
monitorização contínua de ponto-a-ponto de microsserviços em Kubernetes. O
µSherlock calcula proativamente métricas para entender se os modelos de de-
teção estão desatualizados e devem ser retreinados. A validação e avaliação do
µSherlock num cenário dinâmico mostram a sua eficiência e uma deteção eficaz.
Palavras-Chave: Segurança, Microsserviços, Containers, Detecção de
Intrusão, Tolerância a Intrusão

pore: IEEE, 2023, pp. 39–45. doi: 10.1109/PRDC59308.2023.00014.

tection for Microservices,” in 2023 IEEE International Conference on Soft-
ware Analysis, Evolution and Reengineering (SANER), Taipa, Macao: IEEE,

national Symposium on Software Reliability Engineering Workshops (IS-

The work conducted by the candidate has sparked further research that was pur-
sued by other students, culminating in two master thesis with the candidate as
co-supervisor:
• M. Teixeira, “ µDetector: An Intrusion Detection Tool for Microservices,”
Master’s thesis, University of Coimbra, 2022.
• P . Gonçalves, “Detecting Intrusions in Microservices Architectures,” Mas-
ter’s thesis, University of Coimbra, 2022.

CHAPTER 1
Introduction
Microservices1 are nowadays the underlying technology for massive applica-
tions that serve millions of customers every day [Newman, 2015; Dragoni et al.,
2017; Sultan et al., 2019]. The popularity of service-based applications, which
started with Service-Oriented Architectures (SOA) 2, is not new and has grown
steadily over the last decade [Alves, 2023]. This design has allowed applications
to overcome large monoliths [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Dragoni
et al., 2017], which are difficult to maintain, limit scalability, and also reduce the
freedom of technology adoption for developers [Dragoni et al., 2017]. Although
SOA emerged as an approach to mitigate such challenges by promoting reusabil-
ity and facilitating maintainability, it has failed to achieve consensus among users
on fundamental design guidelines, ranging from the communication protocols
used, to the lack of guidance on the granularity of the services, to the selection of
splitting points in the system [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014].
The emergence of microservices is largely due to the definition of guidelines and
concepts that allow developers and system designers to clearly understand how
to build a system based on multiple concise, independent, and coherent tiny ser-
vices [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Newman, 2015; Dragoni et al., 2017].
The microservices approach is to break down systems into small and very focused
services whose goal is well defined and contained [Martin Fowler and James
Lewis, 2014; Newman, 2015]. A service is a unit of software that can be indepen-
dently replaced and/or upgraded without the need to modify or restart the rest of
1Microservices, or microservice architectures, is an architectural style that structures an appli-
cation as a collection of independent and loosely coupled services, generally organized based on
business characteristics [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014].
2Service-Oriented Architectures (SOA) is a paradigm for developing and organizing dis-
tributed components that follow an architectural style built around business features [Perrey and
Lycett, 2003; MacKenzie et al., 2006]. Microservices is based on SOA principles and overcomes
some limitations to make systems more compatible with cloud environments [Richards, 2015].
1

Chapter 1
the application [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Newman, 2015]. This ap-
proach therefore allows systems to be designed according to Conway’s Law [Con-
way, 1968], that is, organized around business capabilities [Martin Fowler and
James Lewis, 2014].
Microservice applications allow for infrastructure automation, reducing the
complexity of releasing software into production [Martin Fowler and James
Lewis, 2014; Newman, 2015; Thönes, 2015]. The lightness of this approach
allows methodologies such as continuous integration 3 and continuous delivery4
(CI/CD) [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014]. The adoption of these method-
ologies is strongly related to the lightweight characteristics of services and their
deployment using virtualization mechanisms, such as software containers 5.
Using containers for deployment enables fast and cost-effective provisioning,
providing users with an elastic service that can be accessed from anywhere [Mell
and Grance, 2011]. As an alternative to traditional hardware virtualization,
Operating System (OS)-level virtualization leverages kernel features such as
control groups and namespaces [Michael Kerrisk, 2023]. These mechanisms provide
resource isolation and limitation, allowing a physical host to be shared by
multiple lightweight and self-contained containers.
The use of containers has increased over the past few years [Portworx, 2018, 2019;
Diamanti, 2019], with container platforms, such as Docker, and cloud environ-
ments contributing significantly, and the trend is expected to continue [Kong
Inc., 2020]. This technology has the potential to increase the adoption of many
solutions, such as Mixed-Criticality Systems (MCS) [Cinque et al., 2019] or Multi-
access Edge Computing (MEC) [Dario Sabella et al., 2019], which are also known
for leveraging microservices. However, microservices increase the attack surface
and network complexity due to their distributed nature [Dragoni et al., 2017]. In
addition, there are still security risks that should be carefully analyzed [Flora and
Antunes, 2019; Sultan et al., 2019] and in some cases may prevent the adoption of
these technologies [Kong Inc., 2020].
It is difficult to ensure system security and reliability, in particular data and re-
source isolation, and as these services may contain sensitive information, they re-
quire careful security assessment as confidentiality and integrity issues may arise.
This is exacerbated when we consider multi-tenant environments where con-
tainers from different application vendors may be running on the same infras-
tructure, raising concerns about data separation and performance isolation [Flora
and Antunes, 2019]. It is possible that legitimately obtained resources could allow
a bad actor to perform malicious actions against neighbors or the host, leading to
3Continuous Integration, a precursor to continuous delivery, is a software development prac-
tice in which developers frequently integrate their work into a common code repository. After
each integration, an automated pipeline is run to build and test the software to catch potential
bugs as early as possible. This practice results in fewer integration errors and ensures a more
reliable and higher quality software product. [Fowler and Foemmel, 2006].
4Continuous Delivery is a development practice for delivering software faster and more reli-
ably. The approach uses automated build, test, and deployment processes to reduce the likelihood
of human error and provide agile feedback. It increases synergy between development and oper-
ations teams, resulting in more effective product deliveries [Humble and Farley, 2010].
5Software containers or containers, are a standard unit composed of all the software needed
to instantiate and deploy an application in different environments (docker.com/what-container).
2

Introduction
harmful consequences. The use of vulnerable container images, privilege escala-
tion, and poor container isolation are examples of security risks that can harm an
organization [Souppaya et al., 2017].
The security of SOA and web services has been studied, and there are several
research papers on the topic [Totel et al., 2006; Saidane et al., 2009; Antunes and
Vieira, 2015a; Nunes et al., 2018], but the dependability and security challenges
for microservice architectures is further exacerbated. This approach inherits secu-
rity concerns from several areas, namely distributed systems, SOA, and the sup-
porting virtualization stack, typically containers. There have been attacks against
microservices that exploit some specific features of these architectures, for exam-
ple, a subdomain of Netflix was compromised, allowing an adversary to serve
malicious content in the context of netflix.com. As Netflix allowed any sub-
domain to access all users’ cookies, the malicious agent was able to manipulate
authenticated users and their data [Sun et al., 2015; Dragoni et al., 2017].
It is of utmost importance to ensure that applications running inside containers
are secure from threats. In fact, several published reports have shown that the
main obstacle to higher adoption rates is still related to ensuring the security
of the systems [Portworx, 2019; Kong Inc., 2020; Mike Loukides, 2020]. Mapping
studies show a trend of increasing research and work related to microservices
security [Pereira-Vale et al., 2019; Hannousse and Yahiouche, 2020]. These studies
show a strong focus on authentication, authorization, credentials, and prevention
and auditing based solutions, which is in line with the most researched threats of
unauthorized access, exposure of sensitive data, and compromise of individual
microservices [Pereira-Vale et al., 2019; Hannousse and Yahiouche, 2020].
Nevertheless, other important challenges, such as dealing with the scalability,
elasticity, and dynamic properties of these architectures, require attention. Deal-
ing with the continuous and adaptive environments of microservices requires
that the approaches used can contribute to more reliable and secure microser-
vice applications. For example, intrusion detection, which is the process of con-
tinuously monitoring system events and analyzing them for possible security
incidents, is a possible solution to monitor system events and analyzing them
for signs of compromise [Bace and Mell, 2001]. Host-based Intrusion Detection
Systems (HIDSs) (HIDSs) have been studied for years and recently applied to
container-based systems, but they do not provide engineers with solutions eas-
ily integrated into real-world microservice applications. Despite some promis-
ing advances [Abed et al., 2015; Lin et al., 2018; Srinivasan et al., 2019; Flora et al.,
2020], they still need improvements to operate at the service level, benefit from
lightweight algorithms, and integration into DevSecOps 6 pipelines and continu-
ous deployment practices.
6DevSecOps means development, security, and operations. It represents the practice of inte-
grating security testing into the software product development process [Mohan and Othmane,
2016]. It promotes synergy between software developers, security specialists, and operations
teams to build secure, reliable, and efficient products. Along with technical changes, DevSec-
Ops influences the culture of teams and makes security a shared responsibility for everyone in-
volved [Sánchez-Gordón and Colomo-Palacios, 2020]. Other terms such as SecDevOps and De-
vOpsSec portray the same meaning as DevSecOps [Mohan and Othmane, 2016; Sánchez-Gordón
and Colomo-Palacios, 2020], which will be the one used in this thesis.
3

Chapter 1
Furthermore, Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery (CI/CD)
pipelines also create security challenges, reason why the DevSecOps practice
emerged [Mohan and Othmane, 2016; Sánchez-Gordón and Colomo-Palacios,
2020]. Leveraging host data from the containers where microservices run seems
to be a viable option to identify malicious actions taking place within the infras-
tructure [Abed et al., 2015; Flora et al., 2020]. In fact, experiments have shown
that the well-defined boundary of containers can positively contribute to higher
detection effectiveness [Flora et al., 2020]. Still, the application of behavior-based
intrusion detection requires to profile the operation of microservices, which
can be modified when new versions are released and make detection models
obsolete, thus reducing their effectiveness. Hence, alternative approaches, which
provide a continuous adaptation for intrusion detection and combine it with
intrusion tolerance7 and resiliency methodologies, are urgently needed.
1.1 On the Security of Microservices
A microservice-based approach actively offers flexibility, agility, and scalabil-
ity, for dynamic environments. Organizations can quickly respond to market
changes and fix issues without a complete system shutdown by using microser-
vices. However, rapid and continuous software delivery increases the risk of
security vulnerabilities, such as exposed APIs and untested integrations. Open-
source software usage also requires proper examination to avoid additional vul-
nerabilities.
The growth of microservice-based systems becomes manytimes unmanageable
would it not be for the utilization of orchestrators, such as Kubernetes, which is
nowadays the de facto standard for microservices orchestration, adopted by many
companies [Vaughan-Nichols, 2020]. In terms of resilience, Kubernetes provides
three main features: automated horizontal scaling, restart of failing containers,
and the ability to define probes to continuously assess the operation of contain-
ers [Kubernetes, 2021]. To increase the resilience of microservice applications,
developers commonly use Kubernetes Probes, which have three different types
available. These resilience features have received limited attention regarding
their effectiveness in dealing with complex microservices and different types of
service faults and failures.
It is unclear how effective these features are at detecting failures related to soft-
ware aging, which is difficult to detect, especially in microservices that have
fewer resources available compared to monoliths. Aging detection mechanisms
need more effective and sensitive ways to quickly detect and report the occur-
rence of aging in microservices, which would allow mitigation actions to be taken
before the affected service becomes a system bottleneck. The experiments con-
ducted before deploying the services cannot be extended for long periods of time
7Intrusion Toleranceassumes that a system contains vulnerabilities and that successful attacks
on system components will occur. Nevertheless, by combining it with dependability concepts
and techniques, such as prevention or removal, and actuating on identified intrusions the overall
system remains secure and delivering the intended service [Veríssimo et al., 2003].
4

Introduction
as in traditional systems, where they can last for days or even months. Under-
standing the impact of faults and aging on microservices, and the ability of Ku-
bernetes probes to detect it, is an important contribution for developers to con-
sider and have a clearer understanding of the effectiveness and impact of avail-
able tools on the security and resilience of microservices.
A proper balance of microservices’ benefits with security measures allows or-
ganizations to leverage dynamic environments effectively without compromis-
ing the intended service. As software containers have a thinner isolation among
them than virtual machines, targeting them with intrusion detection techniques
requires tailoring to the specific characteristics. Intrusion detection mechanisms
should be utilized during the application’s runtime to enhance system security,
but several challenges must be overcome to allow intrusion detection to be ef-
fective in microservice environments, such as dealing with their scalability and
elasticity and fast delivery and continuous modification of available services.
Current techniques are designed for static deployments and do not handle
change well. The scenario becomes even more cumbersome when elasticity is
considered. The continuous, on-demand adaptation of microservice applica-
tions to properly respond to user workloads and keep operational costs low
exacerbates the challenge for detection models to cope with continuous and
sudden changes. This dynamic nature requires intrusion detection techniques
to adapt to scalable and elastic services and handle multiple operational
profiles. It is necessary to account for the behavioral variations that result from
different deployment scenarios. Thus, detection models need be tailored to these
characteristics so that they remain effective in identifying malicious actions.
As services change, it becomes more difficult to maintain the security of the envi-
ronment, especially with behavioral monitoring. Detection models learned dur-
ing previous service releases may no longer be consistent or effective. The differ-
ences introduced can vary in size and impact depending on the importance of the
service or its stage of development. Techniques must be developed toensure that
the knowledge gained during the training phase for a particular service release
is maintained and updated for its various iterations. Updates to the technology
stack, the addition of new features, or refactoring of the service operation will
affect the learned detection model and may compromise its effectiveness if it is
not prepared to cope with such changes.
The lack of a well-accepted framework or dataset that can produce standardized
and meaningful results is a challenge in evaluating proposed methodolo-
gies. There are several datasets used to evaluate intrusion detection mech-
anisms [Khraisat et al., 2019], but most are outdated, and those that target
microservices are not representative of real-world systems and workloads [Flora
and Antunes, 2024b]. In general, current evaluations do not take into account the
characteristics of microservice environments, namely their dynamic, scalable,
and elastic nature, as well as their continuous updates. In this regard, there is
a gap for research and development of a benchmark that takes into account
the challenges of microservice applications in a representative way. Create and
publish a dataset that covers the current scenarios of a realistic microservice-
based application deployment, with autonomous service orchestration and
5

Chapter 1
scaling, and current containerization and deployment technologies.
Having established a representative and meaningful framework for state-of-the-
art comparison, it becomes easier to propose techniques and methodologies to
ensure comprehensive and effective security for dynamic microservice environ-
ments. However, deploying the proposed techniques in online and operational
microservice environments is a significant challenge in itself. Combining op-
erational approaches and dealing with continuous changes without reducing
effectiveness along with integration into DevOps pipelines would enable an
automated and end-to-end intrusion detection approach , which is critical for
successful adoption. Providing and ensuring continuous intrusion detection
for complex and dynamic microservice applications is paramount, requiring
autonomous monitoring and application of detection models to the current
operational deployment of the microservice system. To provide an end-to-end
approach, it should also include reactive and proactive measures to increase the
overall reliability and security of the system by leveraging intrusion tolerance
principles and techniques.
1.2 Contributions
This thesis advances the state-of-the-art on the evaluation and security of dy-
namic, scalable, elastic, and complex microservice applications which inherit
security concerns from distributed and service-oriented systems operating from
two main areas: i) providing a standardized and comprehensive evaluation and
comparison framework, and ii) an integrated approach that allows to continu-
ously maintain, adapt, and improve the security level of microservice appli-
cations by combining intrusion detection techniques into a practical solution to
automate their operation in a DevSecOps microservices pipelines.
In detail, the main contributions of this thesis are:
• A Study on the Fault Tolerance of Microservice Environments
This study focuses on the effectiveness of Kubernetes in dealing with faults
and aging in microservices, and the possibility of using faults to accelerate
aging effects for testing purposes. The study is composed of three main
advancements to the state-of-the-art [Flora et al., 2021, 2022]:
– An experimental study on microservices aging, analyzing the ability
that Kubernetes probes have on detecting aging issues. The experiments in-
cluded two diverse and widely used microservices applications, built using
different technologies and architectures. The key insight is that Kubernetes
probes were not effective in detecting the observed aging failures. This is
particularly significant considering the used probes followed two diverse
and well established development strategies.
– An experimental evaluation that used fault injection to assess how Ku-
bernetes resilience features deal with failures in microservices. The study
6

Introduction
was conducted in two representative microservice applications, and the
faults injected were obtained from an industrial survey on microservice
faults, for representativeness. The key insight is that Kubernetes probes fail
to detect the occurrence of failures. This shows that probes require a deep
understanding of the monitored services, and it still might not be enough
to detect many types of failures.
– The demonstration of the applicability of fault injection in facilitating
the testing of aging detection and mitigation mechanisms for microservices,
as it allows to accelerate aging effects, enabling a faster and simpler evalua-
tion of these mechanisms in microservice environments.
• A Study on Intrusion Detection for Container-based Deployments.
We investigate the feasibility of host-based intrusion detection for microser-
vices. The study covers the applicability and effectiveness of detection mod-
els based on system calls collected from a container [Flora and Antunes,
2019; Flora et al., 2020]:
– The study initially focuses on investigating the usability and applicabil-
ity of intrusion detection to container-based systems. We intend to under-
stand whether intrusion detection techniques and algorithms are capable of
generating usable detection models from information collected from con-
tainers under monitoring.
– We proposed an approach to evaluate intrusion detection techniques us-
ing attack injection to target the monitored application and test the detection
models generated during the training phase. The results show the validity
of the approach as well as the effectiveness of the algorithms, which con-
sistently detect most of the attacks, but also need careful tuning and richer
workloads.
• Metrics and Techniques to Improve Intrusion Detection for Microser-
vices.
A set of techniques to address the challenges of intrusion detection in scal-
able, elastic, and continuously changing microservices [Flora et al., 2023a;
Flora and Antunes, 2024a,c]:
– A set of three data processing techniques that integrate in the frame-
work and enable effective intrusion detection in scalable and elastic mi-
croservices. These techniques allow detection model to be used in a CI/CD
pipelines, contributing to a DevSecOps strategy.
– Three techniques that obtain the core information from the microservice
behavior, which in turn generalize those models to enable their reusability
across different versions of a service. These techniques and algorithms are
evaluated in a context where microservice detection models are reused in a
representative application with multiple configurations and microservices
releases.
7

Chapter 1
– MRI: Model Reusability Index. A metric designed to provide insights into
the reusability of intrusion detection models for microservices. This metric
can assist in making decisions about whether to reuse older detection mod-
els when new microservice releases occur or retraining should take place.
• A benchmarking approach for representative/meaningful evaluation of
microservice IDSs
We propose a comprehensive approach to evaluate and compare differ-
ent intrusion detection approaches for microservice applications. A bench-
marking methodology is proposed to allow users to standardize the pro-
cess for a representative and reproducible evaluation. The benchmark is
composed of several research contributions to deliver a complete and com-
prehensive approach for complex microservice applications [Flora and An-
tunes, 2019; Flora et al., 2020; Flora and Antunes, 2024b,c]:
– A general methodology for generating datasets in the context of mi-
croservices that applies the characteristics of these environments and allows
developers to overcome guidelines limitations and generate representative
and useful data.
– A concrete instantiation of the benchmarking approach to demonstrate
its applicability and feasibility, with six different IDSs evaluated in three
case studies in the context of microservice applications.
– A dataset for intrusion detection in microservice applications generated
using state-of-the-art technologies used in three different microservice ap-
plications with three different types of data: for training, validation, and
testing, with approximately 900 hours of recorded data.
• µSherlock: A Framework for Continuous Intrusion Detection for
Dynamic Microservices
A framework that ensures continuous intrusion detection for complex and
dynamic microservice applications. It delivers autonomous monitoring and
utilization of intrusion detection models according to the active operational
deployment of the application. The framework integrates different research
techniques proposed over previous work in the context of this thesis and
several publications [Flora et al., 2020, 2023a,b; Flora and Antunes, 2024a,c]:
– Integration of the proposed intrusion detection techniques and metrics
in the framework to deliver practical and functional improved detection of
malicious behavior in microservice environments.
– The application of proactive and reactive measures that enforce and in-
crease intrusion tolerance principles for microservice applications. To assist
in this operation, we leverage a custom metric (MRI) that allows to decide
whether a detection model should be retrained given the modifications in-
troduced to each microservice delivery.
8

Introduction
– A scalable design and implementation of a tool that applies the devised
framework and demonstrates its practical application. The tool can inte-
grate into a CI/CD pipeline to receive the changes made to the microser-
vices and deliver the intended security level regardless of the operational
modifications.
1.3 Document Outline
This first chapter introduced the problem addressed and the main contributions
of the work in this thesis.
Chapter 2 presents background and related work on specific areas relevant to this
work. The main topics discussed are microservices, software containers, depend-
ability and security concepts, and intrusion detection systems. The evaluation of
security measures, benchmarking, and intrusion detection datasets are also cov-
ered.
Chapter 3 details the microservice security challenges identified after the state-of-
the-art analysis. We discuss the five key challenges that are relevant to the work
done in this thesis. We also define the threat model considered in this thesis and
detail each of the identified threats.
Chapter 4 presents a study on the fault tolerance of microservice environments.
It considers an experimental study on the aging of microservices, analyzing the
ability of Kubernetes probes to detect aging problems, and the use of fault injec-
tion to evaluate how Kubernetes resilience features handle failures in microser-
vices.
Chapter 5 presents a study of the applicability and effectiveness of intrusion de-
tection for container-based deployments. First, we evaluate the potential to pro-
duce usable detection models based on system calls collected from a container,
and then we evaluate the effectiveness results with several algorithms and con-
figurations.
Chapter 6 proposes a set of techniques and metrics aimed at improving the effec-
tiveness of intrusion detection in scalable, elastic, and dynamic microservice ap-
plications. One set of techniques processes microservice information to increase
detection effectiveness in scalable environments, while another set of techniques
captures the core behavior of the service to produce detection models that can
be used across multiple releases of the microservice. The proposed metric allows
users to obtain an objective measure of the likelihood of reusing detection mod-
els.
Chapter 7 presents a comprehensive approach for evaluating and comparing dif-
ferent intrusion detection approaches for microservice applications. The chapter
details a general methodology for generating intrusion detection datasets in the
context of microservices, and the dataset generated using this methodology along
with a concrete instantiation of the benchmarking approach to demonstrate its
applicability.
9

Chapter 1
Chapter 8 presents µSherlock: a continuous intrusion detection framework for dy-
namic microservices. The chapter provides details on the framework developed
and how it integrates the techniques presented in Chapter 6 to enable effective
intrusion detection in scalable and elastic microservices and to extract the core
information from the microservice behavior and generalize it to enable reusabil-
ity across different versions of a service. It also presents the design and imple-
mentation of a tool that applies the developed framework and demonstrates its
practical application.
Chapter 9 concludes the thesis and proposes future research topics.

CHAPTER 2
Background and Related Work
This chapter provides the background and state of the art into which the thesis
work fits. Expanding the knowledge base for the security of microservice ap-
plications requires an understanding of how these systems are developed and
operated, their basic concepts, and their security vulnerabilities. The underly-
ing technologies commonly used to deploy and run such systems, as well as the
tools and techniques used to monitor and manage them, are also of paramount
importance.
This chapter also introduces some concepts of common dependability character-
istics and concerns, such as fault tolerance, software aging, and intrusion detec-
tion and tolerance. Further details on the design, use, and operation of Intrusion
Detection Systems (IDSs) are provided, along with previous work in this area.
Finally, it analyzes the state of the art in security evaluation, explaining concepts
and reviewing the state of the art so that it is possible to understand the avail-
able methodologies and techniques for evaluating and comparing the different
approaches to ensuring security in microservice applications.
This chapter covers software services and service-based applications (Section 2.1),
microservice principles, characteristics, and testbeds (Section 2.2), software con-
tainers concepts, technologies, and security tools (Section 2.3), dependability and
security concepts, such as fault tolerance and intrusion detection (Section 2.4),
intrusion detection systems, with details on targets, approaches and methods
(Section 2.5), security measures evaluation, detailing benchmarking, metrics, and
datasets (Section 2.6), and closes with a summary and conclusions (Section 2.7).
11

Chapter 2
2.1 Software Services and Service-based Applica-
tions
Software services and service-based applications have significantly changed the
landscape of software delivery and usage and have become a cornerstone of com-
puting infrastructure [Perrey and Lycett, 2003]. The emergence of service-based
applications addressed the limitations of the traditional monolithic model, in
which all components of an application were tightly coupled and deployed as
a unit to scale and adapt to changing business needs. In contrast, software ser-
vices, typically delivered through the Software as a Service (SaaS) model, provide
a more dynamic and cost-effective approach. The services are hosted remotely on
servers managed by the service provider and accessed over the Internet, simpli-
fying the management of updates and security patches.
The adoption of service-based applications began to gain traction through archi-
tectural frameworks such as Service-Oriented Architectures (SOA) [Perrey and
Lycett, 2003]. SOA is a design principle in which software components are struc-
tured as distributed and interoperable services that can be reused across differ-
ent applications or systems [MacKenzie et al., 2006; Josuttis, 2007]. Services are
loosely coupled and designed to operate and interact independently, without the
need for dependencies, by exchanging messages using standardized protocols.
In short, services are reusable components that represent business functionality
within a protocol-independent distributed environment and through a standard-
ized and advertised interface.
SOA also supports modular development of applications, making it easier to up-
date or replace individual services without disrupting the entire system. This
modularity is particularly beneficial for organizations that need to maintain com-
plex environments, as it simplifies the integration of new services and the scaling
of existing ones. However, there are still some limitations in implementing SOA
that lead developers to turn to microservices [Martin Fowler and James Lewis,
2014]. SOA has failed to achieve consensus among users on fundamental design
guidelines, ranging from the communication protocols used to the lack of guid-
ance on the granularity of services and the selection of partitioning points in the
system [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014].
Other shortcomings of SOA were related to its complexity and often heavy re-
liance on centralized governance and infrastructure, such as Enterprise Service
Buses (ESBs), or communication protocols with high overhead, such as Simple
Object Access Protocol (SOAP). The centralized components can become bottle-
necks and lead to reduced agility and scalability.
Microservice architectures address these shortcomings by decentralizing and
modularizing services. In addition, microservices use lightweight protocols
such as HTTP/REST for communication, which reduces overhead and improves
scalability, as opposed to relying on an ESB. By embracing these principles, mi-
croservices offer greater flexibility, agility, and scalability compared to traditional
SOA [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014].
12

Background and Related Work
2.2 Microservices
The microservices approach consists of developing an application based on
multiple loosely coupled, independently deployable services [Martin Fowler
and James Lewis, 2014; Newman, 2015; Thönes, 2015]. Each microservice focuses
on a specific business function and can be developed, deployed, and scaled
independently. These characteristics allow for easy replication, distribution, and
scalability guarantees of any microservice in a production system, regardless of
its dimension [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Dragoni et al., 2017].
Microservices have evolved over time in several waves [Jamshidi et al., 2018],
some have started to advance before the term "microservices" was generally ac-
cepted [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Jamshidi et al., 2018], still there has
been constant improvement of existing technological solutions and development
and release of new ones.
There are several challenges in microservice environments, from resilience pat-
terns to designing fault-tolerant microservices [Garriga, 2018]. Security has not
been extensively researched despite several challenging issues arising from the
distributed nature of microservice-based systems as well as the trust that exists
between services, which can compromise entire systems from a single microser-
vice [Sun et al., 2015; Garriga, 2018].
2.2.1 Principles
The design of applications that follow the microservice approach is based on sev-
eral principles. These guidelines allow applications to be properly and effectively
implemented, taking full advantage of methodologies and processes that deliver
dynamic, adaptive, fault-tolerant systems that are still able to change rapidly with
the evolution of business needs or requirements.
Separation of Concerns : through the separation of concerns principle,
microservice-based systems are designed to be goal-oriented and to have
specific responsibilities [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Yarygina,
2018]. This approach ensures that each microservice becomes easier
to manage, by having lower complexity, maintain, and having parallel
development and/or updates with other services. It empowers autonomy
among services and reduces coupling [Dragoni et al., 2017].
Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery (CI/CD) and DevOps :
microservice applications and CI/CD and DevOps practices are inter-
twined [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Yarygina, 2018]. Both
methodologies empower applications to accelerate the introduction of
source code changes into production deployments while ensuring that
all testing and validation is performed and successfully passed. Time
to market is critical and can often make or break a business, so having
these pipelines in place and leveraging their benefits can be a significant
competitive advantage.
13

Chapter 2
Cloud and Virtualization: leveraging the on-demand availability of resources
from cloud computing platforms in combination with virtualization princi-
ples such as containers allows microservice applications to fully integrate
separation of concerns, CI/CD, and DevOps practices [Dragoni et al., 2017;
Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Yarygina, 2018]. Virtualization pro-
vides the encapsulation and packaging for microservices to be distributed
and deployed in a consistent and predictable way, managed, often with au-
tomated tools, and scaled independently to better serve users.
The combination of technical and technological solutions and concepts that mi-
croservices can deliver allow developers to have a very flexible system that can
change quickly and effectively in accordance with business requirements [Drag-
oni et al., 2017]. Developed independently and ensuring modular components
that can work together increases the maintainability and continuous evolution of
different components.
2.2.2 Advantages and Limitations
As with most technology solutions, there are pros and cons to using them, and
compromises must be made to ensure that the tradeoffs provide the intended
level of service. Microservices offer significant benefits from a development, de-
ployment, and management perspective, allowing applications to be more flex-
ible, adaptable, and dynamic while reaching the target market. However, these
benefits can also create challenges in ensuring the security and reliability of the
overall system, either by placing additional strain on security mechanisms that
may struggle to cope with so much dyanmicity, or on build pipelines and qual-
ity assurance engineers who may need to extend testing beyond typical unit and
integration testing with other techniques as chaos engineering [Yarygina, 2018].
Building applications according to a microservices design allows developers to
deliver systems that take advantage of benefits such as:
Scalability and Elasticity: due to the modular development and separation of
concerns applied in microservice applications, the resulting services are
highly focused on specific tasks [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014;
Dragoni et al., 2017]. This factor allows developers to apply service-oriented
scaling algorithms and techniques that can replicate by scaling up or down
the services that are most in demand at any given time. This approach en-
ables more cost-effective use of resources and higher quality of service.
Development time: with the loose coupling and independence of microservices,
the development of systems can be assigned according to business areas,
with different teams covering or "owning" the work related to each service
composing the application [Martin Fowler and James Lewis, 2014; Yarygina,
2018; Dragoni et al., 2017]. This approach to software development allows
streamlining the process and parallelizing as much as possible, thus reduc-
ing development time and making it an iterative process.
14

Background and Related Work
Technology heterogeneity : because microservices are developed indepen-
dently, they can be implemented using multiple technologies, increasing
the technological stack that can be using during development of a microser-
vice application. Different operating systems can serve as the basis for the
application in each service [Dragoni et al., 2017]. This provides flexibility
in development and allows developers more freedom of choice to select the
most appropriate and effective tool for the job.
Despite all the benefits from a deployment perspective, there are some drawbacks
or limitations to using microservices in production systems, some of which can
also be considered security concerns. For example, as systems grow, so does the
complexity of the architecture supporting them, as the number of services and
communication links increases, resulting in a larger attack surface and higher
network complexity [Dragoni et al., 2017]. Some limitations and concerns are:
Greater Surface Attack Area : the components that used to communicate via
Inter-Process Communication (IPC) or other mechanisms such as Remote
Procedure Call (RPC) are now decoupled, requiring them to expose an Ap-
plication Programming Interface (API) so that they can exchange informa-
tion with other microservices or third parties [Dragoni et al., 2017]. In ad-
dition, the possibility of access from external sources or even third parties
deployed in the same infrastructure due to cloud deployments and multi-
tenancy environments causes the attack surface to be larger than ever be-
fore [Flora and Antunes, 2019].
Network Complexity : migrating to a collection of multiple independent ser-
vices which communicate using message exchange protocols (e.g., REST)
increases the amount of traffic and network connections established. As
a consequence, attackers have multiple access vectors to explore while en-
forcing security mechanisms for monitoring or auditing can become over-
whelming [Dragoni et al., 2017]. Examples are depicted in Figure 2.1.
Multi-tenancy Environments : typically, at the time of deployment, these ar-
chitectures provide services in cloud environments that share physical re-
sources with applications from other vendors, where some may obtain re-
sources with malicious intent given the nature of these environments [Flora
and Antunes, 2019; Nehme et al., 2019]. Therefore, in addition to external
threats, cohabiting applications may also raise concerns about data separa-
tion and resource isolation [Flora and Antunes, 2019; Nehme et al., 2019].
The growing adoption of microservices is favored by their benefits as scalability
and the ability to decompose complex applications into small components. How-
ever, these advantages also enlarge the attack surface, thus increasing security
risks. Reviews of the state-of-the-art of microservices runtime security, which an-
alyzed a large pool of high-impact publications and industry tools, concluded
that orchestration security and security policies verification have a significant
gap in research work [Minna and Massacci, 2023]. Resilience of the application
15

Chapter 2
against external and internal attackers as well as mechanisms to recover from at-
tacks are found to be missing, both from academia research and industry tools,
which do not perform the expected security tests [Minna and Massacci, 2023].
Some challenges and limitations in microservice applications stem from multi-
tenant environments. The use of multi-tenancy in cloud architectures is key
to improving scalability, automation, and usability, but studies are needed to
clarify the gaps in existing research on this topic, especially on the security
of microservices. In a systematic mapping article, the authors analyzed 64
peer-reviewed publications from 2015 to 2022 [Matthew et al., 2023]. The
findings highlight emerging challenges and trends in multi-tenancy, identifying
challenges such as migration, malicious users, and isolation issues that need
more attention [Matthew et al., 2023].
Figure 2.1: Three sample architectures representing the service complexity.
(image from Eric Lehman and Renjith Nair [2017])
2.2.3 Microservice Applications
The normal tendency is for microservice applications to grow over time and
evolve into complex systems with a high degree of interaction. Sneak peaks into
production applications of large companies demonstrate the complexity of re-
lationships between the services, some examples are Netflix, or Twitter (see Fig-
ure 2.1). The applications are, of course, proprietary and closed source, but for re-
search and experimentation we need to have access to microservice applications
that are representative of real-world systems. For this, different parties, such as
companies or research groups, have developed open-source microservice-based
systems, either to demonstrate tools or to provide a well-accepted testbed for re-
search and development.
The microservice applications serve the purpose of being a target for representa-
tive operations commonly seen in real-world applications, to collect data that can
be used by algorithms, and to provide a basis for exploring security measures.
The elicited testbeds are not selected based on, nor do they provide, the security
measures that they may apply. The number of services present in each testbed
16

Background and Related Work
and the technologies involved can be a significant limitation to developing se-
curity measures that rely on data obtained from these systems, so creating and
providing a dataset that relies on representative applications is of great benefit to
advancing research efforts.
Below, we describe some of these applications, often referred to as “testbeds” or
“benchmark applications”. Of course, there are some limitations, usually related
to the number of services provided. Some examples are TeaStore [von Kistowski
et al., 2018], SockShop [Weaveworks, 2017], or the microservice application suite
DeathStarBench [Gan et al., 2019].
Tea Store
TeaStore consists of 5 Java-based services that are designed to discover them-
selves through a Registry component. The architecture of the application is
shown in the Figure 2.2. Each running service instance advertises itself to the
Registry as a way for the application to operate independently of the active ser-
vice replicas and their distribution. The WebUI service uses the Recommender ser-
vice to provide recommendations to users. The system also consists of a database
that stores all relevant data and is only accessed by the Persistence service. All
services communicate via REST, as this is established as the de facto standard in
the microservices domain [von Kistowski et al., 2018].

Sock Shop
SockShop simulates a user-facing e-commerce website that sells socks that are in-

to TeaStore, SockShop also uses REST for the communication between services.
Train Ticket
Trainticket is a large and representative microservice application that provides
a web-based ticketing platform consisting of forty microservices. Each service is
built with different programming languages, such as Go or Java, and each with
a different purpose. The application consists of multiple scenarios and use cases,
such as the ability to search for specific locations or purchase a train ticket, and ac-

DeathStarBench
DeathStarBench is an open source microservice benchmark suite designed to pro-
vide researchers with a set of applications that are representative of large, end-to-
end services, modular, and extensible [Gan et al., 2019]. This microservice suite
includes a social network, a media service, an e-commerce site, a banking sys-
tem, and IoT applications for coordinating control of swarms of unmanned aerial
vehicles (UAVs). As an example, the Social Network (see Figure 2.5a) applica-
tion represents a social network with unidirectional follow relationships imple-
mented using microservices that communicate through Thrift RPCs. The appli-
cation is part of a benchmark suite of microservice applications developed for
research purposes [Gan et al., 2019]. Users can create posts with embedded text,
media, and links, as well as tag other users, which are sent to all of their fol-
lowers. Users can also read, favorite, and repost previously created posts. The
application covers several technologies, such as programming languages (e.g.,
C/C++, Java, Python, NodeJS), storage systems such as Redis, Memcached, and
MongoDB, and search engines such as Xapian.
18

Background and Related Work
Another example is the Media Microservices (see Figure 2.5b) application, which
implements an end-to-end service for browsing movie information, as well as
reviewing, rating, renting, and streaming movies [Gan et al., 2019]. Users can
search and browse movie information, including plot, photos, videos, cast, and
review information, as well as add new reviews to the system for a specific movie
by logging into their account. Users can also choose to rent a movie, which in-
cludes a payment authentication module to verify that the user has sufficient
funds. Movie information is maintained in a sharded and replicated database.
The application spans multiple technologies, including programming languages
(e.g., C/C++, Java, Python) and storage systems such as MySQL, and MongoDB.

2.3 Software Containers
The way microservices are developed and deployed is very well suited to soft-
ware containers. Containers provide an effective and efficient way to package a
microservice by encapsulating all the dependencies and application code to en-
sure that the service behaves consistently across platforms. This section provides
background on software containers, including basic concepts, technologies, mon-
itoring techniques and tools, and security mechanisms.
2.3.1 Software Containers Concepts
The basis for containers was introduced in the late 1970s, more precisely in 1979,
when the chroot1 system call was introduced to UNIX [Michael Kerrisk, 2024a].
As a result, it became possible to isolate the execution of processes, one of the
main characteristics of containers - isolation. Such isolation became achievable
through file system separation, partitioning it into several branches and assign-
ing each of them to a different process, thus ensuring processes’ inability to access
files above their assigned subtree. However, this was only the beginning of pro-
cess isolation.
1The chroot system call changes the root directory of a process and its subprocesses.
19

Chapter 2
Later, in 2000, the FreeBSD Jails2 was released, almost twenty years after the addi-
tion of chroot to BSD, and was a major step towards today’s containers. As a way
to achieve a complete separation between systems, Jails allowed the BSD system
to be partitioned into multiple independent subsystems, consisting of a virtual-
ization of the file system, the user set, and the networking subsystem, resulting
in the ability to assign a dedicated IP address [FreeBSD Community, 2024]. The
notion of operating system level virtualization was also introduced.
In the following years, other technologies similar to FreeBSD jails were re-
leased, namely Linux VServer in 2001, Solaris Containers in 2004, and OpenVZ in
2005 [Marquez, 2023]. However, it was not until 2006 that another big step was
taken with the release of Process Containers by Google. This was designed to
limit, account for and isolate resource usage (CPU, memory, disk I/O, network)
of a group of processes. It was later renamed to control groups (cgroups) and
added to the Linux kernel in 2008, the same year, LinuX Containers (LXC) was
released, which is based on cgroups and namespaces. While namespaces provides
process isolation into groups, cgroups is responsible for controlling resource
usage for each container within the main system.
Another important step in the evolution of containers was the release of Docker,
in 2013, which really drove the adoption of this technology. Docker really simpli-
fied application development and deployment, which led to massive adoption of
containers by cloud service providers. Kubernetes was released in 2014, and the
version 1.0 was released in 2015. Over time, and after the formation of Cloud Na-
tive Computing Foundation (CNCF), the popularity and adoption of Kubernetes
has grown significantly and continues to grow, with the majority of developers
using it [6sense, 2024]. Figure 2.6 shows the described evolution.

Figure 2.6: Software Containers evolution timeline.
The utilization of namespaces and control groups (cgroups) allows a set of processes
and resources, such as memory or CPU, to be grouped together and isolated from
the main system processes and resources. All containers on a host machine share
kernel functionality, providing a lightweight virtualization mechanism.
On the one hand, namespaces create an isolated instance of a global resource,
such as user groups or network devices, by enclosing a system resource in an
abstraction available to processes within a given namespace. This instance is only
available to the processes within the namespace for which it was created. There-
fore, any changes made to it will only affect the processes that are members of
the namespace. There are currently eight different types of namespaces, which are
analyzed in Table 2.1.
2Jails is an extension of the chroot system call to create independent environments for sets of
processes running in a system. These processes cannot access resources outside their file system
tree, so when compromised, the rest of the system remains safe [FreeBSD Community, 2024].
20

Background and Related Work
Table 2.1: Namespaces provided by the Linux Kernel.
Namespace Description
Cgroup This namespaces type isolates the cgroup root directory
IPC IPC namespaces isolate System V IPC and POSIX message queues
Network Responsible for isolating network devices, stacks, ports, among others
Mount Enclose the mount points
PID Limit the process IDs
Time Virtualize two system clocks values
User Enforce user and group IDs
UTS Allow the isolation of the hostname and NIS domain name
On the other hand, cgroups, the other fundamental feature that makes containers
possible, allow the organization of processes into hierarchical groups whose re-
source usage can be monitored and limited as appropriate. This feature provides
an interface through cgroupsfs and globally handles the aggregation of different
processes, furthermore the available Kernel subsystems control resources such as
CPU and memory and ensure that a cgroup’s processes do not exceed their limits.
There are two types of containers: system and application containers.
System Containers are characterized by the virtualization of a Operating Sys-
tem (OS), which is lighter and faster to boot than a Virtual Machine (VM).
Some examples are the LXC and OpenVZ technologies.
Application Containers are a step up from system containers. They are special-
ized containers designed to run only one application at a time, with a file
system built through a composition of multiple layers. Docker is an exam-
ple of this technology.
2.3.2 Software Containers Technologies
Several technology solutions allow users to build, deploy, and manage contain-
ers. Here, we provide details on some of the most frequently used platforms,
such as LXC, Docker, and Kubernetes.
LinuX Containers (LXC)
LXC was introduced in 2008 with the primary goal of providing OS-level vir-
tualization to run multiple isolated containers. Despite some misconceptions,
LXC does not provide virtual machines, but rather a virtual environment that
has its own resources, such as CPU and memory, and the resource control mech-
anism [ArchLinux, 2024].
LXC is based on kernel namespaces and cgroups (see Section 2.3.1), AppArmor,
SELinux, Kernel capabilities, and Seccomp (see Table 2.2 for details). These tools
and Kernel features are integrated to deliver system containers that provide iso-
lation and encapsulation of applications.
21

Chapter 2
Table 2.2: Linux Kernel features used in containers.
Feature Description
AppArmor a Mandatory Access Control (MAC) system, part of the Linux Kernel,
which is used to confine programs to a limited set of resources
SELinux a collection of user-space tools and kernel modifications that eases the
enforcement of security policies
Kernel capabilities allow developers and users to avoid the utilization of the root user by
providing fine grain control over privileged permissions
Seccomp a Kernel feature to restrict the set of system calls a program can execute
This technology allows users to create two types of containers, either unprivi-
leged or privileged, although the former are considered more secure because the
root user ID is not mapped to theroot user ID of the host machine, some Linux dis-
tributions require further configuration to allow their creation [ArchLinux, 2024].
Docker
When Docker first launched in 2013, it was based on LXC and known asdotCloud;
however, Docker has since moved away from both. However, it is still based on
the same Linux kernel features as LXC, including namespaces and cgroups.
The Docker Client, the Docker Daemon, and the Docker Registry are the three
primary components of Docker. The Docker Client is responsible for commu-
nicating with the Docker Daemon based on user commands, providing control
over container instantiation and management. The Daemon component monitors
all images stored in its cache and all containers present on the Docker host. A
Docker image serves as the template for the container and is combined hierar-
chically with other images to create a Docker container. Developers can create
custom images and define their components using a scripting language and a file
typically called Dockerfile to install the necessary libraries, add new users, and de-
ploy their applications. The Docker Registry component acts as an image repos-
itory and distributor. It does this by maintaining base and custom images and
enabling their distribution to other hosts. It also keeps track of different image
versions and allows the selection of a preferred version. These are the primary
reasons for Docker’s rise in popularity, which is largely due to its agnostic fea-
tures and the speed and ease of container deployment.
Kubernetes
Kubernetes is an open source container manager and orchestrator that automates
the execution of container-based applications. Kubernetes uses a distributed
master-node architecture, where a master machine manages and monitors
multiple worker nodes, where running containers are deployed according to
user instructions. The architecture (see Figure 2.7) follows a division between
the control plane, where the master machine manages the authentication and
authorization components of the cluster, as well as security and scheduling
functions, and the node components where workloads are deployed and run.
Although Kubernetes supports multiple container runtimes as long as they com-
ply with the Container Runtime Interface (CRI) 3, containers are most commonly
3CRI was developed to integrate any container runtime with kubelet (github.com/kubernetes).
22

Background and Related Work
Figure 2.7: Kubernetes architecture. (image from wikipedia.org/wiki/Kubernetes)
deployed using Docker. The basic unit is a Pod, which represents one or more
containers and runs on a single machine, has a single IP address, and can com-
municate with other Pods in the same cluster. A Pod can be scaled across dif-
ferent physical machines (e.g., automatically using Horizontal Pod Autoscaling
(HPA)4), which contributes to the fault tolerance guarantees of the application.
Kubernetes also provides a REST API, which allows interaction with the control
plane using the command line tool or automated scripts.
2.3.3 Monitoring for Containers
After containers are deployed, there is a need to manage and monitor them to
understand if there is a problem that needs to be addressed. One way to keep
this technology under scrutiny is to use information about resources, such as CPU
