or memory usage, or even system calls, to infer whether the system is working
properly. This section explains some monitoring tools that can be used.
Sysdig
This tool instruments machines at the OS level, installing itself into the Linux
kernel and capturing system calls and other OS events [Sysdig, Inc, 2019]. It also
provides a command line interface to easily filter and decode such events so that
is possible to extract useful information. Sysdig can be used in both real-time or
in after the fact inspections, through generation of trace files.
The main features provided bysysdig are its native support for all Linux container
technologies, such as Docker and LXC, and providing a “unified, coherent, and
4HPA is a Kubernetes feature that automatically scales resources to match with demand, ac-
cording to user-defined rules on observed metrics (e.g., CPU) [Kubernetes, 2023].
23

Chapter 2
(a) Sysdig eBPF [Borello, 2019]).
 (b) Strace [Degioanni, 2014]).
Figure 2.8: Sysdig and Strace architecture comparison.
granular visibility into the storage, processing, network, and memory subsystems” [Shiv
Dhar, 2017]. Moreover, it makes possible system activity trace files creation, thus
enabling after the fact analysis. These files can include the state of the system
thereby assuring that the context in which the capture was performed does not
get lost, which is available due to the filtering procedures that can be used to
process the information collected easily.
Sysdig uses a kernel-level extended Berkeley Packet Filter (eBPF) 5 hook that moni-
tors the system calls made by a program and stores them in a shared buffer for
later processing by the other user-level components. It also has a communication
channel between the user-level and kernel-level programs for control messages.
Figure 2.8a depicts sysdig’s components and their responsibilities.
strace
This tool consists of a diagnostic, debugging, and instructional user space utility
managed through a command line interface for Linux and can be used to monitor
processes and the Linux kernel, allowing the collection of events such as system
calls, signaling, and process state changes [Michael Kerrisk, 2024b]. The opera-
tion of strace is made possible by a Linux kernel feature known as ptrace.
The main features provided by strace are the ability to attach to an already run-
ning process, which leads to the ability to start monitoring a process at any point
of its execution, to filter the collected data by type of system call, such as file
or process, thus collecting only calls related to file handling and process manage-
ment, respectively. In addition, this tool also allows users to trace only system call
events that access a user-defined path, to perform a full hexadecimal and ASCII
dump of all data read from/written to file descriptors, and to introduce system
call fault injection.
Although strace is primarily a process-oriented monitoring tool, it can also be
used to successfully monitor containers.
The architecture ofstrace is characterized by the introduction of additional context
switching operations. When a system call is executed, there is a context switch
from user to kernel mode. However, when strace monitors the process executing
5eBPF is a Linux kernel feature that provides support for dynamic and efficient tracing of
several system events, such as network packets, function calls, and other system events. It has
multiple uses like security, auditing, and observability (ebpf.io/what-is-ebpf).
24

Background and Related Work
the call, two additional context switches occur. That is, this utility uses the ptrace
kernel feature to pause the process each time a system call is executed, collect and
decode it, and return the process to normal execution [Degioanni, 2014]. There-
fore, strace performs a blocking collection of data and affects the performance of
running processes. Figure 2.8b shows the execution flow of a system call when
strace is monitoring the process.
Kubernetes Probes
As part of its adaptive and monitoring capabilities, Kubernetes provides three
different probes whose goal is to ensure that applications are delivering the
expected service and are ready to respond to incoming requests [Kubernetes,
2020a]. These mechanisms have the potential to be used in a wide range of
critical applications deployed in Kubernetes today. Three types of probes are
provided by the Kubernetes platform [Kubernetes, 2020a]:
• Readiness probe: evaluates whether a container is ready to accept requests.
If it fails, the endpoint controller removes the IP of the pod containing the
failed container from all matching endpoints. A pod is considered ready if
all of its containers are ready. Otherwise, it is removed from the service load
balancers.
• Liveness probe: continuously probes the container to assess its health. If a
failure is detected, the container is killed and the restart policy controls the
next operation performed. When a container is killed, a new one is created.
• Startup probe: verifies whether the application within a container is
started. Liveness and readiness probes are disabled until this probe’s check
is successful.
A probe can be defined using three different approaches: an HTTP request (e.g.,
a GET request); a TCP connection, which connects to a specified port; and a user-
defined command (e.g., check if a file exists).
After the container is started, the startup probe validates the correct initialization
of the container. Then the readiness and liveness probes kick in. The readiness
probe verifies that the container can receive and process requests. The liveness
probe performs continuous checks and restarts the container if it detects a failing
state.
cAdvisor
Container Advisor (cAdvisor) keeps track of a container’s performance metrics
and resource utilization [Google, inc, 2018]. This tool is a daemon that gathers,
combines, processes, and exports data about containers that are currently in use
in a variety of formats [Google, inc, 2018]. The tool records network statistics,
resource isolation parameters, and past resource usage for every container.
As a main feature, it offers a REST API through which queries can be made to
obtain the metrics that it has gathered. With the help of this feature, cAdvisor can
25

Chapter 2
be effortlessly integrated with an information processor that handles specialized
tasks while still remaining independent and modular.
cAdvisor focuses on performance issues like CPU usage and offers guidance on
container performance, even though it offers native support for Docker contain-
ers [Google, inc, 2018].
2.3.4 Security Tools for Containers
Containers provide a popular, flexible, and portable way of distributing and de-
ploying applications, but they also pose relevant security threats that need to be
taken into account when using them. There are security tools available to tackle
security issues that can arise across the complete pipeline when developing and
deploying applications that use containers. Besides common concerns with stor-
age and network security, developers need to keep in mind environment and
orchestration security along with the obvious concerns with registry and runtime
security.
To accomplish container security across the pipeline, there are multiple tools com-
monly used by developers. Here, we detail a few that are very popular and fre-
quently used in research papers as well.
Clair
Clair is an open source project that provides a static vulnerability analysis tool for
Application Container Spec (appc)6 and Docker containers, allowing developers
to keep an eye on the security of their containers. First developed by CoreOS
and currently part of the CNCF, it scans container images using a layer-by-layer
analysis engine to find known security vulnerabilities in the included software,
such as frameworks and libraries [Documentation, 2024]. It enables the detection
of security vulnerabilities by integrating with CI/CD pipelines that automate the
scanning of container images during the build process. At the same time, it en-
sures efficient and scalable operation, which is critical when working with large
container-based applications. Clair provides a API that allows for easy integra-
tion with other solutions that developers may use in the deployment pipeline,
contributing to a combination and possible centralized visualization of any build
issues that may occur.
Calico
Calico is an open source network security tool designed primarily for container
environments [Tigera, 2024]. It provides network policy enforcement as one of its
main features, allowing system administrators to set communication rules based
on labels, ensuring isolation and preventing lateral movement or unauthorized
access to resources. Calico is efficient and scalable, and is tightly integrated with
Kubernetes, using the Kubernetes API server for policy enforcement. By provid-
ing strong networking and policy enforcement capabilities designed specifically
6appc is an open specification that defines multiple aspects of how to run appli-
cations in containers: an image format, runtime environment, and discovery protocol
(rocket.readthedocs.io/en/latest/Documentation/app-container).
26

Background and Related Work
for Kubernetes and containerized environments, Calico makes a relevant contri-
bution to improving container security.
Snyk
Founded in late 2015, Snyk has been steadily gaining momentum and will con-
tinue to do so [Podjarny, 2021]. Snyk offers a wide range of products, all of which
deal with the security of container applications, whether through source code
analysis or even by examining cloud configurations. It covers almost all major,
well-known and widely used systems and supports a wide range of languages
and deployments.
One of Snyk’s products is included in the Docker command line, making it pos-
sible to scan a Docker image for vulnerabilities. When Snyk receives an image
as input, it examines the installed dependencies and packages, compares them
to a database of vulnerability data, and generates a list of vulnerabilities ranked
by severity along with comprehensive details about each one. It is able to pro-
vide a comprehensive and detailed list that the developer can review and search
for solutions because of the large and highly consistent database. It is scalable
for systems with many microservices and helps in vulnerability assessment of
different containers.
Falco
Falco is a cloud-based security threat detection engine for Kubernetes [Falco,
2024], developed by Sysdig, Inc. and donated to the CNCF. This tool provides
a container-native approach to monitoring distributed infrastructure, collecting
events and applying a set of user-defined rules to report any detected malicious
activity on the platform. In this way, Falco can provide scalable and effective
rule-based intrusion detection with a set of pre-defined and easy-to-deploy poli-
cies. Despite all the benefits, the tool only provides point-based threat detection,
meaning that it can report the use of an unauthorized system call, but is unable
to map a collection of anomalies to provide a contextual report [Gantikow et al.,
2019].
2.4 Dependability and Security
The basic concepts of dependability and security of computer systems rely on
three areas: attributes, threats, and means [Avizienis et al., 2004]. Dependability
and security are defined based on a set of attributes that must be ensured during
the design and development of a system to face a set of threats that can com-
promise the delivery of the intended service, through the application of various
means whose main goal is to enable engineers to develop trustworthy applica-
tions that meet the requirements [Avizienis et al., 2004]. Figure 2.9 outlines the
taxonomy of secure and dependable computing [Avizienis et al., 2004].
The definitions of the various attributes that form the basis for security and de-
pendability are defined as follows [Avizienis et al., 2004]:
27

Chapter 2
. Fault prevention means to prevent the occurrence or
introduction of faults.
. Fault tolerance means to avoid service failures in the
presence of faults.
. Fault removal means to reduce the number and
severity of faults.
. Fault forecasting means to estimate the present
number, the future incidence, and the likely con-
sequences of faults.
Fault prevention and fault tolerance aim to provide the
ability to deliver a service that can be trusted, while fault
removal and fault forecasting aim to reach confidence in
that ability by justifying that the functional and the
dependability and security specifications are adequate and
that the system is likely to meet them.
2.5 Summary: The Dependability and Security Tree
The schema of the complete taxonomy of dependable and
secure computing as outlined in this section is shown in Fig. 2.
3T HE THREATS TO DEPENDABILITY AND SECURITY
3.1 System Life Cycle: Phases and Environments
In this section, we present the taxonomy of threats that may
affect a system during its entire life. The life cycle of a
system consists of two phases: development and use.
The development phase includes all activities from
presentation of the user’s initial concept to the decision that
the system has passed all acceptance tests and is ready to
deliver service in its user’s environment. During the
development phase, the system interacts with the develop-
ment environment and development faults may be introduced
into the system by the environment. The development
environment of a system consists of the following elements:
1. the physical world with its natural phenomena,
2. human developers , some possibly lacking competence
or having malicious objectives,
3. development tools: software and hardware used by the
developers to assist them in the development
process,
4. production and test facilities .
The use phase of a system’s life begins when the system
is accepted for use and starts the delivery of its services to
the users. Use consists of alternating periods of correct
service delivery (to be called service delivery ), service
outage, and service shutdown. A service outage is caused by
a service failure. It is the period when incorrect service
(including no service at all) is delivered at the service
interface. A service shutdown is an intentional halt of
service by an authorized entity. Maintenance actions may
take place during all three periods of the use phase.
During the use phase, the system interacts with its use
environment and may be adversely affected by faults
originating in it. The use environment consists of the
following elements:
1. the physical world with its natural phenomena;
2. administrators (including maintainers): entities (hu-
mans or other systems) that have the authority to
manage, modify, repair and use the system; some
authorized humans may lack competence or have
malicious objectives;
3. users: entities (humans or other systems) that receive
service from the system at their use interfaces;
4. providers: entities (humans or other systems) that
deliver services to the system at its use interfaces;
5. the infrastructure : entities that provide specialized
services to the system, such as information sources
(e.g., time, GPS, etc.), communication links, power
sources, cooling airflow, etc.
6. intruders :m a l i c i o u se n t i t i e s( h u m a n sa n do t h e r
systems) that attempt to exceed any authority they
might have and alter service or halt it, alter the
system’s functionality or performance, or to access
confidential information. Examples include hackers,
vandals, corrupt insiders, agents of hostile govern-
ments or organizations, and malicious software.
As used here, the term maintenance, following common
usage, includes not only repairs, but also all modifications
of the system that take place during the use phase of system
life. Therefore, maintenance is a development process, and
the preceding discussion of development applies to main-
tenance as well. The various forms of maintenance are
summarized in Fig. 3.
It is noteworthy that repair and fault tolerance are
related concepts; the distinction between fault tolerance and
maintenance in this paper is that maintenance involves the
participation of an external agent, e.g., a repairman, test
equipment, remote reloading of software. Furthermore,
repair is part of fault removal (during the use phase), and
fault forecasting usually considers repair situations. In fact,
repair can be seen as a fault tolerance activity within a
larger system that includes the system being repaired and
the people and other systems that perform such repairs.
14 IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON DEPENDABLE AND SECURE COMPUTING, VOL. 1, NO. 1, JANUARY-MARCH 2004
Fig. 1. Dependability and security attributes.
Fig. 2. The dependability and security tree.
Figure 2.9: The dependability and security tree. (from Avizienis et al. [2004])
• Availability: readiness for correct service;
• Reliability: continuity of correct service;
• Safety: absence of catastrophic consequences for the user(s) and the envi-
ronment;
• Confidentiality: absence of unauthorized disclosure of information;
• Integrity: absence of improper system modifications;
• Maintainability: ability to be modified and repaired.
Dependability is an integration of the characteristics defined above, while secu-
rity, which is a part of dependability, is a composite of confidentiality, integrity,
and availability. The threats to the security and dependability of a system begin
with the presence of a fault, which is a flaw in the implementation of a system
that, when activated, can cause an error in the internal state of the system, which,
when observed from the outside, is called a failure [Avizienis et al., 2004]. Fig-
ure 2.10 depicts the flow of fault, error and failure.
Figure 2.10: Chain of Dependability and security threats. (adapted
from Avizienis et al. [2004])
Regarding the means to achieve dependable and secure systems, these have
mainly revolved around four main groups: prevention, tolerance, removal, and
forecasting of faults. While fault prevention and fault tolerance aim to ensure
that the system is trustworthy, the use of fault removal and fault prediction
aims to provide confidence that the system meets the dependability and security
requirements [Avizienis et al., 2004].
28

Background and Related Work
The concepts of trust and trustworthiness are very important, especially in the
context of microservices. The concept of trust serves as the foundation for trusted
computing and consists of the accepted dependence of one component on a set of
properties of another component, subsystem, or system [Veríssimo et al., 2003].
In the case of trustworthiness, it is the degree to which a component, subsystem,
or system satisfies a set of properties [Veríssimo et al., 2003].
Now, when malicious intent comes into the picture (e.g., from a malicious actor),
there are additional concepts that need to be considered. A system has a bound-
ary that represents the boundary between it and its environment [Avizienis et al.,
2004]. This boundary can be studied and evaluated from a security perspective
by applying an attack surface calculation methodology [Manadhata and Wing,
2011]. A malicious agent attacks a system through its vulnerabilities, which are
flaws that can be exploited with malicious intent [Veríssimo et al., 2003].
For the purpose of security concerns, the dependability chain has been adapted
in the Attack-Vulnerability-Intrusion (AVI) model, as shown in Figure 2.11 [Verís-
simo et al., 2003]. A security vulnerability is analogous to a reliability vulnera-
bility. The act of exploiting a vulnerability in the system constitutes an attack,
while a successful exploitation (attack) constitutes a security intrusion into a sys-
tem [Veríssimo et al., 2003; MAFTIA, 2002; Welch et al., 2003].
Figure 2.11: AVI composite fault model. (image from Veríssimo et al. [2003])
The way microservices create dynamic environments requires that security and
dependability concepts be introduced at design time, using techniques such as
fault and intrusion tolerance or access control to increase the level of security.
However, even with all the policies and patterns in place, malicious activity still
needs to be monitored. Unknown vulnerabilities, unanticipated attack vectors or
strategies require an online approach that can adapt to changes over time, such as
behavior-based intrusion detection. These mechanisms allow systems to address
relevant security vulnerabilities and should be employed to complement the use
of intrusion detection. The following sections provide details on concepts that
can be used to increase the security and reliability of systems, as well as other
threats they may face.
2.4.1 Fault Tolerance
Based on dependability concepts and research, several works have focused on
fault tolerance for microservices as a measure to provide capabilities for continu-
ous operation even in the presence of deviations from the intended service level.
29

Chapter 2
Fault tolerance has been applied to different levels of cloud computing, with dif-
ferent approaches ranging from architectural topology to different mechanisms,
both proactive and reactive [Hasan and Goraya, 2018; Kumari and Kaur, 2021].
Fault tolerance for microservice applications has also received attention, with sev-
eral papers on the topic. The research conducted has focused on implementing
fault tolerance as a pluggable framework that operates based on complementary
microservices to process events for real-time fault detection and fault pattern de-
tection to proactively mitigate fault activation [Power and Kotonya, 2018]. Tech-
niques to enable fault-tolerant xApps in the RAN Intelligent Controller platform
without compromising scalability have been researched, exploring the concept of
fault tolerance in low-latency networks through state partitioning, partial repli-
cation, and fast re-routing with role awareness [Huff et al., 2021].
Fault tolerance mechanisms have been built into microservice orchestrators, such
as Kubernetes, to monitor system components and take actions to overcome
and tolerate potential problems. Kubernetes provides self-healing Kubernetes
probes7, which periodically perform diagnostics on containers implementing
services and are used by Kubernetes to continuously verify that operations are
performing as expected. Although previous work has shown some shortcomings
of these mechanisms [Flora et al., 2022], it is still advisable to implement probes
in the microservices deployed in Kubernetes, as a discussion of the usefulness of
probes for horizontal autoscaling and how the underlying Kubernetes platform,
when operating under extreme conditions, affects the applications deployed on
it [Larsson et al., 2020].
2.4.2 Software Aging
Software aging can be defined as the accumulation of resource utilization owing
to faults present in the software released to production [Parnas, 1994]. These
faults, which are hard to detect during testing, have an effect on the system over
time, tackling the performance and resulting in higher response times. Software
defects are inevitable, so aging is likely to occur due to the accumulation of their
effects. There are several approaches to recovering a system from software aging,
but the most common is to restart the application with the goal of restoring it to a
clean state; this process is called rejuvenation [Parnas, 1994; Grottke et al., 2008].
Several methods can be used to estimate the time to aging failure, primarily
through aging indicators such as resource usage or reliability attributes [Grot-
tke et al., 2008; Pietrantuono and Russo, 2020; Cotroneo et al., 2014]. Web servers
have been studied with different models used to monitor the system [Li et al.,
2002]. This work was then extended to focus on Apache, concluding that when
subjected to overwhelming workloads there are traces of aging, but also minor
rejuvenation [Grottke et al., 2006].
However, aging in microservices has not received significant attention. Previous
work performs a preliminary study on the topic by applying known principles
7Kubernetes Probes are a monitoring and adaptive feature of the Kubernetes orchestrator, for
details see Section 2.3.3.
30

Background and Related Work
of aging to microservices and observing the results [Yue et al., 2020]. To detect
aging, a deep learning model was applied and proved to be effective. However,
it does not deepen the issue, but only proves that it is a relevant issue that needs
more attention. Some previous works have used fault injection to accelerate aging
effects on different types of systems for testing and evaluation purposes [Alonso
et al., 2011; Langner and Andrzejak, 2013], but without focusing on microser-
vices. Other work has focused on container engines [Vinícius et al., 2022] and
containerized applications [Tola et al., 2021], showing that aging is a concern.
Leveraging the containerization of microservices to introduce more measures of
rejuvenation may be an avenue to explore. The low cost of creating and moving
containers, as opposed to virtual machines, allows for less impact on availability
and response time penalties that can be leveraged when deciding when to apply
proactive rejuvenation measures.
2.4.3 Intrusion Detection
Intrusion detection is the process of monitoring system events and analyzing
them for possible incidents [Bace and Mell, 2001], automated by IDSs [Bace and
Mell, 2001] (see Section 2.5 for details on IDSs). Over the last two decades, this
technique has been applied to various contexts [Hofmeyr et al., 1998; Bharadwaja
et al., 2011; Abed et al., 2015; Srinivasan et al., 2019; Flora and Antunes, 2019],
and recently attention has shifted to microservices architectures despite existing
limitations [Pereira-Vale et al., 2019; Hannousse and Yahiouche, 2020]. The pro-
cess of intrusion detection follows two main approaches; the most established
in industrial systems is a signature-based process, which tries to identify pat-
terns of known attacks [Bace and Mell, 2001], and an anomaly-based approach,
where deviations from learned behavior are reported as attacks [Chandola et al.,
2009]. However, some papers also use a hybrid approach that combines the two
approaches [Milenkoski et al., 2015b]. More details about each approach can be
found in Section 2.5.2.
Regardless of the approach, the use of IDSs as a security countermeasure is rec-
ommended because of their ability to provide information about events occurring
on the infrastructure and allow system administrators to act quickly upon detec-
tion of attacks [Bace and Mell, 2001; John Felber, 2008]. They provide good guar-
antees against external and internal attacks, which is why the technique needs to
be studied in the context of microservices.
Some attempts at container-based intrusion detection have been made [Abed
et al., 2015; Srinivasan et al., 2019; Röhling et al., 2019], which is relevant to this
work because microservices are commonly deployed using containers.
A research paper proposed the use of Bags of System Calls (BoSC) to represent
the normal behavior database entries [Abed et al., 2015]. The methodology con-
sidered an anomaly-based IDS which, during the experimental analysis, obtained
a True Positive Rate (TPR) of 100% with a False Positive Rate (FPR) of 2%. Other
works have considered an approach based on the NGram probability with Maxi-
31

Chapter 2
mum Likelihood Estimator8 and Simple Good Turing9 techniques to obtain prob-
abilities of unseen values. Results from experimental campaigns using a web ap-
plication inside a Docker container and the UNM dataset yielded accuracy values
in the range of 87-97%, recall values in the range of 78-100%, and FPR values in
the range of 0-14%. While these are steps in the right direction, the representa-
tiveness has several shortcomings and more realistic scenarios are needed.
Other research has addressed intrusion detection for microservice-based systems,
for example, researchers have proposed an optimized intra/inter-class-structure-
based variational few-shot learning model [Liang et al., 2021]. The proposed
model addresses out-of-distribution problems in unbalanced learning by opti-
mizing intra-class distances through a variational Bayesian process and inter-
class distances through feature concatenation. Evaluation experiments on two
public datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the model, particularly in de-
tecting novel attacks in highly unbalanced data scenarios, outperforming four
baseline methods [Liang et al., 2021].
For container-based environments, intrusion detection research has also covered
the use of anomaly detection and proposed Ab-HIDS, which focuses on using N-
grams of system call frequencies and incorporates two ensemble learning models
- voting-based and XGBoost - for training and testing [Joraviya et al., 2024]. The
evaluation, conducted on the Leipzig Intrusion Detection Data Set, shows that the
methodology achieves an average 5% increase in detection accuracy and reduces
the false positive rate by 0.9% and 0.8% [Joraviya et al., 2024].
2.4.4 Anomaly Detection
Anomaly detection is a methodology that aims at identifying data patterns that
do not comply with the expected or normal behavior [Chandola et al., 2009]. The
non-compliant data patterns are deviations from the typical behavior, also known
as outliers. The application of anomaly detection has seen widespread adoption
in several areas, such as intrusion detection, fraud detection, medical anomaly de-
tection, or image processing. Figure 2.12 illustrates an example of data classified
for anomaly detection; in this image we can observe that two groups have been
formed (N1 and N2), where most of the observations fall into, being considered as
normal behavior, and the points o1 and o2 and the region O3 are considered as
anomalies.
There are several challenges in using anomaly detection methods [Chandola
et al., 2009]. Defining a normal region is cumbersome because it is not a very
precise boundary, and data points close to the boundary tend to have some
misclassifications. In addition, when detecting malicious activity, attackers try
to disguise their activity to appear as normal behavior, which increases the
difficulty of detection; also, the definition of normal behavior in this context
8Maximum Likelihood Estimator is a method for estimating the parameters of a distribution
through the maximization of a likelihood function, so that the observed data is most probable in
the presumed model [Abaeian et al., 2015].
9Simple Good T uringis a technique for estimating the probability of previously unseen classes
given past observations of different classes [Gale and Sampson, 1995].
32

Background and Related Work
Figure 2.12: Simple example of anomaly detection. (from Chandola et al. [2009])
requires frequent updates to keep up with its evolution. Anomaly detection is
a useful approach for several domains, but it is not possible to define a general
threshold for raising an anomaly signal, since different domains have different
characteristics. A major obstacle to defining meaningful and complete normal
behavior profiles is the availability of labeled data, which results in either more
focus on algorithms that require only one class (see Figure 2.13).
Some examples of the use of anomaly detection in intrusion detection are works
such as those described in the Section 2.4.3. These mainly focus on collecting
system call data and using it to construct a normal behavior profile of a system;
further, they detect the attacks by assuming a deviation from the normal behavior.
In the area of fraud detection, the application of anomaly detection focuses
on detecting criminal activity in institutions such as banks or stock markets,
where malicious agents try to pose as legitimate customers and use resources
illegally [Chandola et al., 2009]. Detecting such events is extremely important in
order to prevent economic losses for legitimate customers and companies.
Several works have been done in the field of anomaly detection, focusing on in-
trusion detection in general and host-based anomaly detection in particular. In
addition to using system calls to detect malicious behavior, researchers have pro-
posed to consider the attributes of the call invocation, such as its return value
Figure 2.13: Example of one- and multi-class anomaly detection.
(from Chandola et al. [2009])
33

Chapter 2
and arguments [Tandon and Chan, 2005]. The experimental validation conducted
demonstrated the effectiveness of the proposed approaches, with the argument-
based representation and the representation merging system call sequence and
argument information of the current system call achieving the most promising re-
sults in detecting Denial-of-Service (DoS) and remote-to-local attacks, while the
root-to-user attacks were more accurately detected using a sequence of system
calls representation. The authors also provide an analysis of some of the detected
anomalies. Nevertheless, the work has some limitations, mostly related to the
overhead created by the complexity of the techniques.
The utilization of machine learning techniques, in particular of Artificial Neu-
ral Networks (ANNs) and Support Vector Machines (SVM), has been explored
by several works [Chen et al., 2005]. These approaches have demonstrated their
ability to provide useful and effective profiles of the behavior of the systems to be
monitored. In this work, the authors combine ANNs and SVM with two different
coding methods to understand which one achieves better results in intrusion de-
tection using the DARPA 1998 dataset. The results show high FPRs for each case
where the models achieve usable detection rates, which reduces the reliability
of the results produced, still the experiments show that SVM using the t f× id f
encoding approach performs better than the rest, but improvements are needed.
A research paper proposed an anomaly detection methodology focused on re-
ducing the false positive rate, which is a major threat to the adoption of behavior-
based intrusion detection systems, through the combination of frequency with
the temporal information from the system call traces and uses One Class Support
Vector Machines (OCSVM), which eliminates the need to provide information
about malicious events during training [Khreich et al., 2017]. In this case, the clas-
sifiers construct only a normal behavior profile as opposed to SVM where a hy-
perplane is developed for each type of data [Cortes and Vapnik, 1995; Chen et al.,
2005]. A feature extraction is proposed to segment sequences of system calls into
n-grams of variable length, which, after being mapped to fixed-size traces, are
fed to the classifier for training. The approach lowered the false positive rate with
high detection accuracy compared to other state-of-the-art techniques. However,
the authors should have provided results for the classifiers’ accuracy, as these
metrics suffer from biases and can be misleading [David Martin Powers, 2011].
2.4.5 Intrusion Tolerance
Despite the application of very effective IDSs, the occurrence of intrusions must
be taken seriously. That is, the application of a defense in depth approach further
reduces the risk faced by computer systems [Viega and McGraw, 2002]. Thus,
IDSs should be complemented with measures that provide additional guaran-
tees of Quality of Service (QoS) when these systems are misled or fail to detect
malicious behavior.
Intrusion tolerance [MAFTIA, 2002; Welch et al., 2003; Veríssimo et al., 2003]
builds on the concepts of fault tolerance [Avizienis et al., 2004] and allows sys-
tems to continue to provide the expected service even when security intrusions
34

Background and Related Work
occur [Veríssimo et al., 2003; Stavridou et al., 2001; Deswarte et al., 1991]. Systems
whose robustness and reliability are higher incorporate the ability to withstand
higher adversarial capabilities due to the mechanisms and design patterns used.
One such mechanism is intrusion tolerance, which can be proactively built into
the architectural design [MAFTIA, 2002; Welch et al., 2003], through the appli-
cation of independent replication and redundancy of services and components,
or data dispersion, or through the application of additional measures such as
proactive migration of resources when parts of the infrastructure stack are com-
promised [Kuang and Zulkernine, 2008].
This mechanism has been the focus of several research projects and papers in
the past. Organically Assured and Survivable Information System (OASIS) was
a program of the US Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency (DARPA)
that funded 35 projects [Duren, 2004] with the goal of providing “defense
capabilities against sophisticated adversaries to allow sustained operation of mission
critical functions in the face of known and future cyber-attacks against information
systems” [Veríssimo et al., 2003]. This program had a strong emphasis on
intrusion tolerance as a way to develop systems capable of providing secure
services despite being based on potentially vulnerable components [Cukier et al.,
2001; Wang et al., 2003a]. Several projects have focused on measures such as
redundancy, graceful degradation, or diversity [Cukier et al., 2001; Wang et al.,
2003a] to provide systems with intrusion tolerance capabilities. Furthermore,
the Malicious- and Accidental-Fault Tolerance for Internet Applications (MAF-
TIA) [MAFTIA, 2002; Welch et al., 2003] project was an initiative funded by
the European Union (EU) whose main goal was to research the development
and design of large-scale dependable distributed applications following the
intrusion tolerance paradigm [MAFTIA, 2002]. The project acted in three main
action areas, namely: definition of architectural and conceptual models; design
of mechanisms and protocols; and validation and evaluation [MAFTIA, 2002].
The main methods for applying intrusion tolerance are [Wang et al., 2003b]:
Redundancy and Diversity: Redundancy can act on three areas to provide sys-
tems with tolerant properties, namely: information, time, and resources.
This measure consists of providing a system with additional resources be-
yond its normal operating requirements [Wang et al., 2003b]. To effectively
reduce the probability of being affected by an intrusion, diversity must also
be applied so that redundant components do not suffer from the same vul-
nerabilities, thus enforcing the independent fault assumption [Wang et al.,
2003b; Ouffoué et al., 2019].
Detection and Recovery: This approach is based on detecting an error after
an attack has successfully penetrated the system. Error recovery is the tech-
nique that allows the system to provide service despite the error and recover
from the consequences of an intrusion [Veríssimo et al., 2003].
Voting: The application of redundancy measures requires a consensus to be
reached after redundant execution. A voting mechanism acts as an ag-
gregator of outputs produced by multiple components and ensures that a
35

Chapter 2
non-faulty output is propagated [Wang et al., 2003b]. There are several ap-
proaches to voting mechanisms, among the most common are formalized
majority voting, generalized median voting, and formalized plurality vot-
ing [Wang et al., 2003b].
Fragmentation redundancy scattering: This technique is based on a process of
fragmentation of the data to be concealed, which is then replicated and dis-
tributed among the components of a system in order to reduce the like-
lihood of the fragments being collected and the confidentiality of the in-
formation being violated. The replication (redundancy) of some fragments
allows overcoming the deletion of some information [Wang et al., 2003b].
Dynamic Reconfiguration: This concept typically draws on detection of intru-
sions, although it can also be regarded as a proactive measure [Wang et al.,
2003b; Ouffoué et al., 2019]. It allows systems to dynamically modify their
behavior towards a client (e.g., blocking it), remove or restart a malfunction-
ing component (e.g., rollover), or change traffic or information flows (e.g.,
load sharing). Nevertheless, dynamic reconfiguration should be applied
cautiously owing to its possible performance penalty and possibility of be-
ing leveraged by a malicious agent if compromised [Wang et al., 2003b].
Threshold Scheme and Distributed Trust: This principle builds on crypto-
graphic concepts, where a secret is divided into n pieces and distributed
across the infrastructure while requiring at least k pieces to be combined to
retrieve the information back, otherwise it does not reveal any useful data.
The application of this method requires a trade-off between performance,
availability, and confidentiality constrained by the selection of the values
for n and k [Wang et al., 2003b; Ouffoué et al., 2019].
Indirection: It consists in the insertion of barriers in between components, or
clients and servers, aiming at providing an additional protection, such as
a proxy, a wrapper or a sandbox. The integration of an indirection comes
with performance costs owing to the overhead introduced of an additional
logical and processing layer that should be weighed when designing sys-
tems [Wang et al., 2003b; Ouffoué et al., 2019].
In the context of microservices, the application of intrusion tolerance still has
open avenues that can be explored to take advantage of fast and easy service
instantiation and more efficient application of intrusion-tolerant policies.
Nevertheless, there have been several works in other domains and environments
where intrusion tolerance has been explored. For example, one work proposed
an intrusion tolerance approach for distributed systems based on physically
replicated distribution of pieces of information to different parties in the sys-
tem [Deswarte et al., 1991]. The proposed approach requires trust and consensus
of a majority of entities, thus tolerating malicious activity from some actors and
still ensuring the security properties were not affected [Deswarte et al., 1991].
Authors of other work in for intrusion tolerant systems aim at providing appli-
cations with capabilities to survive the consequences of intrusion, thus working
36

Background and Related Work
at the middleware level, allowing adaptive properties that in turn make appli-
cations aware of changes so that they are able to participate in their defense by
improving detection mechanisms [Pal et al., 2000].
A research paper examined the impact of OS diversity on the number of identified
bugs. The authors analyzed several OSs in terms of vulnerabilities reported over
a 15-year period with the aim of understanding the similarity of bugs across the
diversity of OSs [Garcia et al., 2011]. The conclusions reached indicate that the
use of different operating systems, that is OS diversity, increases the tolerance of
the system since the repetition of vulnerabilities is very low [Garcia et al., 2011].
A framework has been proposed to address the challenges of recovering mi-
croservices from intrusions that compromise application state [Matos et al., 2023].
µVerum is tailored to the distributed and heterogeneous nature of microservices
by logging user requests and operations across multiple microservices, and has
been implemented to allow evaluation of its recovery capabilities. The evalua-
tion demonstrates its ability to revert intrusion effects in one second while the
application remains operational [Matos et al., 2023].
2.5 Intrusion Detection Systems
An IDS can be referred to as a piece of software that allows the automation of
the intrusion detection process [Milenkoski, 2016]. In addition, there are different
methods by which IDSs can be designed, as distributed or not, there are also
different monitoring methods, real time or polling and the target for which is
implemented, host or network.
2.5.1 Monitoring Target
A IDS has one main target to monitor and keeps a close eye on it, collecting data
about it and trying to detect intrusion attempts against it. There are two main
types of targets, which are either machines, i.e., host-based, or networks, i.e.,
network-based, although in some cases it is possible to monitor both types.
A Host-based Intrusion Detection System (HIDS)monitors the activity of a host
and the events that occur within it in order to detect malicious actions. This aspect
allows HIDSs to analyze activity with high reliability and precision, being able to
infer the origin of an attack against the OS, namely which users were involved
and which processes were affected [Bace and Mell, 2001]. Typically, these mecha-
nisms use OS events, such as system calls or inter-process communications, and
system logs generated during its operation, monitor them, and try to detect the
presence of malicious actions in them.
These characteristics give HIDSs the ability to detect code execution attacks by
monitoring activities such as stack and heap usage or system calls issued by a
process, and file system violations through the use of mechanisms such as file
integrity checking or unauthorized file access attempts. The main reasons for
37

Chapter 2
Figure 2.14: Deployment of network and host-based IDS. (from Stephen [2024])
such results are the ability to monitor local events and, if the network traffic is
encrypted, HIDSs are not affected because the information is decrypted when it
arrives at the target host. However, these IDSs are costly to manage due to their
well-defined and narrow configuration, as they only work with that particular
machine, and they also consume memory on the host they reside on, as well as
computing resources, which can lead to performance degradation for the system
being monitored [Bace and Mell, 2001].
Among the IDSs that use host-based intrusion detection is the Sagan IDS10.
A network-based IDS monitors network traffic between devices present in net-
work segments, analyzing packets from layers of the network stack, such as the
transport layer and the application layer, to detect malicious actions. Typically,
this type of IDSs is often deployed in dedicated machines or sensors, which al-
lows to more easily ensure its security due to the single-task devices are respon-
sible for. These devices are generally placed as a passive asset on a network,
observing a copy of the traffic packet’s data, such as the origin and destination
of the packet, and raising alerts when appropriate. However, on some occasions,
IDSs are also placed as any other device present on the network, this is called
inline mode, and these analyze the actual traffic passing through them. Figure 2.14
depicts these two modes of deployment.
As a result of their placement, IDSs can collect a huge amount of information
about the network in which they are deployed, such as host identification, com-
municating applications, and even the OSs running on the machines. Thus, all
this information can be processed and logged for forensic procedures if needed.
In addition, these security measures are able to detect application layer, transport
layer, and network layer reconnaissance and attacks, such as password guessing,
port scanning, and IP spoofing [Scarfone and Mell, 2007]. Nonetheless, they can-
not analyze encrypted traffic or process fragmented packets and also face com-
plications to handle high number of packets, for example when a network is very
busy, which may result in undetected attacks [Bace and Mell, 2001].
A research paper has proposed an ensemble learning-based IDS for a zero-touch
network automation process by leveraging ML to improve the trustworthiness
10https://github.com/quadrantsec/sagan
38

Background and Related Work
of IoT systems [Bugshan et al., 2023]. The approach uses a feature selection tech-
nique to elicit the most relevant ones for the model to use, and mix the predictions
using an ensemble learning approach. The evaluation shows higher precision, ac-
curacy, and recall than the alternatives [Bugshan et al., 2023].
An example of an IDS which monitors network traffic and detects intrusion at-
tempts is the Suricata IDS11.
2.5.2 Detection Approach
The intrusion detection applied used by IDSs generally follows one of three main
ways: signature detection, anomaly detection, and hybrid detection.
The signature-based approach consists of compiling previously used attack pat-
terns and using them to identify their occurrence in network traffic or on a com-
puter system. These attacks typically exploit known vulnerabilities with attacks
that have been used in previous actions.
This approach is very effective in detecting attacks without generating a high
number of false positives, but it lacks the ability to improve its detection range
without constantly updating the signature database. The misuse approach al-
lows to quickly and reliably diagnose the attack tool or technique used, but the
inability to adapt and detect variations of known attacks is a major drawback.
Several research papers have leveraged this approach due to its effectiveness and
nearly nonexistence of false positives, when correctly applied, to detect network
malicious actions. An example would be a research paper in which a study con-
ducted compiles two novel evaluation metrics and a framework to evaluate dif-
ferent IDSs that use signatures to detect malicious events [Le Jeune et al., 2021].
The anomaly-based approach relies on the assumption that illegitimate activity
that may occur on a system or network differs from the behavior that character-
izes the normal activity of that system or network. Thus, the creation of a profile
of a system or network during legitimate activity, using data such as the normal
behavior of users or network connections, allows the detection of deviations and
thus the detection of the occurrence of intrusion attempts [Bace and Mell, 2001].
These features allow the detection of new attacks without requiring prior knowl-
edge of them, which in turn can be used to define signatures for misuse detection.
However, this approach typically produces many false positives due to the diffi-
culty of defining a stable profile of a system or network, which requires a large
amount of data to produce reliable results.
This intrusion detection approach is widely used [Islam et al., 2018b; Khreich
et al., 2018]. One proposes a solution that follows the MapReduce12 programming
11https://suricata.io
12MapReduce is a programming model with an accompanying implementation for big data
processing of large datasets in a parallel, distributed manner using a cluster. It applies a two-
step procedure where initially performs filtering and sorting followed by a reduce step, where it
merges all intermediate values into the final result [Dean and Ghemawat, 2004].
39

Chapter 2
model and profiles large system call traces using anomaly detectors, resulting in a
scalable and effective approach to detecting bad actors based on host data [Islam
et al., 2018b]. The other presents a multi-algorithm IDS that combines decisions
using boolean combinations, which reduces false positives while outperforming
single-algorithm IDSs [Khreich et al., 2018].
There are IDSs that apply a hybrid detection approach and use both signature
and anomaly detection methods [Milenkoski, 2016]. Thus, this method can detect
both known and unknown attacks. An example of this detection approach is a
research paper that presented a hybrid architecture to detect malicious events in
networks based on a decision support component that combines both approaches
to classify multiple types of attacks [Depren et al., 2005].
2.5.3 Detection Algorithms
An intrusion detection system uses one or more algorithms to analyze events
and identify malicious activity. Several types of algorithms have been used in the
past [Liu et al., 2019].
For Machine Learning (ML), ANNs consists of a graph-like structure with mul-
tiple processing units (nodes) and weighted links between them. The weight of
each link determines the influence of a node’s value on the outcome of the output
node [Ghosh et al., 1999]. The nodes of ANNs are divided into three subsets: the
input nodes, the intermediate nodes, and the output nodes. The input nodes are
the starting point of the computations, which are distributed to the intermedi-
ate processing units through existing connections, and arrive at the output nodes
where the final results are produced [Ghosh et al., 1999]. ANN is a supervised
learning method able to generalize from incomplete data [Dua and Du, 2011].
K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) is based on distance heuristics between new and
classified data as a way to group the events into different classes based on their
similarity. For a new event, the K nearest neighbors are computed, and the most
frequent label among them is assigned to it [Liao and Vemuri, 2002].
SVM uses prior knowledge acquired during training to classify events into dif-
ferent classes separated by a hyperplane defined by a set of support vectors used
to define boundaries [Yuxin et al., 2011; Creech and Hu, 2014].
For an anomaly detection approach, it is common to use OCSVM, where this class
defines the normal behavior. OCSVM eliminates the need to expose the model to
anomalous traffic during training. This feature also increases the probability of
detecting unknown attacks. It does not try to classify them [Khreich et al., 2018].
Naïve Bayes (NB) is a statistical approach based on the Bayes theorem and the
assumption that each feature is completely independent. The NB method is a
widely used classifier due to its high scalability and effectiveness. This method
allows classifying new events based on previous ones, resulting in the ability to
identify intrusions with a given probability [Vishwakarma and Kesswani, 2023].
The Hidden Markov Models (HMM) approach is structured as a set of states,
40

Background and Related Work
a matrix of emission probabilities, which is the probability of observing a sym-
bol given the current state, and a matrix of transition probabilities, which is the
probability of transitioning from one state to another given an observation. This
method has also been used in intrusion detection, where a sequence of events is
considered anomalous if the probability of it belonging to the defined profile is
below a certain, user-defined, threshold [Warrender et al., 1999].
The BoSC method is based on a window that slides over a trace of system calls
used to detect intrusions and define a baseline behavior database that contains
bags of system calls that are considered normal. These bags do not take into ac-
count the order of the system calls, but rather focus on the frequency of each
system call within the window being processed [Dae-Ki Kang et al., 2005].
The Sequence Time-Delaying Embedding (STIDE) method is based on a window
that slides over a trace of system calls used to detect intrusions, and defines a
baseline behavioral database that contains the sequences that are considered nor-
mal. This technique preserves the original order of system calls, unlike BoSC,
which is frequency based [Hofmeyr et al., 1998].
2.5.4 Monitoring Method
Another feature that distinguishes IDSs is the monitoring method it uses. This
method can be either real-time, monitoring events as they happen, or polling, not
intercepting events as they happen, but receiving them asynchronously.
IDSs, which monitor events in real time, analyze system and/or network activ-
ities as they occur [Milenkoski, 2016]. The main advantage of these IDSs is the
ability to detect attacks as they happen, but unfortunately this results in the ad-
dition of some overhead that may result in performance loss of the monitored
system. However, this disadvantage may be compensated by the fact that coun-
termeasures against the attack may still allow minimizing or even prevent its
damage. An example of a IDS that monitors events in real time is the one pro-
posed in a research paper where the authors leverage a bi-phase network IDS that
carefully evaluates the decisions of machine and ensemble learning frameworks
to reach a decision [Chowdhury et al., 2023].
On the other hand, polling IDSs do not intercept ongoing actions with the aim of
obtaining information for analysis, instead these devices obtain the required data
in other ways, such as asynchronous retrieval of system logs or periodic obser-
vation of the monitored system [Milenkoski, 2016]. These systems therefore do
not introduce any overhead into the normal flow of information between com-
ponents of the monitored system, but they inevitably cannot generate an alert as
soon as the attack occurs, which means that the damage caused by the attack may
be substantially greater than it could be if it were acted upon when first detected.
One IDS that monitors events asynchronously is the OSSEC IDS13.
13https://www.ossec.net
41

Chapter 2
2.5.5 Architecture
The architecture of IDSs is also a distinguishing feature. A IDS can be either
distributed or non-distributed, consisting of multiple nodes in the former case or
a centralized component in the latter case.
Non-distributed IDSs, also called centralized IDSs, consist of the traditional type
of IDS deployment, in other words, the system is deployed locally, either at the
network or host level, and performs the intended functions [Milenkoski, 2016].
These devices do not have a global view of the machines present on the network
segment or of the network itself, which may make them less successful at detect-
ing coordinated attacks on a set of hosts. Any traditional IDS is a good example
of a non-distributed IDS, such as Snort IDS14.
Distributed IDSs consist of multiple intrusion detection subsystems, called
nodes, that exchange data relevant to the detection of possible intrusions. There
are two ways to communicate, the nodes can establish communication links
among themselves, such as a peer-to-peer network, or with a centralized node
that aggregates the data collected by the remaining nodes to manage them or
perform further analysis on the information available [Milenkoski, 2016]. This
type of architecture allows for a global view of the state of a network or group of
hosts, filling the gap left by non-distributed IDSs by sharing information about
what is happening in multiple locations.
One of the first distributed IDSs is Distributed Intrusion Detection System
(DIDS) [Snapp et al., 1991]. DIDS is a network-based IDS that oversees dis-
tributed machines with a series of monitors that send information to the expert
system, which in turn aggregates the data and defines the security policy for each
host. Based on the collected data, it infers the state of the entire system [Snapp
et al., 1991; Jones and Sielken, 2000].
2.6 Evaluation of Security Countermeasures
To understand the limitations of the approaches being researched and developed,
it is necessary to perform an evaluation procedure capable of producing mean-
ingful results. Depending on the technologies to be evaluated, there are different
procedures that can be applied in this area. Several techniques are available for
evaluating software security, such as security testing, attack surface analysis, and
field measurements [Fonseca et al., 2009].
In the case of IDSs and other security countermeasures, the most common ap-
proach is to perform a benchmarking procedure that allows to evaluate and com-
pare, under the same conditions, different techniques, tools or algorithms with
respect to their ability to detect malicious actions. Such an evaluation process re-
quires the availability of workloads, datasets, and the calculation of meaningful
and well-accepted metrics that represent the performance of the system.
14https://www.snort.org
42

Background and Related Work
Therefore, this section provides details on security benchmarking, workload
types and generation concepts, metrics commonly used in evaluations, and
publicly available and commonly used intrusion detection datasets.
2.6.1 Benchmarking
The standard approach to evaluate and compare different tools or techniques for
a given purpose is benchmarking [Vieira et al., 2012]. A benchmark is a standard
tool that allows its users to evaluate and compare different systems in a fair and
useful way, according to specific aspects such as performance, dependability or
security [Vieira et al., 2012]. A benchmark should adhere to several criteria to
ensure that it is meaningful and useful [Vieira et al., 2012]:
• Relevance: the benchmark should be relevant to the objectives of the eval-
uation, providing insight on the effectiveness of intrusion detection in mi-
croservice environments and their challenges.
• Repeatable and Reproducible: the results obtained can be reproduced un-
der similar conditions, allowing for independent verification. This means
that the IDS results should be statistically similar across multiple runs.
• Portability: the benchmark should be possible and easy to implement on
different systems. That is, it should be able to accommodate multiple types
of intrusion detection algorithms and techniques.
• Simplicity: it should be easy to understand the benchmark and its results
to increase credibility. It should be simple to understand what is being re-
ported and how the results translate to a practical application for security
intrusions.
• Economical: to run the benchmark should be cost-effective. The benchmark
should be able to run on average hardware and should not significantly
increase these requirements as the complexity of the workload increases.
A typical benchmark consists of three main components [Gray, 1993]: i) work-
load, ii) measures, and iii) procedure and rules. The workload is the representa-
tive set of cases that contribute to the evaluation. The measures used allow users
to calculate and compare results for each system to understand how they per-
form against the target. The procedure outlines how the benchmark should be
performed according to the defined rules. When following a measurement-based
approach, the workload is the most influential component and focused on next.
The workload used by a benchmark should ensure a group of five properties
that directly affect its representativeness, usefulness, and potential impact [Vieira
et al., 2012; Nunes et al., 2018]. The workload applied should be representative
of the real-world use case of the systems or components being benchmarked. It
should be composed of a representative set of interactions that the system faces in
43

Chapter 2
its operational environment. The workload should be comprehensive in the com-
ponents or features of the system that are exercised, according to the target do-
main. It should realistically cover different scenarios of the system’s operation.
The workload should be a true representation of the systems being benchmarked.
Three criteria are essential: coverage, to ensure a wide range of tests and applica-
tions; relevance, the significance of the workload in the area of the benchmark; and
ground truth, so that it is possible to compare the results of the evaluation with
what was expected. The workload should also be configurable to allow its users to
adapt it to their application scenarios and objectives. The workload should also
be scalable, allowing to have different levels of complexity in the evaluation, to
better represent possible intensity use cases for the systems.
Performance Benchmarking
Performance benchmarking in software systems is the process of evaluating and
comparing the performance of different hardware and software configurations by
running standardized tests [Gray, 1993]. These benchmarks are designed to mea-
sure various aspects of system performance, such as CPU utilization, memory
usage, or read/write speeds [Hennessy and Patterson, 2017]. By using consistent
and repeatable tests, benchmarks provide a quantifiable metric that allows users
to evaluate the performance of a system and compare it to alternatives or dif-
ferent configurations. This information is critical for making informed decisions
and identifying potential bottlenecks in the system architecture.
In a typical scenario, a performance benchmark uses workloads that represent ex-
pected system operations. For example, for transactional systems, the user would
define a workload of varying intensity and several common transactions that the
system is expected to execute at runtime to collect and compare measurements.
Dependability Benchmarking
Dependability benchmarking in computer systems involves evaluating and com-
paring the reliability, availability, and overall robustness of different systems or
configurations under various conditions [Avizienis et al., 2004]. This form of
benchmarking goes beyond performance metrics, focusing instead on how well
a system can maintain its functionality in the face of hardware failures, soft-
ware bugs, or other adverse conditions. Dependability benchmarks often in-
clude tests for fault tolerance, error recovery, and system resilience, ensuring
that systems can handle unexpected disruptions with minimal impact on their
operations [Gray, 1986; Avizienis et al., 2004]. These benchmarks are critical in
high-criticality environments, such as data centers or financial institutions, where
downtime or data loss can have a significant impact.
Dependability benchmarking typically involves introducing controlled faults
into the system to observe how it responds [Kanoun and Spainhower, 2008].
This can include simulating hardware failures, injecting software faults, or
overloading the system with excessive workloads. By analyzing the system’s
behavior under these conditions, reliability benchmarks provide valuable insight
into the strengths and weaknesses of a system’s design. These benchmarks
also help identify areas for improvement, with the results of dependability
44

Background and Related Work
benchmarking being essential for engineers and decision makers who need to
ensure that their systems can deliver consistent service. Various research works
have proposed benchmarks for transactional system dependability, such as
DBench-OLTP [Vieira and Madeira, 2003], which evaluates the performance and
key dependability characteristics of OLTP systems.
Security Tools Benchmarking
Benchmarking can also be applied to security tools to assess and compare the
effectiveness of different security tools [Antunes and Vieira, 2015a; Nunes et al.,
2018; Martinez et al., 2020]. The tests performed in this process simulate various
threats in a representative manner to evaluate the effectiveness of the tools in
detecting and reporting the attacks. In this way, organizations can identify the
most appropriate security tools to ensure that they are deploying solutions that
provide the highest level of security without compromising system performance.
This benchmarking process can be applied to a wide range of security tools, in-
cluding intrusion detection/prevention systems, antivirus or malware detection
tools, and static analysis tools [Nunes et al., 2018; Fallah and Bidgoly, 2019]. The
evaluation of security tools is typically based on True Positives (TPs) and False
Positives (FPs), which are derived by comparing the ground truth with the output
of the tools [Martinez et al., 2020]. Metrics such as precision, recall, and F-measure
are calculated from these values and used to rank the different tools. Organiza-
tions can make informed decisions about which security solutions provide the
best guarantees by comparing these metrics across tools.
Security benchmarks that have addressed the evaluation and comparison of static
analysis tools [Nunes et al., 2018] or vulnerability detection tools [Antunes and
Vieira, 2015a]. But also resilience and trustworthiness benchmarks that address
self-adaptive software systems [Almeida and Vieira, 2011].
Intrusion Detection Systems Benchmarking
To provide an accurate benchmark for intrusion detection, the workload should
closely represent the real-world scenarios that IDSs are expected to encounter
during operation. To be comprehensive, it should include different types of at-
tacks and intrusions that test the different classifications provided by the detec-
tion models [Milenkoski et al., 2015a]. For ensuring broad coverage, the work-
load should consist of event data from at least two different testbed applications
of different types, covering their full feature sets when exercised. The attacks
and applications used should reflect current technologies and attackers to ensure
relevance. They should provide labeled data for ground truth comparison, be
partially used (e.g., a portion of the attacks) to facilitate configurability, and be
scalable with varying complexity based on the testbed applications.
Several works tackled benchmarking IDSs in the past, ranging from proposing
dataset with network-based data with simulated and real attacks against a real
network. Researchers attempted to propose a real-world dataset and a system-
atic way of generating datasets [Zuech et al., 2015]. Other work has focused on
benchmarking anomaly-based intrusion detection techniques to compare differ-
ent appropriate algorithms, parameters, and test criteria [Maseer et al., 2021].
45

Chapter 2
2.6.2 Workloads
To evaluate an IDS, it is necessary to use workloads, so that the system has in-
formation to process and thus produce results that can be analyzed, Figure 2.15
represents the structure of the definition of workloads. These workloads can
be either benign, consisting of normal, non-malicious interactions, or malicious,
consisting of the opposite of the former. In addition, these workloads can be
used separately or in combination, resulting in three types of workload content:
purely benign, consisting only of non-malicious activity; purely malicious, con-
sisting only of attacks; and mixed workloads, which contain both types of data,
malicious and non-malicious, and are typically used for realistic testing scenar-
ios [Milenkoski et al., 2015b].
Figure 2.15: IDS evaluation workload types. (from Milenkoski et al. [2015b])
In addition, workloads can also be categorized by their form, which can be either
executable form or trace form. While the executable form is usually used for live
testing of a IDS, thus evaluating events as they happen, thetrace form consists of
a live execution recording, thus allowing the experiment to be repeated multiple
times with the same data. In general, the trace form is more commonly used
due to the fact that the executable form requires an explicit victim environment,
which can be very expensive to create [Milenkoski et al., 2015b].
There are two methods to generate purely benign executable workloads, using
workload drivers or generating them manually. Regarding workload drivers ,
these are typically used to generate pure benign activities, mainly aimed at test-
ing the system with different types of interactions, however, these drivers do
not correctly reproduce the normal interactions with a system [Milenkoski et al.,
2015b]. An example of such workload drivers is themysqlslap15 tool, which allows
you to make queries to a MySQL database and allows the user to select various
configurations, as the number of queries and clients interacting with the system.
Regarding manual generation, which could be an acceptable alternative to work-
15mysqlslap is a program that emulates client load for the MySQL server. It operates as multi-
ple clients would, when accessing the server (dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/mysqlslap.html).
46

Background and Related Work
load drivers, it consists in the execution of real tasks by real systems and/or
users. This method allows to overcome the problem of workload drivers that
do not generate realistic activities, since manual generation, if based on a realistic
activity model, generates data similar to what a system would do in normal op-
eration [Milenkoski et al., 2015b]. However, with this approach it is not possible
to customize the workload in terms of activity intensity, for example.
Changing the focus to purely malicious workloads, there are also two approaches
that can be used to produce them, those are exploit databases and vulnerability
and attack injection [Milenkoski et al., 2015b]. For exploit databases, they can
be considered as penetration testing tools, such asMetasploit16 or Nessus17, which
have a database for themselves that allows exploiting known vulnerabilities of
certain systems. There are also several exploit repositories available online that
can be used to collect working proof-of-concept code that successfully exploits a
known vulnerability.
To model the attackers, one can leverage different frameworks such as MITRE
ATT&CK [Strom et al., 2018] or STIDE [Howard and Lipner, 2006]. MITRE
ATT&CK is a knowledge-based framework that incorporates adversary tactics
and techniques based on real-world observations. It is commonly used for threat
modeling in enterprise, mobile, and industrial control system environments. The
framework is extensive and provides details about 14 tactics, 201 techniques,
and 424 sub-techniques ( attack.mitre.org) that are commonly used by malicious
actors in an enterprise setting. It is a useful tool for modeling the attacker and
defining attack vectors that can be exploited. The selection of threats to represent
malicious behavior can be derived from a risk-based assessment to represent the
most relevant and impactful attacks.
The STRIDE method is a threat modeling approach that allows users to reason
and find threats that system may be facing. It was proposed by Microsoft and
its name is an acronym for six categories of security threats: i) Spoofing: posing
as an authorized user, process, or system component; ii) Tampering: altering or
changing authentic information; iii) Repudiation: rejecting or denying an action
carried out within the system; iv) Information disclosure: unauthorized access to
information; v) Denial-of-Service (DoS): interrupting the normal flow of services
to authorized users; and vi) Elevation of privilege: an unprivileged user gains
privileged access to compromise the system.
Focusing on vulnerability and attack injection, a technique based on fault injec-
tion, it provides the ability to exploit vulnerabilities that have been added to a
particular system. This method is often used when it is not possible to use pub-
licly available exploits, either because they do not exist or because the vulnerable
version of the system being monitored is not available.
Finally, regarding the generation of trace forms, there are two methods that al-
low to generate any of the three types of workloads (pure benign, pure mali-
cious and mixed), these are trace acquisition and trace generation. Regarding
16Metasploit is a commonly used penetration testing framework (metasploit.com).
17Nessus is a vulnerability assessment solution commonly applied to audit and assess the se-
curity of computer systems and networks (tenable.com/products/nessus).
47

Chapter 2
trace acquisition, there are two ways to achieve this. The first is to collect traces
from real systems when they are in production at organizations, but companies
are usually reluctant to share real data from their systems due to privacy con-
cerns [Milenkoski et al., 2015b]. Second, there are publicly available traces that
can be used to train and test the IDSs, but these traces tend to be considered out-
dated due to their long existence [McHugh, 2000]. In the context of trace genera-
tion, there are also two main ways to use this technique, namely generating traces
in a testbed environment and using honeypots to record interactions with it. Focus-
ing on the use of atestbed environment, these test systems can be used as a target
for the attacks performed to collect traces using the techniques described previ-
ously, such as the use of workload drivers and the manual generation method.
However, this technique may generate traces that are not representative of real-
istic interactions with services, since results collected in small environments are
not usually applicable to larger ones [Sommer and Paxson, 2010]. As for honey-
pots that intend to simulate the operation of real systems, it is possible to collect
the interaction of attackers interacting with them, providing the traces needed
to evaluate IDSs. Since the actors involved are unaware that they are interact-
ing with a fake system, this approach allows for the generation of realistic traces,
although it is more difficult to classify the traces generated, e.g., attack types.
2.6.3 Metrics
To understand whether a IDS is performing according to what it is supposed to
do, it is necessary to compute meaningful metric values. These metrics allow
researchers to understand, from different perspectives, the results obtained, as
they can be related to performance and security. Here, we outline and explain the
metrics commonly used when performing a IDS evaluation procedure.
Regarding performance metrics, which are not the focus of this work, it refers to
the non-functional properties such as capacity and resource consumption [Meng
and Li, 2012]. The focus of this work is on security metrics, which are based on
the results produced by a IDS and the ground truth knowledge of the workload
being used. Although not all IDSs output results are in binary form, either nor-
mal or anomalous, those that produce some other output, such as a likelihood or
multi-class classification (other than anomalous or normal), are easily converted
to this output form. The basis for IDSs evaluation is therefore the comparison of
the expected results with the obtained results, which give four possible combina-
tions, as shown in the confusion matrix in Table 2.3.
The classifications produced by a IDS are categorized into one of the four cate-
gories shown in table 2.3. If the baseline classification of the event is normal, i.e.,
Table 2.3: Confusion matrix of IDSs results categorization.
Expected Obtained
Normal Malicious
Normal True Negative (TN) False Positive (FP)
Malicious False Negative (FN) True Positive (TP)
48

Background and Related Work
not malicious, and the IDS considers it normal, then we get a true negative cat-
egory because the IDS correctly classified the event and it was not an intrusion
attempt, but if the IDS had categorized it as malicious, then it would instead be
categorized as false positive due to misclassification of a non-malicious event.
On the other hand, if the baseline classification of an event is malicious, then the
result produced by the IDS will be considered a false negative if the IDS does
not raise an alert, and a true positive if it does. Therefore, based on these four
categories, it is possible to define several metrics [Antunes and Vieira, 2015b].
The proportion of malicious cases that are classified as malicious denotes the met-
ric Recall, also known as the true positive rate or sensitivity. Although this met-
ric focuses only on detected anomalies, it provides a good understanding of IDSs
performance in terms of detecting the true anomalies.
recall = TP
