Precision is the ratio of correctly classified malicious events to all events classified
as malicious by the IDS. However, this metric, like recall, is susceptible to bias
from prevalence and skew [David Martin Powers, 2011].
precision = TP
TP + FP (2.2)
F-Measure, or F1Score, is based on precision and recall, being the harmonic mean
of both metrics. Although this metric assigns the same cost to true positives and
false positives, it provides a good understanding of which IDS detects a high
number of anomalies while reporting a low number of false positives [Antunes
and Vieira, 2015b].
F-Measure = 2× recall× precision
recall + precision = 2× TP
2× TP + FN + FP (2.3)
The False Positive Rate, or miss rate, is the proportion of normal events that are
classified as anomalous [Antunes and Vieira, 2015b].
FPR = FP
N = FP

Furthermore, another commonly used representation for IDS selection is a Re-
ceiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curve, which plots the true positive rate
(recall) against the false positive rate of an IDS with different configurations, so
that it is possible to compare them and identify the one that fits best. An example
of a ROC curve plot is available at Figure 2.16.
However, some argue that ROC curves do not take into account all meaningful
and relevant data to assess the performance of an IDS [Gaffney and Ulvila, 2001].
Consequently, as a manner to overcome such limitations, the expected cost anal-
ysis was proposed in 2001 [Gaffney and Ulvila, 2001]. This approach takes into
account not only the ROC but also on cost metrics and the hostility of the operat-
ing environment through the probability of intrusion [Gaffney and Ulvila, 2001].
Gaffney and Ulvila [2001] define the expected cost based on the probability of
intrusion, thus taking into account the hostility of the environment, the false pos-
itive rate and recall values, and the proportion of cost associated with responding
to a non-intrusion versus not responding to an intrusion.
Thus, the expected cost can be calculated through the following equation:
ExpectedCost = Min{Cβp, (1− α)(1− p)} + Min{C(1− β)p, α(1− p)} (2.7)
Having: C =
Cβ
Cα, α = FPR, (1− β) = Recall , p = P(I) = TP+FN
TP+FN +TN +FP
For an example where C = 10, from previous work [Gaffney and Ulvila, 2001],
it is ten times more costly to fail to respond to an intrusion than to respond to a
non-intrusion.
2.6.4 Intrusion Detection Datasets
Benchmarking an IDS or approach requires a comprehensive dataset that takes
into account different scenarios and consists of benign and malicious data. Vari-
ous datasets have been generated and published for evaluation purposes, allow-
ing operators to perform impartial evaluation and comparison of different tech-
niques. Their characteristics differ, making each proposed dataset advantageous

for a given purpose. Table 2.4 provides an overview of the most relevant publicly
available datasets. The table is not an exhaustive or complete analysis of avail-
able datasets, but rather focuses on the most recent and relevant datasets that
have been more widely adopted by the research community. The datasets were
collected from several surveys on the topic and characterized according to differ-
ent criteria. Some of these datasets have been available and used for more than
a decade and may no longer be representative of current challenges [Tavallaee
et al., 2009; CAIDA, 2007; UNM, 1999; MIT Lincoln Laboratory, 1998].
The analysis presented focuses on the representativeness of the environment and
workloads used on the monitored system. Representativeness is a very important
property that has a huge impact on the applicability of the evaluated techniques
in real-world scenarios and on the acceptance of a benchmark. A technique may
perform well according to a given dataset used during the evaluation, but if that
dataset is not representative of a real use case, the results are meaningless, or at
least not of much use. Table 2.4 also indicates whether the datasets have labeled
data, which is useful for validating the results obtained during evaluations. It also
indicates whether the dataset is outdated, describes the types of data available,
and includes references to the papers in which the datasets were published.
The majority of publicly available datasets focus on network environments, typ-
ically providing pcap files containing traffic data across multiple hosts [Tavallaee
et al., 2009; Shiravi et al., 2012]. Only a small fraction of the datasets consist of
host information (e.g., audit logs, file system data, system calls, etc.) [Creech and
Hu, 2014; Grimmer et al., 2019]. This is a significant drawback for researchers
attempting to propose techniques for host-based intrusion detection.
The challenge is compounded for those attempting to address security through
intrusion detection in emerging environments, such as microservice-based sys-
tems. Of the datasets analyzed, only two focus on container-based deployments
(the common case for microservices) [Grimmer et al., 2019; El Khairi et al., 2022].
The LID-DS dataset was proposed in 2019, with some updates in 2021, and col-
lects information at the container level used by an application [Grimmer et al.,
2019]. However, it has some limitations; for example, it lacks a representative
testbed with users to collect benign and attack data, and the dataset consists pri-
marily of vulnerable deployments from vulhub [Hub, 2021] without a realistic
51

Chapter 2
use case. While useful for initial testing, a scenario that ensures effectiveness in
a real microservice deployment is still needed, as the traces are also generally
short, about 45 seconds, with a total recording time of about four days [El Khairi
et al., 2022]. The CB-DS dataset focuses on container breakout scenarios in a con-
tainerized application with ten attack scenarios with known vulnerabilities and
misconfigurations, resulting in 23 hours of recording [El Khairi et al., 2022].
Other work has been done in the area of intrusion detection for microser-
vices [Cavalcanti et al., 2021; O˘ gur, 2022]. Some works perform experiments
with a single containerized application, a simple scenario for microservice
applications, but do not publish the datasets used [Flora et al., 2020; Flora and
Antunes, 2019; Cavalcanti et al., 2021]. Similar LID-DS work takes an approach
that does not focus on microservice applications, but rather on collecting
information from Docker images containing known vulnerabilities [O˘ gur, 2022].
2.7 Conclusion
The body of knowledge about service-oriented applications, and distributed sys-
tems in general, is immense. The tools and techniques for ensuring their relia-
bility and security are constantly evolving, but still need to be improved to ad-
dress many of the challenges posed by microservice applications. The constantly
changing, highly dynamic, and heterogeneous environments that characterize
these systems present difficulties for security mechanisms that must be addressed
through research and development.
This chapter gave the background information and introduced the fundamen-
tal ideas needed to comprehend the challenges and unresolved issues this thesis
addresses, in addition to its scope. The dependability and security of microser-
vices and containers, as well as the concepts used to guarantee and assess their
security, were covered along with the relevant existing literature. It examined the
state-of-the-art in several contributions that can be used as a starting point to im-
prove the methods and techniques used to evaluate and improve the security of
microservice-based applications.
Static detection techniques are not applicable in such a dynamic environment
where changes are common and can easily render anomaly-based detection in-
effective as modifications are incorporated. There is a need for improved ap-
proaches that account for the dynamic nature of deployments and behaviors.
Evaluation efforts that properly represent a representative environment and use
updated microservice testbeds and technologies would facilitate the develop-
ment of such techniques, as evaluation campaigns would shed light on current
limitations. However, as the state of the art review shows, the available datasets
are not prepared for use in these campaigns.
All of these limitations and challenges are detailed in Chapter 3, which focuses
on the security threats faced by microservice applications and serves to inform us
about the key challenges that require solutions to increase the level of security in
dynamic and complex microservice environments.
52

CHAPTER 3
Microservices Threats and Research Challenges
The adoption of microservice architectures is increasing due to the use of de-
velopment pipelines that provide automation for testing and deployment. Still,
studies show friction due to security challenges that these scalable, elastic, and
dynamic systems create for security mechanisms. Following the review of the
state-of-the-art regarding microservices security, we identify the threats to which
a microservice application is exposed and the main vectors of attack a malicious
agent can opt to take in order to compromise these applications. Clarifying the
threats aids in the prioritization of security measures to apply and the definition
of the most appropriate ones. It also provides a target for improvements which
allows us to better understand the challenges faced.
In this chapter, we detail the microservices threat model considered in this thesis
work that these systems face during deployment. We also present the key open
research challenges that constitute the unsolved problem of practical intrusion
detection in microservices and the main approach to address them in our work.
These challenges arise from the operational environments of microservice appli-
cations and the current research gaps in microservice security.
This chapter covers the microservices threat model (Section 3.1), the key chal-
lenges in intrusion detection for microservices (Section 3.2), the approach fol-
lowed to address them (Section 3.3), and closes with a conclusion (Section 3.4).
3.1 Microservices Threat Model
Microservice environments face several security challenges that are compounded
when combined with container-based environments. In addition to the typical
concerns, multi-tenancy is currently the biggest challenge. While this is not a
53

Chapter 3
new problem - multi-tenancy was already a known issue with Virtual Machines
(VMs) - its use in container-based environments is more concerning. Containers
differ from VMs in their isolation of resources. While VMs had an additional
layer called a hypervisor and a more complete set of resources that were virtu-
alized, such as hardware and the full Operating System (OS) layer, in the case
of containers, the separation layer is thinner and mainly based on features of the
Linux kernel, such as cgroups and namespaces, thus providing a lower level of
security and isolation guarantees compared to VMs.
Therefore, the primary concern with containers is to ensure that entities that be-
nignly share the same infrastructure are not compromised by malicious opera-
tions by attackers. It is assumed that containers with malicious intentions will
initially acquire resources in a legitimate and non-abusive manner. That is, only
after gaining access to a container on the infrastructure does its owner use its
resources to perform harmful activities, such as attempting to compromise the
execution of other tenants or even the normal execution of the host machine. The
fact that cloud computing services assume that their consumers are trustworthy
allows an attacker to allocate and use resources just like any other customer.
As a result, our concerns are not only about remote malicious agents, but also
about the threats posed by these legitimately allocated resources, which are ini-
tially considered trustworthy despite the possibility of having malicious intent
toward other tenants or the cloud service infrastructure. This poses a major threat
to the underlying layers of the cloud service stack and to the normal execution of
other legitimate containers.
Figure 3.1 represents the threat model to be addressed during this work and is
described below. As can be observed, we are particularly concerned with mali-
cious microservice containers that share infrastructure (multi-tenancy) with non-
malicious ones and perform three different types of malicious activities.
Malicious
Microservice
Benign
Microservice
Virtualization Engine
Host Operating System
Attack
Attack
Attack
Figure 3.1: Threat Model considered for microservice applications.
In general, each attack aims to compromise the infrastructure or its components
and gain some type of reward, such as more resources or information that the
agent should not have access to. Other threat models have been defined in a
similar way [Sultan et al., 2019], but this can be adapted to include additional in-
formation, so four attack vectors can be defined according to the threat landscape
presented:
• the malicious microservice attacks other microservice (Section 3.1.1)
• a malicious microservice targets the virtualization engine (Section 3.1.2)
54

Microservices Threats and Research Challenges
• the attacker microservice attacks the host (Section 3.1.3)
• the host leverages its privileges to target a microservice (Section 3.1.4)
3.1.1 Microservice → Microservice
We assume that a maliciously infected microservice targets another microservice
in the infrastructure with attacks. The malicious microservice can originate from
either: i) a malicious actor legitimately obtaining resources within the infrastruc-
ture and using them to perform malicious operations; or ii) gaining access to a
benign microservice in the infrastructure through remote over-the-network at-
tacks that give the attacker access to the application.
The malicious actor can then use the privileged or unprivileged access to the in-
frastructure to gain information, explore vulnerabilities, or compromise the ex-
ecution of another tenant. In this scenario, an attacker could explore the char-
acteristics of microservice applications to perform multi-target attacks (see Sec-
tion 3.2.3), or simply move laterally to a more rewarding microservice, either in
terms of privilege access level or the criticality of the information stored there.
It is common and understandable for developers and security professionals to
devote more attention and resources to more critical services, so the gateway to
an attacker may come from a less privileged and protected service that can still
be used to further compromise the application’s infrastructure.
3.1.2 Microservice → Virtualization Engine
An attacker with access to the infrastructure on which the target microservice
application is deployed can attempt to compromise it from multiple fronts. To
gain unauthorized access to the microservices’ data, they can try to compromise
the virtualization engine if the microservice interface is not vulnerable or easily
accessible.
Microservices typically run on containers that are managed by orchestration mid-
dleware, commonly referred to as a container engine. This also acts as an attack
vector to reach the OS layer, so this software component can also be compro-
mised in cloud deployments by rogue containers, assuming the role of a path-
way to achieve both attacks, to the host machine and to other tenants. Thus, the
container of a malicious microservice would compromise the container engine to
extend its damage.
As a result, attacks on the engine are of great concern because of the damage that
can result from its compromise. Not only can an attacker access the container
data and runtime of the target microservice, but also all containers running in
that virtualization engine. This attack requires a high level of expertise, but given
the complexity of the virtualization components, it is possible that motivated at-
tackers will target this layer of the infrastructure.
55

Chapter 3
3.1.3 Microservice → Host
Similar to the previous threats, an attacker seeking to increase the level of access
gained may attempt to leverage his privileges by performing a container breakout
attack1. A container breakout attack occurs when a malicious agent is able to
break the isolation mechanisms that characterize a container [Jian and Chen, 2017;
VS et al., 2023]. A successful attack of this type allows the attacker to escalate
privileges and gain access to the host on which the infrastructure is running.
An attacker’s malicious container may attempt to exploit vulnerabilities in the
cloud stack to gain access or control of privileged resources. Typically, these at-
tacks are motivated by a desire to gather information or control how resources
are allocated.
3.1.4 Host → Microservice
Threats to a microservice can also come from inside the infrastructure. In this
scenario, the malicious agent is the host running the application. In such cases,
the host attempts to compromise the normal execution of a container running on
its infrastructure by a malicious insider who has access to the supporting infras-
tructure, or by a malicious agent who has gained unauthorized access.
Although this threat is often not considered because there are contracts between
the various parties involved to ensure trust between them. There may be envi-
ronments, often the edge, where the host is not in the trusted computing base.
Some hosts already provide a trusted computing module to increase their trust-
worthiness to other parties, such as Intel Software Guard Extensions (SGX) or
ARM Trustzone. A service that does not require instrumentation by the deployed
application can also help increase trustworthiness (e.g., intrusion detection).
3.2 Challenges in Intrusion Detection for Microser-
vices
In dynamic environments, adopting a microservices-based approach actively of-
fers flexibility, agility and scalability. By actively leveraging it, organizations can
quickly respond to market changes and resolve issues without a complete system
shutdown. However, rapid and continuous software delivery increases the risk
of security vulnerabilities, such as exposed APIs and untested integrations [Sul-
tan et al., 2019].
Figure 3.2 shows an example of a microservice-based application architecture,
other architecture diagrams are presented for the testbeds analyzed in Sec-
tion 2.2.3. This example application, a mock e-commerce store, is composed
of five services: a front-end, a shipping, a purchase, a payment, and a review

Figure 3.2: Example microservice-based application high-level architecture.
service, each taking care of one business area. For the presented architecture, the
load balancer distributes user requests among three replicas of the front-end ser-
vice. These services scale independently, as indicated by the different number of
replicas for each service. The front-end service communicates with all the other
services, gathering information to respond to user requests. The review service
only communicates with the purchase service, while purchase communicates
with all three other services.
Microservices are commonly deployed in multi-tenant environments, where var-
ious containers with different owners co-exist on the same machine [Ali et al.,
2015; Flora and Antunes, 2019]. Software containers have thinner isolation be-
tween them than virtual machines, because they use kernel-level mechanisms to
ensure resource and data separation, rather than creating an emulation of the en-
tire operating system and hardware [Gu et al., 2023]. Thus, intrusion detection
methods for containers must be tailored to these characteristics to address them.
Balancing the benefits of microservices with security measures allows organiza-
tions to effectively leverage dynamic environments without compromising the
intended service. Intrusion detection should be used at application runtime to
enhance system security, but there are challenges to ensure effectiveness.
Figure 3.3 depicts different challenges identified for microservice environments
where intrusion detection is applied as a security measure. It represents the dif-
ficulties of ensuring the use of the most effective detection models, the hardships
of having to deal with scalable and continuously changing deployments, either
by automated scalability or continuous delivery of modifications, and the possi-
bility of attackers targeting several microservices during an attack that explores
the inherent microservice trust and larger attack surface.
3.2.1 Scalability and Elasticity
Microservices leverage scalability to handle increasing workloads by using ad-
ditional resources. This action is even commonly automated using auto-scaling
processes that monitor microservices and decide when to apply modifications.
An example, often used, is Kubernetes Horizontal Pod Autoscaling (HPA).

Figure 3.3: Challenges in Intrusion Detection for Microservices.
In microservice architectures, deployment changes frequently. Service replicas are
created and removed based on user demand, and with the constant addition of new
features and updates to the service, different versions can be deployed quickly. During
the detection phase, behavior-based detection models will face these challenges and
need to adapt to ensure effective protection of the system. They either need to be
retrained, which requires efficiency so as not to delay the release of changes, or they
will need to be able to accommodate a certain level of change from the beginning.
Detection models need to be more flexible and adaptable, with ability to generalize. In
addition, one needs approaches to select the best detection model. Also, techniques on
how to deal with attacks targeting multiple services across the application.
This means that the application will have a different number of active replicas
for a given service at different times, and the detection models need to account
for this. Current techniques are designed for static deployments and do not han-
dle changes well. The scenario becomes even more challenging when elasticity is
considered. The continuous, on-demand adaptation of microservice applications
to properly respond to user workloads and keep operational costs low exacer-
bates the challenge for detection models to cope with continuous changes.
This dynamic nature requires intrusion detection approaches to adapt to scalable
and elastic services and handle multiple operational profiles. It is necessary to ac-
count for the behavior variations that result from different deployment scenarios.
Figure 3.3 illustrates an example of this challenge, where the detection models
need to overcome the modifications in active service replicas. We can observe the
replicas of the service being reduced (scale-down) and increased (scale-up).
3.2.2 Fast Delivery and Continuous Modification of Microser-
vices
The adoption of Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery (CI/CD)
pipelines with automation mechanisms has exacerbated some security concerns,
while also contributing greatly to the popularity and adoption of microservices-
based systems [Soldani et al., 2018]. Combined with containers, services can
be lightweight and easily take advantage of cloud computing benefits such as
58

Microservices Threats and Research Challenges
rapid deployment without significant overhead [Sultan et al., 2019], allowing the
system to quickly adapt to the changes in the business environment.
However, as services are continuously modified, it becomes more difficult to
maintain the security of the environment, especially with behavior monitoring.
Detection models learned during previous service releases may no longer be con-
sistent or effective. The differences introduced can vary in size and impact de-
pending on the relevance of the service or its stage of development.
Any modification to a microservice will affect the existing detection models. Up-
dates to the technology stack (e.g., update the web server), addition of new fea-
tures (e.g., add new endpoints), or refactoring of the service operation will af-
fect the learned detection model and may compromise its effectiveness if it is
not prepared to cope with such changes. Traditional techniques such as static
analysis help identify vulnerabilities and prevent them from being introduced
into production services, but there is no approach to understand the impact of
development changes on existing behavior-based detection models. Thus, tech-
niques must be developed to ensure that the knowledge gained during the train-
ing phase for a particular service version is maintained for its various iterations.
In Figure 3.3, we provide an example of continuous change. In the lower right
corner, we see that a service has multiple versions released over time (e.g., v1.0,
v1.1). These releases have different behaviors due to changes introduced by de-
velopers, either as requirements change or as new ones need to be satisfied.
3.2.3 Multi-target Attacks and Pinpoint Intrusion Entry Point
Microservice architectures take advantage of their distributed and loosely cou-
pled nature to work together to achieve a common goal. This is possible largely
because microservices operate on implicit trust among each other [Newman,
2015; Dragoni et al., 2017]. The interconnectivity of microservices within a
system allows for the segmentation of data and business functions, which means
that the authentication and authorization mechanism is extremely important.
Common approaches to validating authorization and performing authentication
can be done using tokens or frameworks such as OAuth 2.0 or OpenID Connect.
However, an attacker who is able to compromise a service can use it to gain access
to the entire infrastructure or simply manipulate the actions of other components
by exchanging messages that appear trustworthy. By gaining access to a service
in the system, malicious agents can use it as a stepping stone to infiltrate other
services that share the same multi-tenant infrastructure [Dragoni et al., 2017].
It is critical that intrusion detection approaches not only be prepared to detect
these multi-target attacks, but also be able to identify the point of entry once an
intrusion is detected in a microservice environment. There are several scenarios
where this is an issue.
For example, identifying the affected service without specifying the instance may
slow response time or lead to unnecessarily broad actions. In other cases, the
Intrusion Detection System (IDS) may report an intrusion on a service that was
59

Chapter 3
not the first to be compromised. In such cases, a multi-target attack may evade
detection in the initial service(s) and only be detected in later stages, requiring
analysis of microservice chains to locate the entry point. Another situation that
can occur is when an intrusion triggers a cascading effect on neighboring services,
and the IDS reports the impact of the intrusion on other services, ignoring the
initial service through which the attacker entered the system.
Thus, to effectively respond to an intrusion, it is imperative to pinpoint the com-
promised services, even if concerns arise at different points in the system, by lo-
calizing the entry point of the security breach. However, accomplishing this task
is not easy. The complex interactions between different services, the number of
replicas in operation, and the geographic distribution of the deployment must be
taken into account. As a result, more sophisticated algorithms or mechanisms, as
well as additional contextual information, are required to make the connections
between different services and correlate them with IDS reports.
In Figure 3.3, two services are under attack. In this example, an IDS could only
identify that the service with four active replicas was attacked, and either: i) fail
to specify the replica where the attack occurred, or ii) fail to identify the attack on
the database service.
3.2.4 Proactive Maintenance of Services
The continuous execution of a software system tends to lead to higher probabil-
ity of software failure, this is generally accelerated by software aging [Parnas,
1994; Avizienis et al., 2004]. Software aging is characterized by the accumulation
of resources consumed by production systems containing software faults [Par-
nas, 1994; Grottke et al., 2008]. Microservice applications are not exempt from
this problem [Yue et al., 2020; Flora et al., 2022]. There are several approaches
to recovering a system from software aging, but the most common is to restart
the application with the goal of restoring it to a clean state; this process is called
rejuvenation [Parnas, 1994].
Several works have approached this problem from the perspective of trying to
predict the time to aging failure and apply a rejuvenation measure in that time
span [Cotroneo et al., 2014; Pietrantuono and Russo, 2020]. Different methods
can be used to estimate the time to aging failure, primarily through aging indica-
tors such as resource usage or reliability attributes [Cotroneo et al., 2014; Pietran-
tuono and Russo, 2020]. These indicators could be leveraged and extended to
consider security metrics, producing a wider range of attributes that would al-
low microservice orchestrators to proactively apply measures to increase the se-
curity level of the application. Leveraging microservices containers to introduce
low-cost rejuvenation measures would allow for reduced availability impacts.
The continuous execution of microservices increases the likelihood that existing
faults, either malicious or non-malicious, will be activated and cause errors in
the state of operation that could be exploited by malicious actors. Instead of re-
acting to these errors, you can prevent them by monitoring the microservice and
providing rejuvenation when necessary.
60

Microservices Threats and Research Challenges
3.2.5 Evaluation of Intrusion Detection Techniques for Mi-
croservices
Developers and security practitioners select the measures to implement for mi-
croservice security according to their effectiveness. The utilization of intrusion
detection is dependent on the ability of the approach to detect malicious activity
on the infrastructure.
The research, development, and improvement of intrusion detection techniques
requires the comparison of novel approaches with the state of the art. To make
this comparison, researchers need access to updated benchmarks and datasets
that consider the current challenges of intrusion detection mechanisms, as repre-
sentativeness is crucial to demonstrating the improvements of new techniques.
Microservice applications bring new challenges and the available datasets do not
yet reflect them [Mishra et al., 2017; Vasilomanolakis et al., 2015; Liu et al., 2019].
In addition to the current datasets being quite old and outdated for the current
trends [Mishra et al., 2017; Alkasassbeh and Al-Haj Baddar, 2023], microservices
also introduce the distributed, scalable, and very dynamic environments pow-
ered by containers and typically managed by automated orchestrators that apply
auto-scaling (e.g., HPA) or recovery operations (e.g., Probes2).
In this regard, there is a gap for research and development of a benchmark that
takes into account the challenges of microservice applications in a representative
way. Create and publish a dataset that covers the current scenarios of a realistic
microservice-based application deployment, with autonomous service orchestra-
tion and scaling, and current containerization and deployment technologies.
In Figure 3.3, the method of selection of the algorithms/detection models used
to increase the security level of the microservice application is unclear. Currently,
the dataset and/or benchmarks available do not yet consider the challenges faced
in a microservice deployment.
3.3 Approach to Address the Key Challenges
We devised an approach that addresses the key challenges for the security of mi-
croservice applications. This approach covers the initial studies of the state of mi-
croservices dependability and security and then contributes with techniques and
metrics that allow us to evaluate and improve the effectiveness of intrusion de-
tection approaches for microservices. The devised techniques and metrics are in-
tegrated into a framework that delivers practical, continuous, and adaptive intru-
sion detection to microservices that use CI/CD pipelines, which is implemented
into a tool. Figure 3.4 provides an overview of all the steps of the approach and
maps them with the corresponding chapter number in this document. The phases
that compose the approach are detailed below.
2Kubernetes Probes are a monitoring and adaptive feature of the Kubernetes orchestrator, for
details see Section 2.3.3.

Our approach to tackle the identified challenges started with two main studies
that allowed us to establish the state of the security and dependability of mi-
croservice environments. They also allowed us to understand the applicability
and effectiveness of intrusion detection as a potential security measure to be ap-
plied in microservice environments.
First, we determined the state of security and fault tolerance capabilities designed
and currently deployed in microservice environments, We conducted a study to
determine the state of dependability and fault tolerance provided by Kubernetes
Probes when applied to microservice applications using the Kubernetes orches-
trator. This study also covered whether microservices suffer from aging effects,
which tend to increase the likelihood that a system will fail over time.
We proposed an evaluation methodology for intrusion detection in container-
based systems, which is the most common case for microservices. The study
showed the effectiveness of detection models built with the same configuration
across different deployment methods and allowed us to validate the promising
applicability of the security measure to microservice applications.
The results of the intrusion detection effectiveness study allowed us to identify
the limitations of the measure. Based on these limitations and the key challenges
identified in Section 3.2, we tackled the scalability and elasticity characteristics
(see Section 3.2.1) by proposing a set of techniques that allow detection models to
achieve higher detection effectiveness by generalizing the knowledge acquired
during the training phase and applied it to different operational deployments.
Furthermore, to address the continuous modification challenge (see Section 3.2.2),
we designed a group of approaches to capture the core behavior of microservices
and increase the reuse of detection models. To allow developers and researchers
to understand whether a detection model is more or less likely to be reused, we
proposed a metric (Model Reusability Index (MRI)) to measure this factor.
Evaluating intrusion detection approaches for microservice applications required
a tailored and standardized methodology (see Section 3.2.5). To address this chal-
lenge, we devised a representative and fair benchmarking approach that consid-
62

Microservices Threats and Research Challenges
ers the threat model of microservices and state-of-the-art technologies. To in-
crease the representativeness of the benchmark, we generated a dataset that con-
tains training, test, and validation data, which allows researchers to evaluate and
compare their approaches in an independent framework that follows the set of
rules that characterize a benchmarking process.
Finally, all the accumulated knowledge and experience from the experiments con-
ducted were integrated into a framework that delivers continuous and adaptive
intrusion detection for microservice applications. The µSherlock framework was
implemented into a tool that provides a constant level of security regardless of
changes and updates, by using the techniques and metrics developed to address
the identified challenges and increase the security of microservices.
3.4 Conclusion
In this section, we summarize the contributions and key takeaways from this
chapter. The definition of the microservices threat model allows us to have a
clear idea of the main actions a potential attacker can take against the system and
how to prioritize the security measures to be applied.
We also identified the key challenges that need to be addressed to increase the se-
curity level of microservice applications. Among these challenges, we identify the
shortcomings of intrusion detection techniques in dealing with the dynamic na-
ture of microservice environments, mainly due to the scalable and elastic behav-
ior the system has during runtime as a way to adapt to changes in user demand
and resource consumption. The CI/CD practices also contribute to a highly dy-
namic environment that is constantly changing and sees new changes to the ser-
vices put into production, which creates difficulties especially for behavior-based
intrusion detection techniques.
The challenges identified are related to the ability of attackers to launch attacks
that target multiple services in the infrastructure and to identify intrusion entry
points so that the most appropriate and effective remediation techniques can be
applied. Shortcomings were also identified with respect to proactively resolving
future microservice failures and the lack of approaches to evaluate intrusion de-
tection techniques that take into account the technical characteristics of microser-
vices.
Furthermore, we detail the approach taken to address the identified challenges
and map them to the chapters presented in this document. The approach es-
sentially consists of five sub-steps that comprise three main phases. We present
a security and dependability study of container and microservice deployments
to understand the state of fault tolerance and intrusion detection mechanisms,
which are detailed in Chapter 4 and Chapter 5, respectively. This is followed by
the design and proposal of novel components to evaluate and improve the effec-
tiveness of intrusion detection in microservice applications, due to its potential
practical use.
63

Chapter 3
In Chapter 6, we propose a set of techniques and metrics aimed at improving
the effectiveness of intrusion detection in scalable, elastic, and dynamic microser-
vice applications. To improve the evaluation of intrusion detection approaches,
Chapter 7 presents a benchmarking approach tailored for microservice applica-
tions, with a custom dataset and up-to-date technologies.
Chapter 8 leverages the accumulated knowledge and proposed components to
design the µSherlock framework. We implement a tool following this guideline to
ensure an automated and end-to-end, continuous and adaptive intrusion detec-
tion approach for dynamic, scalable and elastic microservice applications.
64

CHAPTER 4
A Study on the Fault Tolerance of Microservice Environments
Microservice-based applications are increasingly being adopted along with cloud
service models supported by container-based architectures and orchestrated by
platforms such as Kubernetes that monitor, manage, and automate most tasks.
While these tools provide failover capabilities, their effectiveness in dealing
with different types of faults has not been studied. Fault injection is an effective
method for validating components that are supposed to detect, report, and
correct faults.
The study presented in this chapter focuses on the effectiveness of Kubernetes
in dealing with faults and aging in microservices. It also covers the possibility
of using faults to accelerate aging effects for testing purposes. We conducted an
analysis of the implementation and tuning of Kubernetes Probes, followed by ex-
periments with varying loads and fault injections in two different and represen-
tative microservice testbeds to analyze the ability of Probes to detect problems in
microservices.
The goal is to improve researchers’ and developers’ knowledge of whether Ku-
bernetes can detect various faults and aging issues. Observations show that al-
though some services tend to accumulate aging effects with increasing resource
consumption, Kubernetes does not detect or act on them, suggesting that probes
may be insufficient for aging scenarios. The results also show that fault injection
is useful for accelerating aging effects for testing and evaluation purposes.
This chapter details the experimental environment (Section 4.1), Kubernetes
Probes with best practices and an analysis of the implementation (Section 4.2),
aging experiments on microservices (Section 4.3), Kubernetes fault tolerance
evaluation (Section 4.4), the threats to validity of the experiment (Section 4.5),
and closes with a conclusion (Section 4.6).
65

Chapter 4
4.1 Experimental Environment
Kubernetes supports various microservice applications, some of which are
mission-critical systems that require different levels of resiliency and perfor-
mance. It provides some features that can increase the resiliency of systems by
performing continuous monitoring and detecting faults as they occur. Probes
are an example of the available features 1. Their use is recommended, as they
perform continuous verification and act on the failed container to overcome
the problems. Nevertheless, there is little information about their detection
effectiveness in identifying service failures when aging affects the microservice.
The objectives of this study are two: i) evaluate the effectiveness of Kubernetes
probes in detecting faults and aging effects in microservices; and ii) investigate
the acceleration of aging effects in microservices through the use of software de-
fects.
In this section, we describe the context that is common to the experiments de-
tailed in Section 4.3 and Section 4.4. Section 4.2 details how the Kubernetes Probes
were prepared and applied during the experiments conducted, and provides best
practices for their implementation. The experiments were performed on two dif-
ferent representative microservice applications with different workloads, while
collecting metrics with multiple tools.
4.1.1 Representative Microservice Applications
We chose two different and representative microservice applications, TeaStore
[von Kistowski et al., 2018] and Sockshop [Weaveworks, 2017]. Both applications
were developed with the intention of being used in real-world microservices ex-
perimentation.
TeaStore is an e-commerce application that uses 5 microservices to provide a
web-based store for buying tea. SockShop simulates an e-commerce website that
sells socks, supported by 8 microservices developed in multiple programming
languages. In both testbeds, the services communicate using Representational
State Transfer (REST). For more details and architecture, see the Section 2.2.3.
To deploy the applications, we followed the guidelines available in the applica-
tions’ GitHub repositories and deployed each to a Kubernetes cluster. We per-
formed preliminary experiments to tune the intensity of the workloads and the
applicability of the user behavior profiles. The main objective of these tests was to
ensure that the services were not stressed outside the stress scenario, and that the
faulty systems operated effectively. During the final experiments, we considered
two replicas for the WebUI and Persistence services, as these were subjected to
the most issues related to scalability concerns during the preliminary testing.
1See Section 2.3.3 for a detailed analysis of Kubernetes probes.
66

A Study on the Fault Tolerance of Microservice Environments
4.1.2 Workloads
To exercise the microservice testbeds, we utilized Locust 2, which is an easy-to-
use, scalable tool that allows defining the behavior of users that interact with the
target application.
For TeaStore, we created the clients’ behavior profiles based on the request pro-
files available at the public GitHub repository; there are two types of interactions:
browse, that emulates users browsing the store, selecting items and adding them
to the shopping cart as if they would purchase them, but no orders are submit-
ted; buy, emulates users browsing the store and conducting purchases of selected
items. The buy profile was used during the experiments, as it is a more complex
and representative workload.
For SockShop, the interaction profile publicly available was already ready to be
used with Locust. In this profile each transaction includes the user connecting to
the application, listing the items available, and complete purchases.
4.1.3 Measurements
To understand the performance and behavior of each system, we used several
tools for different purposes. We used Locust to get information about the trans-
actions executed, Kubernetes Metrics3 to collect CPU and memory usage per ser-
vice, and Kubernetes Events to extract information about the state of the pods.
The CPU and memory metrics are those typically used in Kubernetes, with CPU
usage measured in millicpu, which is one thousandth of a CPU unit4 and memory
usage measured in bytes.
The transactions performed allow us to track the responsiveness of the system
and the impact of failure, or more specifically, aging, on the system. The use of
Kubernetes metrics is necessary to observe the impact of load changes and faults
on the resource utilization of the system, allowing us to verify if the impact is
noticeable. Events are a resource type in Kubernetes that are generated when
components have errors, state changes, or other messages, and are useful for de-
bugging faults in the system [Jackson, 2020]. Because containers have a state that
can be changed, we can use Kubernetes events to log information about whether
a probe check detected an erroneous state in its operation. If so, the container is
unhealthy, otherwise it is healthy. Using the typical kubectl commands to get infor-
mation about the active pods and resources, the logs of each service and pod, and
the detailed information from the describe command was enough to understand
the state of the container.
2Locust is a load testing framework that simulates user interacting with applications based on
scripted endpoint calls (locust.io).
3Kubernetes Metrics server is a scalable and efficient resource to obtain container resource
metrics for Kubernetes deployments (github.com/kubernetes-sigs/metrics-server).
4A CPU unit is equal to one physical CPU core or one virtual core [Kubernetes, 2020b].
67

Chapter 4
4.1.4 Experimental Setup
The experimental setup consists of three machines. The Kubernetes master ma-
chine has a 2-core processor with 16GB of RAM, while the worker node machine
runs on a 4-core processor with 16GB of RAM. The test driver runs on a sepa-
rate machine to generate the workload with 2 cores and 16GB of RAM. All three
machines run Ubuntu and operate as shown in Figure 4.1, with the Kubernetes
cluster consisting of a master and a worker.
The test driver controls the execution of the experiments, emulates the clients us-
ing the system, and activates the faults. It includes the workload generator based
on Locust, which executes the workloads described above. The Kubernetes clus-
ter deployed for each testbed, following the available recommendations, used the
two available machines and monitored the resource usage of each active service
and the continuous probe operation, looking for possible malfunctions.
Test Driver
Figure 4.1: Experimental setup: microservice application deployed in
Kubernetes, with a fault injected in a microservice.
4.2 Kubernetes Probes
Given the distributed nature of microservice applications, developers tend to use
every tool available to increase the robustness and reliability of their application.
The background concepts on Kubernetes Probes and the types of probes available
is detailed in Section 2.3.3. With the popularity of Kubernetes as an orchestration
platform, there is an urge to understand Kubernetes probes and analyze their
advantages and disadvantages.
Our goal is to analyze and understand the relevant features in the design and
implementation of Kubernetes probes for microservice applications. By under-
standing the design choices made for monitoring the probed service and the re-
sults obtained, we can determine whether these mechanisms actually contribute
to system-wide fault tolerance. This knowledge can contribute to more informed
decisions during the design process.
4.2.1 Best Practices for Probe Design
The design and implementation of probes is a delicate process that affects the
effectiveness of detection. It can also impact the performance of a specific ser-
68

A Study on the Fault Tolerance of Microservice Environments
vice and have repercussions throughout the system. The definition of the readi-
ness and liveness probes needs to take thoughtful consideration to maximize the
outcome from its utilization. They provide the system with effective levels of
resilience and self-adaptiveness.
Based on prior knowledge and analysis from experience [Timofte, 2018; Kuber-
netes, 2020a], a set of best practices emerge. They are presented so that Kuber-
netes Probes can be designed and implemented more effectively:
BP1 Independent application handlers: create specific handlers in the appli-
cation to handle the requests from each probe. This allows each probe to
have a specific endpoint to call and remain an integral part of the applica-
tion, which is important due to Kubernetes’s additional monitoring mech-
anisms. By following these recommendations, developers can rest assured
that probes will not cause complications in the operation of the application.
BP2 Focused goals: the handlers designed for each probe should only perform
verifications and nothing else. For the readiness probe, the components
required for successful operation of the service should be checked. For the
liveness probe, the operational status should be checked and returned as a
simple code (e.g., OK or NOT_OK).
BP3 Report without correcting: probes must not attempt to correct any errors
that may be detected. The only goal is to understand if the system is opera-
tional. Correcting problems found is responsibility of other mechanisms.
BP4 Parameters tuning: in addition to correctly defining the Probes, it is impor-
tant to tune the parameters. The configuration of the parameters should be
carefully tuned so that it does not become an obstacle to the operation of
the service.
The most important parameters are periodSeconds: which specifies how often to
run the probe; initialDelaySeconds: which specifies how long to wait to initi-
ate the probe after the container starts; and timeoutSeconds: which specifies the
number of seconds after which the probe is considered failed [Kubernetes, 2020a].
A small value for the periodSeconds parameter could force the probe to perform
too many checks. Conversely, a large value could leave the container in a broken
state for longer than desired. If the value of the initialDelaySeconds parameter
is too small, the probe might start the diagnosis before the application is ready,
forcing a false restart of the container or forcing the pod to stop accepting traffic.
This requires the programmer to leave time to start the application inside the
container. Regarding the timeoutSeconds parameter, a short timeout may cause
false alarms because the application did not have enough time to process the
action, while a long timeout may leave a broken container running longer than
expected.
69

Chapter 4
4.2.2 Analysis of Probe Design and Implementation
In this study, we use two different approaches to implement Kubernetes Probes.
For TeaStore, we used theregistry service, where each service of the application
is registered when it is online, to perform the health checks. For SockShop, we
used the probes already configured in the deployment configurations available
in the public repository.
Each SockShop service exposes an endpoint that returns the health status of the
service. These endpoints are used by the HTTP liveness and readiness probes.
Regarding the probes already implemented in SockShop, these are specific end-
points for each service that report the ability of the service to operate without
fixing any problems found with it. Thus, the probes already present in SockShop
comply with the best practices mentioned above.
We then conducted a qualitative analysis of the implementation of probes for
TeaStore and the difficulties that may arise. In our experiments, we aimed to
provide each of the five services with a liveness probe. Therefore, we studied
the configurations and actuation mode of the microservices in the application to
understand what were the important points where the probes should focus. Pri-
marily, the communication between the services is done through REST. In addi-
tion, the aforementioned registry collects the list and corresponding information
of each active service. The infrastructure uses a heartbeat mechanism that en-
sures the correct and continuous availability of each service, since if the heartbeat
check fails, the service is removed from the list of active components.
As for the implementation, we started by analyzing the system to understand
it. We used a request to the status page of the WebUI service to understand if
it was working correctly (see Listing 4.1). However, this would add significant
overhead to the service, and this method only satisfied two of the four proposed
best practices, BP2 and BP3.

The configuration changes were shown to have an impact on probe operation.
Since there are multiple probe moments, the requests should be lightweight and
direct to the target. Clearly, this was not the case in the first implementation at-
tempt, as the probe received a full HTML page where a simple status code was
sufficient. A more thoughtful method was devised that allowed the probe to be-
come faster and reduced the overhead it caused in the service. These findings
allow developers to better understand the conditions and steps required to de-
sign and implement Kubernetes probes for microservice applications. It can be
useful when deciding whether to implement probes. It also helps to guide their
implementation by focusing on some aspects that require special attention.
4.3 Microservices Aging Experiments
There is limited information available about the performance of Kubernetes
Probes with respect to detecting aging issues in microservices. We conducted
an experimental campaign on two microservice testbeds instrumented with
Kubernetes Probes to understand how the resilience mechanism responds in the
presence of aging indicators.
The goal of these experiments are:
• to evaluate the effectiveness of Kubernetes probes in detecting aging effects
in microservices
• to study the acceleration of aging effects in microservices through the use
of software faults
71

Chapter 4
For this purpose, we designed an experimental campaign based on representative
microservice-based systems and applied three different scenarios. These scenar-
ios consist of a normal operation, a stress operation, and the exploitation of an
aging fault in a stress environment. The information obtained from this study
contributes to a clearer picture of the protection provided by Kubernetes probes.
In addition, it provides an opportunity to understand whether aging can be ac-
celerated to a usable timeframe within the typical constraints of developing and
deploying microservice-based systems.
4.3.1 Experimental Details
Building on the experimental context described in Section 4.1, we defined the
following specific details for the aging experiments. We develop different opera-
tional environment scenarios (described below) and analyze the behavior of the
services in each of them. The main goal is to look for aging effects and under-
stand how Kubernetes probes react to them. Based on the results obtained from
the stress load experiments, we injected faults into the WebUI service of TeaStore
and the Orders service of SockShop, as these were the services most affected by
the stress scenario. For the experiments, we only injected a memory leak fault
that is active for the duration of the experiments and tends to accumulate mem-
ory resources. The fault used meets the requirements of a typical fault injection
procedure for the purpose of causing aging in the software components it af-
fects [Parnas, 1994; Yue et al., 2020; Grottke et al., 2006].
To analyze the aspects of microservice aging, we designed three experimental
scenarios for the operating environment of the system. The simple scenario con-
sisted of a normal operation of the system over 48h exercised with the normal
workload depicted in Figure 4.2, having 9 active users over time. The stress sce-
nario executed over a period of 48h with the use of a stress load represented in
Figure 4.2, thus overloading the system to provoke aging behavior under stress-
ful conditions. The faulty scenario is a combination of the stress scenario with
the presence of a fault in the WebUI and orders services of the applications. This
scenario runs for a shorter period, only 24h, as it is enough to demonstrate the
intended use of accelerating the effects of aging in microservices.
Each scenario is intended to achieve the following goals:
i) obtain the baseline behavior for the system;
ii) obtain the effects of aging for the system under stress conditions, and
iii) understand if faults can accelerate the effects of aging in microservices.

4.3.2 Results for Aging Experiments
The results obtained during the experimental campaigns with the TeaStore and
SockShop applications are presented in Figure 4.3.
For TeaStore, the Recommender and Auth and DB services were suppressed be-
cause their behavior was similar to that of the Registry and Image services, re-
spectively, so they did not add much and the visualization was clearer. For sim-
ilar reasons, we also suppressed some SockShop services. Namely catalogue,
session-db, shipping, user, and user-db services are not shown.
For both testbeds, the first row contains CPU usage, the second row contains in-
formation about memory usage, and the third row contains data for transactions
executed by the system. The experimental scenarios are shown from left to right:
simple scenario, stress scenario, and failure scenario.
For all scenarios, the systems are exercised with the corresponding workloads as
described in Section 4.1. It is possible to observe the consumption of resources,
which indicates instability, at the beginning and end of the experiments. This is
consistent with the warm-up and cool-down phases. These phases are not consid-
ered in the results analysis, which focuses only on the stable phase of the system.
During this period, the microservices have warmed up and are functioning as
they would in a real environment.
CPU Usage Analysis
For TeaStore the CPU usage is very stable for each scenario analyzed, although
some spikes are observed. For SockShop, there is a similar behavior with the
exception that during the faulty scenario, the orders service shows two spikes in
usage related to the crashes experienced by the service. However, there are clear
differences in CPU usage between the three scenarios for both testbeds.
For TeaStore, the simple scenario shows a fairly stable CPU consumption for
all services, with an upper bound around 200 millicpu. For SockShop, there is a
stable consumption for all services during the stable scenario. The overall CPU
usage stays at a low level without crossing the 200 millicpu line for any service.
This shows that when the load is manageable, the services have very stable and
low CPU usage.
In the stress scenario, the spikes in some TeaStore services (mainly Persistence
and WebUI) are constantly increasing, exceeding 400 millicpu by the 16 th hour
of the experiment and 600 millicpu by the 30 th hour. For SockShop, there is a
noticeable fluctuation in CPU consumption for each service. This indicates that
resources are being used according to the level of user demand. There is a set of
four crashes for the front-end service around the 26 th hour of the experiment,
which corresponds to an identifiable spike in CPU usage in Figure 4.3.
On the faulty scenario, there are some oscillations inTeaStore, similar to the sim-
ple scenario, with CPU consumption barely exceeding 400 millicpu. Still, there is
a slight increase in oscillations to higher levels in the second half, as in the stress

scenario. For SockShop, the behavior of the services remains quite similar to that
observed during the stress scenario. However, there are two moments in the ex-

Memory Usage Analysis
The memory usage observations show more dissimilarity between services
in TeaStore than in SockShop. Some TeaStore services, such as WebUI and
Persistence, suffer from memory accumulation over the course of the experi-
mental campaign. However, the services have different degrees of accumulation,
as WebUI grows faster than Persistence. The SockShop services carts-db and
orders-db show a pattern of continuous increase in memory usage.
74

A Study on the Fault Tolerance of Microservice Environments
Analysis of the simple scenario shows very consistent memory usage across all
services. TeaStore Registry and Image services remain very stable, showing only
slight increases over the course of the experiment. On the other hand, WebUI and
Persistence have a tendency to accumulate memory. This indicates that these
services are more susceptible to memory accumulation and aging effects. For
SockShop, all but the carts-db and orders-db services remain constant in their
memory consumption. These database services are based on MongoDB and may
have problems managing the established connections.
During the stress scenario, we observe similar behavior for all TeaStore services
except WebUI and Persistence. When subjected to stress, WebUI service instances
increase their memory consumption by more than 300% (from 600 to nearly 2500
MebiBytes). This is a concern when dealing with microservices, which tend to be
stressed for a while before scaling events take place. Persistence has a smaller
increase. SockShop orders-DB and cart-DB have increasing memory consump-
tion. This may indicate that stressed services may suffer from aging if their work-
load varies frequently. For these reasons, we decided to instrument the fault in
the TeaStore WebUI and SockShop orders (interacts with orders-db) services.
The faulty scenario has a shorter experimentation period. Nevertheless, we suc-
cessfully achieve more intense aging effects on microservices. The fault injected
in the WebUI service increases memory consumption by more than600% (from 600
to nearly 4300 MebiBytes) in each replica. This is achieved in just under 10h of
experimentation. For SockShop, the orders service cannot continue to process
requests because it is too affected by the fault and crashes twice.
The use of faults can significantly reduce the time required to achieve higher
levels of demonstration of aging effects, which in turn makes the evaluation of
mechanisms and tools that should detect aging on microservices more practical.
Transactions Analysis and Kubernetes Probes Detection
TeaStore successfully responds to most of the transactions it receives during the
three scenarios examined. On average, it fails to respond to 0% during the simple
scenario; it has a failure rate of 6% during the stress scenario, and 5% during the
faulty scenario. Although the rate of failed transactions is lower during the faulty
scenario, the experiment only runs for 24h and shows an increasing pattern. The
successful transactions vary according to the workload intensity used, and it is
possible to see that the system tends to respond successfully to fewer transac-
tions over time. This observation is a consequence of resource accumulation. In
SockShop there is a clear increase in the number of transactions satisfied with the
stress scenario. This is also true for the faulty scenario. For the simple scenario,
SockShop has a failed transaction rate of 13%, while during the stress and failure
scenarios it increases to 17%.
Although there are several service restarts for SockShop, none are due to Probes.
The Kubernetes default mechanism restarts the front-end service four times, all
around the 23th hour of the stress scenario; and it restarts the orders service two
times, around the 9 th and 18th hours of the faulty scenario experiment. The Ku-
75

Chapter 4
bernetes Probes did not detect any problem with the services, and therefore were
not adequate for aging detection.
Overall, the main observations are that aging also affects microservices, and that
Kubernetes probes are not able to detect aging problems and therefore do not ef-
fectively address their correction through service rejuvenation. The experiments
show that a stress load can cause resource accumulation, which can be acceler-
ated by fault injection to have faster effects.
4.4 Kubernetes Fault Tolerance Evaluation
After conducting the aging experiments, it was clear that Kubernetes probes were
unable to detect aging in microservices. So, we extended the study to understand
if this is also true for different types of faults that affect microservices.
The goal of this experiment is to focus on evaluating the effectiveness of the
probes in terms of fault detection rate capabilities, and to understand which
type of faults are more prone to result in probe actuation. This information is
highly valued by developers who are responsible for ensuring maximum avail-
ability and continuous operation of the system. To this end, we have developed
a complete fault injection procedure that is executed through an experimental
campaign that is further detailed to fill this gap and provide empirical knowl-
edge. The experimental campaign is based on two representative testbeds where
faults were injected into different services and activated during the monitoring
of the system.
For TeaStore, the required probes were designed and implemented following the
best practices elicited in Section 4.2; for the case of SockShop, the services already
had probes implemented. We also present experimental details and the results
obtained during the experiments.
4.4.1 Experimental Details
The experimental campaign followed a three-step methodology. First, we set up
the infrastructure, deployed the corresponding application in Kubernetes, and
started the load generator after a waiting period for the application to initialize.
At the midpoint of the experimental slot (15min after startup), the fault is injected
and the slot remains operational for other 15min, resulting in a 30min slot period.
Finally, the information is collected from Kubernetes and the output is analyzed
to see if the probes can detect the malfunction of the system. To serve as a baseline
of the service behavior, we also run a series of golden runs, which are executions
without injecting a fault.
Figure 4.4 presents an overview of the methodology of the experiments with the
slots performed. Each fault was injected into three different slots for each work-
load used. For each repetition, the seed that feeds the random generator for the
selection of the issued requests was modified, so that we ensure independence
76

A Study on the Fault Tolerance of Microservice Environments
between executions. For the experimental runs, we designed and implemented
an intensity load profile characterized by an oscillating pattern of active clients
between 4 and 35 with a period of 10min between peaks.

4.4.2 Faultload
The faults presented in this work are based on an industrial survey, which sum-
marizes the typical faults observed in microservice systems and the difficulties
faced by developers [Zhou et al., 2018a]. The work then replicates the identified
faults in a representative microservice benchmark, and analyzes current debug-
ging practices and explores possible improvements. Based on the set of faults
identified in the work, we selected those that are representative of different cat-
egories and most applicable to the context analyzed in this experiment. These
faults are generalizable and can be found in any application that follows this ar-
chitectural design.
Parts of the deployment and the source code of the applications have been mod-
ified to include the faults presented in Table 4.1. These faults are now part of the
testbeds used in this work and can be activated as needed at runtime through
a dedicated endpoint. The triggered faults have the potential to cause different
failure modes in the microservices they affect. Based on the CRASH scale [Koop-
man et al., 1997] the different faults triggered have the potential to cause any of
the states in the microservices they affect. However, probes are only expected
to identify cases where either the catastrophic, restart, or abort failure modes are
observed. These are the cases where the system has delivery problems that can
cause the service to fail the health check performed by the probe system.

77

Chapter 4
As we can see in Table 4.1, we have six faults that cover different defects in a mi-
croservice application. We represent periodic failures with internal server errors
(fault F3); Server response delays through fault F4; fault F5 sometimes returns
exceptions to user requests; fault F7 covers a business logic error in the applica-
tion; and, F15 and F15t represent a failure of the database, a necessary core part
of any system. All faults focus on the operation of the service they affect and do
not consider specific characteristics of each service such as the transactions or re-
quests processed, as this would reduce the representativeness of the fault. In this
part of the work, we do not analyze aging-related bugs, as that was the focus of
Section 4.3 and was extensively analyzed there.
As we can see on the right side of Table 4.1, onTeaStore faults were injected into
the Recommender, WebUI services and the database. On Sockshop, the injection
happened in the frontend and orders services, as well as the databases for the
orders and catalogue services.
Most of the faults are transient, but there is one ( F15) that is persistent. Since
all the faults are software faults, we have classified them according to the repro-
ducibility of the bug: as Mandelbugs when there is difficulty in reproducing and
detecting them (F3 and F4); and as Bohrbugs for deterministic faults that are eas-
ier to detect (F5, F7, F15, and F15t). All faults occur during the operational phase
and are perceived by the user when a failure occurs.
In microservices environments, faults are complex and can propagate across ser-
vices. For example, the fault that causes the database to go down for a while
may cause failures in other services, even though it is not the root cause. Some
characteristics are inherent to the nature of the injected faults. Namely, the in-
fluence they cause in the system in which they occur. Functional faults cause
malfunctions of the system, as error manifestations or unexpected outputs. Non-
functional faults degrade the quality of service, as performance penalties or in-
crease the probability of system unavailability [Zhou et al., 2018a].
From the root cause perspective, the presented faults have two causes: environ-
ment, where the typical cause of the failures lies in the runtime execution envi-
ronment configurations; and Interaction, resulting from the interactions among
the various services and customers [Zhou et al., 2018a]. The frequency of a fault
provides information about the number of times it is injected during the exper-
imental period, which represents its real-world frequency. These faults can be
low, medium or high frequency.
4.4.3 Results
After the experimental campaign, we collected the data generated. This data al-
lows us to understand the ability of the probes to detect the failures caused by
the faults. As described, the experiments performed are composed of six differ-
ent faults that affect different services in the applications. All services are instru-
mented with probes to detect failures that, if detected, would cause the applica-
tion to restart the component. The results obtained are presented in Table 4.2.
The numbers presented are the results of the average of several experiment slots,
