as we repeated each execution three times. Following we analyze the results ob-
tained in relation to the different characterization of the faults activated. We will
start with a general analysis, ignoring the specific categories or types of faults,
and then carry out a more focused discussion.
All the injected faults were correctly activated, resulting in errors and then fail-
ures [Avizienis et al., 2004]. However, none of the failures are detected by the
probes that monitor the services. In both cases, TeaStore and SockShop, the fail-
ures do not cause the Kubernetes probes to raise an alarm or restart the service.
Regardless of the reproducibility of the fault that occurred, Mandelbug or
Bohrbug, the probes were unable to detect failures in the services of either
system. The boundary definition was also not sufficient to cause the probes to
respond to failures. It was expected that internal failures would be more easily
detected, but both external and internal failures were not detected. Similar
behavior was verified for the root cause and impact of the faults.
The probes did not detect any of the database faults (F15 and F15t). These faults
cause a failure in a service that did not have a probe configured. The failure prop-
agates to the operation of the service that interacts with the database. It causes
the service to be unable to process and respond to requests. As a result, the fail-
ure should be noticed by the probe that monitors the Database API service (e.g.,
Persistence). This indicates that probes check the liveness/readiness of services,
but are not capable of detecting failures in a dependent service. These faults differ
in their persistence, withF15 being persistent and F15t being transient, indicating
that even higher frequency may not be sufficient to ensure detection of faults.
Despite the lack of detection from probes, Kubernetes restarts the database ser-
vice pod when it is affected by a fault F15t. The orchestrator can detect that the
container is having problems due to the intermittent failures it is experiencing,
and restarts the pod to try to resolve the fault. There is no attempt to restart the
pod in the case of a F15 fault. In both testbeds the behavior of Kubernetes is the
same. This mechanism takes place because of the restart policy defined for the
service, and Kubernetes detects that the container is in a failing state.
4.5 Threats to Validity
The experiments conducted allowed us to perform a comprehensive analysis of
the resilience of Kubernetes, evaluating its ability to detect both aging and faults
79

Chapter 4
through the use of probes. It provides both developers and researchers with var-
ious insights that can be useful when dealing with these probes, allowing them
to better understand their limitations and their use. However, it is still worth
mentioning some threats that may arise and raise doubts about the validity of the
experiments conducted.
Regarding internal validity, no real threats can be raised, since each experiment
was conducted independently and different metrics were used. As for external
validity, population validity can be considered a problem due to sampling bias,
which in our case is also related to content validity, as in:
Only one configuration of the probes was used. Probes can be configured to
adjust response times, which are also directly related to their sensitivity. If the
probes are too sensitive, it can cause a pod to restart even if it only experienced
a momentary bottleneck, but the response will be really fast. We decided to use
only one configuration to simplify the problem and the experiments. Neverthe-
less, we followed the probe best practices, which are representative of a real case
implementation.
For the experiments regarding fault tolerance, only 6 different faults were used.
Faults have many classifications according to different characteristics. The faults
we chose, although few, are representative of different categories. Therefore, it
can be argued that they are representative of a reasonable variability of scenarios,
which is sufficient to accurately evaluate the robustness of the probes.
It can also be a threat, when considering the experimenter effect:
Aging experiments were executed only once, therefore random factors may
have affected the experiments, and consequently, the results. Given the na-
ture of the experiments, randomness is prone to occur due to network latency
and even task scheduling, but we feel that these differences are negligible when
looking at the full runs, which are extended periods of time (24h and 48h). There-
fore, even if we had done more repetitions, we believe that the results would not
vary much and the research findings would remain the same.
Ultimately, we believe that these are not enough to negate the findings that have
been made, which are important research advances that should be capitalized on.
4.6 Conclusion
In this section, we summarize the lessons learned from the study presented. The
main findings are the inability of Kubernetes Probes to detect the effects of faults
and reduce their impact on the execution of microservices.
Aging effects can be observed in microservices as in traditional monolithic ap-
plications, (see Figure 4.3). Monolithic applications have an exaggerated com-
plexity compared to microservices, but these services tend to be stressed a rel-
atively higher number of times due to the use of auto-scaling mechanisms. Al-
though this provides good quality attributes in terms of scalability and elasticity,
80

A Study on the Fault Tolerance of Microservice Environments
the use of replication mechanisms also leads to more fault accumulation as a re-
sult of the stress periods. The longer the auto-scaling mechanisms take to operate,
the worse the aging effects of the microservice will be, as the stress is not relieved
in time, which can affect its normal operation.
The utilization of faults in microservices can reduce the time required to ob-
serve the aging effects. Considering the scenario described with aging fault in-
jection, even though it was executed for 24h, the effects seen are more damag-
ing than those experienced during the stress scenario (see Figure 4.3). Therefore,
faults can accelerate pre-deployment testing processes, resulting in a reduced risk
of affecting the performance and even the security of the overall system. Research
into the aging of microservices can also take advantage of this to provide faster
experiences. The fault used can be configured to further accelerate or even decel-
erate the aging process, allowing the microservice’s resource consumption to be
adjusted. It is worth noting that a single slow microservice can deem the entire
system dead by preventing it from performing any requests or transactions if the
system affected by the slowness is critical and the bottleneck for the entire sys-
tem. Aging faults can be used to identify system bottlenecks, or even to evaluate
microservice aging detection techniques, or mechanisms, all in real time.
Kubernetes’s mechanisms were unable to detect both the aging and the faults
of the microservices affected. In our experiments, Kubernetes probes did not
detect any failures in any of the microservice applications (see Table 4.2). The fail-
ures perceived were not significant for the sensitivity of the mechanism. It seems
to detect only significant faults that cause the service to stop responding com-
pletely or to have a noticeably hanged state. However, the use of probes in Ku-
bernetes clusters remains advisable. Even so, developers should be aware of the
limitations of the technique, even when best practices are applied correctly. Using
an approach such as the one presented here has shortcomings and limitations in
detecting faults of different types and frequencies, although it can help with some
assurances. The criticality level of the system must be considered when deciding
which self-healing mechanism to use.
Given the inability of Kubernetes Probes to detect aging and failures in microser-
vice applications, we understand that the security of microservices requires im-
proved mechanisms. Although in this study we do not address security attacks,
it is understandable that if the cause of microservice failures was malicious the
mechanisms studied would fail the same way to detect them.
Hence, we turn our attention to other mechanisms, more focused on security
issues, that can improve the detection of malfunctions on microservice applica-
tions. On Chapter 5, we focus on the applicability and effectiveness of intrusion
detection approaches on simple container-based deployments, the main deploy-
ment for microservice applications.
81

CHAPTER 5
Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
The study on the fault tolerance capacity of microservices mechanisms demon-
strated that a runtime measure is needed to increase the security and dependabil-
ity of microservice applications. The current runtime measures to detect malfunc-
tions and security issues on microservices are limited and fail to detect and act ef-
fectively, which leaves applications exposed to several risks. Intrusion detection
is a promising approach, with several successful application cases across multiple
environments, that has even been applied to very simple container-based scenar-
ios, although still lacking representativeness. Thus, as a conceptual approach
with effective and demonstrated applications to increase the security of systems
by identifying malicious activity, we decided to study its use on container-based
deployments, the common case of microservices.
In this chapter, we focused on studying the applicability and effectiveness of in-
trusion detection in container-based systems. For this, we propose a methodol-
ogy for conducting an evaluation of intrusion detection techniques in the context
of container deployments and environments.
Initially, we focused our efforts on investigating the usability and applicability
of intrusion detection to container-based systems. The goal of this analysis was
to understand whether intrusion detection techniques and algorithms are capable
of generating usable detection models from information collected from containers
under monitoring.
Subsequently, we proposed an approach to evaluate intrusion detection tech-
niques using attack injection to attack the monitored application and test the de-
tection models generated during the training phase. The results obtained demon-
strate the validity of the proposed approach as well as the effectiveness of the al-
gorithms in this context, consistently detecting most of the attacks (89+%), while
the precision values show more variance, but with careful tuning and richer
workloads the problem can be resolved.
83

Chapter 5
This chapter details the effectiveness evaluation of intrusion detection ap-
proaches in container-based systems (Section 5.1), the experimental campaign
(Section 5.2), results and discussion (Section 5.3), the threats to validity (Sec-
tion 5.4), and closes with a conclusion (Section 5.5).
5.1 Evaluating Intrusion Detection Algorithms in
Containers
Intrusion detection has been studied as a way to increase the security of container
deployments, but the application scenarios used to demonstrate the technique
are not very representative of real-world applications [Abed et al., 2015; Tunde-
Onadele et al., 2019; Lin et al., 2018].
In this section, we devise an approach to evaluating intrusion detection algo-
rithms in container-based systems . This is an important aid for administrators
who lack publicly available datasets or well-defined approaches that allow them
to measure the effectiveness and compare different solutions [Abed et al., 2015].
Our approach relies on a target system that is a representative service running on
containers and subjected to realistic workloads. Representative attackloads are
generated by combining these workloads with an attack injection technique. The
approach further innovates in a novel view of false positive analysis that takes
into account the characteristics of containers.
To validate our proposal, we conducted an experimental campaign focusing on
three different environments, Docker and LXC containers, and in a traditional
Operating System (OS) deployment for comparison. The experiments followed
two phases, where first the algorithms processed benign workloads to learn the
behavioral profile, followed by a second phase where we exploit a set of repre-
sentative vulnerabilities with different attacks and verify if the algorithms report
them.
Figure 5.1 provides an overview of the proposed approach, which consists of four
main phases:
1) generation of a training dataset, using representative workloads;
2) generation of a test dataset, by adding attack injection to the workloads;
3) train detection models, using convergence analysis to decide the stopping
point;
4) execute the testing phase to obtain results.
The following subsections describe the different steps of the methodology. We
detail the workload and training step (Section 5.1.1), which describes how the
workload influences the detection models and how training is performed, the
attack injection phase (Section 5.1.2), which is crucial for obtaining test datasets,
and the approach to collecting measures and calculating the selected metrics (Sec-
tion 5.1.3).

5.1.1 Workloads and Training
The workloads used to train the detection models will have a great impact on the
results obtained during the evaluation period. Therefore, it is important to select
appropriate workloads that perform realistic operations against the target sys-
tem, thus maintaining the representativeness of the evaluation and the validity
and acceptance of the results.
The selection of the workload should take into account the usage of the target sys-
tem and the context in which it is deployed. The detection models are then able
to build meaningful and stable profiles, reaching a learning steady state and com-
plete profiles that provide fair results in case of comparison, and more complete
and usable profiles. In our approach, the detection models are trained incremen-
tally, storing their state at a predefined moment during the training period. This
allows to understand and evaluate the impact of the training time on the effec-
tiveness of the algorithm.
Convergence Analysis
To select an appropriate stopping point for training, we use a convergence ap-
proach based on the slope of the growth curve of the profile database, proposed
in [Milenkoski et al., 2015a]. As shown in Figure 5.2, we monitor the addition
of new patterns to the algorithm’s profile database and calculate how much it
grows in intervals of 100s. The formula 0 ⩽
Sts2−Sts1
ts2−ts1
⩽ σ shows whether an
interval is in the learning steady state [Milenkoski et al., 2015a]. Moreover, this
condition must be satisfied 5 times in a row to ensure that the learning procedure
has reached a steady state [Flora and Antunes, 2019] and not a transient interval
where the growth rate has slowed down. σ = 0.15 was chosen based on previous
experiments [Milenkoski et al., 2015a].
5.1.2 Attack Injection
The procedure for injecting an attack requires some parameterization: the selec-
tion of the target vulnerabilities and the corresponding attacks; there is also the
need to select an attack injection point, that is, the moment of the experiment at
which the attack is launched.
85

Chapter 5
Training: STIDE w=4, Docker
Training: STIDE w=4, LXC
Figure 5.2: Convergence of training STIDE, for Docker juxtaposed with LXC.
Regarding the selection of the attackload, the selected vulnerabilities should be
realistic and representative, giving priority to known vulnerabilities in older ver-
sions of systems, for example. The attackload selected will affect the representa-
tiveness and results obtained during the evaluation, so it is important to carefully
consider the options available.
The exploits for the selected vulnerabilities can be collected from several exploit
databases, which are either publicly available or specially developed for this pur-
pose. Obviously, available exploits are preferred because they are faster and eas-
ier to use, but there are also automated tools that can help with the injection of
attacks. However, these should be used with caution because they may compro-
mise representativeness.
In addition, they must represent risks that a system may face in real-world scenar-
ios and have different attack vectors and types of exploitation. That is, different
types of exploits, such as privilege escalation or Denial-of-Service (DoS), should
be covered, ideally using several of the same type.
5.1.3 Measurements and Metrics
The analysis of the results is performed in two main steps. The detection models
perform their evaluation of the test traces using a sliding window according to
their configuration. Each window is classified as normal or anomalous. Next,
the sequence of resulting classifications is analyzed according to an epoch and
detection threshold procedure, where an epoch is classified as anomalous if the
number of anomalous results it contains exceeds the detection threshold.
The epoch and detection threshold procedure is illustrated in Figure 5.3. The aim
of this procedure is to increase the stability and reliability of the results, to reduce
the false positives and to increase the precision by adding parameters that can
be tuned to the required needs. The detection models results are aggregated into
epochs that are classified as either normal or anomalous, depending on whether
the number of anomalous events exceeds the defined detection threshold.
86

Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
NAAANNNNNNEpoch Size: 5
Threshold:   2
Epoch 1: Normal
Epoch 2: Anomalous
Figure 5.3: Example of epoch analysis of the results.
The results obtained in this step are compared with the available ground truth
and four measurements are calculated: True Positive (TP), False Positive (FP),
True Negative (TN), and False Negative (FN). As shown in Figure 5.4, the entire
test slot has been divided into three self-explanatory phases: i) pre-attack; ii) at-
tack; and iii) post-attack. These phases are crucial for the measurements and the
analysis of the results.
All alarms outside the attack section are considered FP, while all alarms inside
the attack section are considered TP (⋆). The symmetrical applies to FN and TN.
They are also crucial because containers are lightweight, and thus their behavior
can be greatly affected after an attack, meaning that the anomalies reported in
the post-attack phase are less relevant. Based on these categories, it is possible to
compute several metrics as defined in [David Martin Powers, 2011], such as recall
((Equation 2.1) proportion of malicious cases), precision ((Equation 2.2) ratio of
correctly classified malicious events), F-Measure ((Equation 2.3) harmonic mean
of precision and recall), and False Positive Rate (FPR) ((Equation 2.4) proportion
of normal events classified as anomalous).
Another widely used analysis for Intrusion Detection System (IDS) selection is a
Recall-Precision curve which plots the true positive rate (recall) against the pre-
cision of an IDS with different configurations, so that they can be compared and
the impact of different parameters on the results produced can be analyzed.
5.2 Evaluation Experimental Approach
To demonstrate the approach outlined in Figure 5.1, we designed an experimen-
tal campaign to validate its usability and applicability. We used three MariaDB
deployments: i) Docker containers; ii) LXC containers; and iii) KVM virtual ma-
chine. This campaign allowed us to understand if the algorithms used in other
contexts [Modi et al., 2013; Warrender et al., 1999; Abed et al., 2015; Forrest et al.,
1996] are effective in container-level deployments, and if this methodology can
be applied to different scenarios.
The use of KVM aims to represent a traditional deployment of an application at
the OS level as a way to provide a basis for comparison with container deploy-
ments. Also, the use of KVM allows to reset the state of the system without the
need to perform a clean installation of an OS for each test.

5.2.1 Experiment Description
The experimental campaign follows the approach described in Section 5.1. The
generation of training datasets consisted of collecting benign data (up to 24H)
and the test datasets containing both benign and malicious traces (over 30min)
resulting from the attack injection procedure. Detection models were built using
each algorithm with different configurations based on the training datasets.
In these experiments, we focused on lightweight algorithms that are effective
at detecting anomalies in event sequences. We chose two sliding window algo-
rithms, Sequence Time-Delaying Embedding (STIDE) and Bags of System Calls
(BoSC), which group system calls into a defined window size and store the order
of the calls in the first case and the frequency of each call within a window in the
second case. We also chose Hidden Markov Models (HMM), a stochastic-based
algorithm that also focuses on the order of system calls.
The state of the detection model is stored at each 6H of training for BoSC and
STIDE, and at 30min, 1H, and 2H for HMM. The detection models were then used
to evaluate the test datasets to produce classifications of the collected events, and
metric values were computed with the obtained measurements.
Each collection consists of the start of data collection and the execution of a work-
load, and an injection attack procedure noticeably in the middle (15min) of the col-
lection period (30min). The collection is then run through the algorithms trained
on one of the workloads. The results are then evaluated as positive or negative
based on the actual truth, giving us four measurements: TP, FP, TN, and FN.
Three different workloads were tested to represent different interactions with the
system (see Section 5.2.2). They represent a constant workload, a variable work-
load, and a variable workload with operator interaction. The experiments include
using the same workload during training and testing, but also using one work-
load for training and another for testing. This allowed us to analyze the impact
of the workload being trained on the IDS in different scenarios.
The attackload consists of a normal workload, but in the middle of the collection
period (15min) the attack on MariaDB is made. It exploits one of the vulnerabil-
ities listed in the 5.2.3 section using the available PoC code. The reason for the
time interval is that it shows how the IDS works before, during and after the at-
tack. This ensures that we get representative data of how the IDS would behave
if the attack were performed on real systems.
During the evaluation phase, we explore every combination of the training work-
loads with the test workloads, resulting in fifteen different combinations.
5.2.2 Benign Training Workloads
In the experiments conducted, three workloads based on the TPC-C benchmark
were used. These workloads differ in the number and stability of the connections
established with the service running in the container, mainly aiming to emulate
88

Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
different service use cases, where one is more stable and predictable, another
experiences more variability in the number of connections established and the
last one, besides being also variable, includes maintenance operations to simulate
the work of an administrator.
– wlSteady is a steady workload with 50 clients continuously performing the
TPC-C transactions to the server.
– wlVariable is a variable workload with a changing number of active
clients. It starts with 10 active connections, and periodically increases the
number of clients until it reaches the maximum of 90 active connections,
when it reduces again until it reaches 10.
– wlOperator is a variable workload with operator interaction which ex-
tends wlVariable with periodically running commands that emulate ad-
ministrative tasks such as verifying the available disk space. Three differ-
ent sets of commands were used, distinguished by wlOperator-n, with n
corresponding to the command set shown in Figure 5.5.
These workloads were selected to provide different operational profiles to help
us understand the impact of workload type on the attack detection capabilities of
detection models.
Figure 5.5: wlOperator sets of command used in the experiments.
5.2.3 Attackload
In the course of collecting the test traces, we performed an attack injection pro-
cedure to generate the malicious events. To do this, we elicited five different
representative vulnerabilities present in an older version of MariaDB with Proof
of Concept (PoC) code available. These PoC, obtained from ExploitDB1, required
some customization to work properly with our setup. Based on our preliminary
research and analysis of the vulnerabilities, we chose MariaDB version 5.5.28. An
overview of the selected vulnerabilities can be found in Table 5.1.
1ExploitDB is a CVE compliant database that stores and provides public exploits for known
vulnerabilities that were developed penetration testers or researchers (exploit-db.com).
89

5.2.4 Test Subjects Used
In this experimental campaign, we used three different environments: Docker,
LXC, and a traditional OS deployment. These deployments cover the most pop-
ular container platforms and also provide a representative OS-based infrastruc-
ture; thus, all of these environments represent real-world scenarios. For the OS
deployment, we used a Kernel-based Virtual Machine (KVM) 2 Virtual Machine
(VM) because it is representative of real-world deployments and allows for easy
rollback of state.
Docker and LXC have different characteristics, even though they are both con-
tainer virtualization software. Docker is designed to support only one applica-
tion per container, called an application container. LXC is more similar to VMs,
although lighter and easier to create, known as OS of system containers. For
more details on container types and technologies, see Section 2.3. These differ-
ences affect the experiments, where we observed a much higher volume of traces
captured from an LXC container; almost double that of Docker (see Figure 5.2).
Thus, different results can be expected for different experiments.
To collect the system calls , we used sysdig because of its native support for
containers. It also supports OSs, which allows a correct comparison between
environments. For the containerized deployments, we collected the system calls
issued by the container as a whole. For the OS deployment, we collected the
system calls issued by the MariaDB application: its root and child processes.
The definition of the monitoring surface affects the results, and this is where
containers have an advantage. In traditional deployments, where there is no
clear separation between relevant applications, an administrator has to define the
boundaries based on acquired knowledge, possibly omitting important informa-
tion or including irrelevant data. For a container, the surface is well defined, and
the entire container can be monitored without including irrelevant data. LXC can
have multiple applications running within it, which can have a negative impact.
The deployments used in this experimental campaign were configured with
Ubuntu 14.04 LTS; MariaDB (v5.5.28) was installed with the default configura-
tion. MariaDB was built from source to create the LXC and Docker container
base image used during the experiments. Two machines were used: 1) target
infrastructure and 2) test driver. The Target Infrastructure machine supports
2KVM is a full virtualization solution for Linux running on x86 hardware with virtualization
extensions (linux-kvm.org).
90

Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
the software being evaluated and is a 6-core 2.8GHz CPU with 32GB of RAM,
running Ubuntu 18.04.2 LTS. The test driver is a 3.20GHz CPU with 16GB of
RAM, running Ubuntu 16.04.3 LTS.
5.3 Results and Discussion
This section presents and discusses the results obtained during the experimental
campaign. The main results are discussed in the following subsections. First, we
provide an overview of the overall results, and then a detailed feature-oriented
analysis is performed. The evaluation results are online (see Appendix A).
The goal of the experiments is to validate our approach in terms of its ability to
compare and facilitate the evaluation of different intrusion detection algorithms.
It also allows us to understand which algorithms and configurations perform
better, showing that the approach allows us to compare and evaluate multiple
intrusion detection algorithms.
The experiments also show that the approach can be generalized, as being ap-
plied to three different scenarios (Docker, LXC, and OS) the approach still pro-
duces meaningful results and makes visible important aspects of different tech-
niques in different scenarios. More specifically, it demonstrates the generally
higher recall values in container-based deployments, regardless of the platform,
when compared to traditional deployments (OS).
5.3.1 Overview of the Results
Looking at the overall results shown in Figure 5.6 allows us to understand some
trends in our data. Namely, the improvement of the precision values with more
training time as well as with more complex and complete workloads. This is
mainly due to the greater amount and variety of data being fed to the recognition
models. This, in turn, contributes to more stable and complete profiles with better
ability to distinguish malicious events from normal ones.
In general, and as shown in Figure 5.6, the recall values are mainly high (hitting
low points only for HMM), remaining between 0.387 and 0.984, while the preci-
sion varies from0.017 to 0.720, with at most a FPR of0.281. Analysis of the chart
also allows us to understand a prevailing detection capacity of STIDE and BoSC
over HMM in both Docker and OS deployments. In fact, HMM is responsible for
producing the lowest results in both precision and recall.
Another interesting observation is that recall values are higher in container de-
ployments in general and in Docker in particular, demonstrating the impact that
a well-defined monitoring surface has on the result obtained. Furthermore, the
differences between Docker and LXC show that even among container solutions,
the numbers diverge, in this case we believe the main reason is the amount of
data produced by each target under monitoring - LXC datasets were on average
several times larger than Docker [Flora and Antunes, 2019].

Figure 5.6: Overview of the experimental campaign results, aggregated by
training time, algorithm, and training workload for each platform.
Despite the low precision values in some cases, we must note that these results
are aggregated and include all test workloads and configurations; careful tun-
ing and configuration allows us to achieve better results. Next, we analyze the
data in more detail, filtering the best results at each step and going deeper into
the configurations used to analyze their impact on the results and select the best
ones. The analysis is divided into several sections, focusing first on the impact
of the training data, then on the configuration of the algorithms, and finally on
the different platforms and how the quality of the profile definition depends on
them. Finally, we analyze the ability of the detection models to distinguish non-
customer interactions, such as administrative operations, from malicious ones.
5.3.2 Training Analysis: Training Time and Workload
In this case, we are looking at the test slots, where the training workload is the
same as that used during the test phase. First, we have to point out that, due
to computational resources, the HMM detection models were trained with only
30min, 1H and 2H of data, which is significantly less than BoSC or STIDE; hence
the lower quality profiles and results.
Nevertheless, the detection models with a longer training time, even in the case
of HMM, show a higher ability to detect malicious events across all platforms and
configurations, with a higher level of precision in the alerts generated. Although
the increased time does not have a significant impact on detection rates, the ef-
fects on precision and FPR are visible. These observations are clear in Figure 5.7
where the increase in precision is noticeable, especially in the case of OS.
The recall values remain high, emphasizing the point of clear distinction between
the malicious events and the normal operations of the system; however, there is a
slight decrease with more training time for LXC and OS. Nevertheless, the values
remain high (80+%), while Docker remains extremely close to 100% for BoSC and
STIDE, and close to 70% for HMM. With respect to OS, the recall values remain
close to 80% for BoSC and STIDE, while HMM does not exceed 50%.
92

Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
Figure 5.7: Impact of the training time in the detection models’ effectiveness.
These results demonstrate the ability of our approach to show the importance of
choosing an adequate training time and the impact on the result. The information
gathered can be used to make a cost-effective decision given the multiple training
times applied, allowing users to segment the training procedure and stop when
requirements are met.
Another important factor during training is the workload used. In this experi-
mental campaign, as mentioned in Section 5.2.2, three training workloads were
used for LXC and Docker, while only two were used for OS. The workloads are
diverse and have different operational profiles and tasks. Figure 5.8 shows the
impact of each workload on the result of the detection models evaluated for each
platform.
The results show an increase in classification correctness when using the most
intensive and complex workload ( wlOperator-1). This workload is based on
wlVariable and supplemented with maintenance tasks performed by an admin-
istrator. In both Docker and LXC, there is an increase in the precision values for
both BoSC and STIDE. Again, HMM performs worse than the other techniques,
showing small increases in precision at a high cost in detection rates.
For both LXC and OS, false positives remain low for each workload used, but for
Docker, there is a significant decrease as more complex workloads are used. In ad-
dition, BoSC achieves better results than STIDE, suggesting that frequency-based
techniques may be more applicable in this environment. Sequence-based tech-
niques (STIDE) have a larger number of possible combinations and take longer
to learn.
In short, the data shows better results for Docker in terms of detection and
demonstrates the importance of considering multiple training times and work-
loads, both characteristics covered by our approach. The detection models
trained over longer periods and with more complex and diverse workloads
show better results in terms of precision and FPR. The computed values favor
wlVariable and wlOperator-1 over the simpler wlSteady; nevertheless, the
latter seems to provide LXC detection models with clearer information due to
93

Chapter 5
Figure 5.8: Impact of the training workload in the detection models’
effectiveness.
the results obtained, which in this case are better when using this workload.
As mentioned before, our approach recognizes that multiple workloads should
be used during the training and testing procedures. The results show that con-
sidering only one workload would contribute to misleading information; having
workloads that contain representative operations and map with different oper-
ation profiles generates a more complete and fair comparison and evaluation of
the algorithms used.
5.3.3 Configuration Analysis: Window Size, Epoch Size, and De-
tection Threshold
The selected algorithms and the methodology have several parameters that can
influence the result. Therefore, here we analyze the impact of the window size,
the epoch size, and the detection threshold on the final results obtained. Fig-
ure 5.9 shows the results of the detection models for the three implementations.
We can observe a very low false positive rate for all detection models, with the
particular exception of HMM when the threshold used was 50. This clearly in-
dicates that this decision threshold is still within the normal behavioral range of
operations.
The general effect of the window size remains constant; that is, increasing the
window size increases the recall level (the algorithms find it easier to detect ma-
licious events), while decreasing the precision with which they are detected. The
reason for this observation lies in the stability and completeness of the profiles
generated; higher values for the window size increase the combinations of se-
quences that a model can hold, resulting in a more incomplete profile that often
raises alarms of malicious behavior that are often incorrect, hence precision goes
down. In summary, to maximize both precision and recall, a smaller window size

Figure 5.9: Impact of the window size parameter in the results.
must be chosen, and either 3 or 4 seem to be the most appropriate values in this
case.
Other factors that affect the results obtained are the epoch size and the detection
threshold. These parameters are applied in the final stage of the results analysis
and their results are used for the metric calculation. To analyze their influence,
recall-precision curves have been chosen so that it is possible to observe the evo-
lution of the detection capabilities for several values.
Figure 5.10 shows the evolution of recall and FPR values over different combi-
nations of epoch size and detection threshold for Docker and OS. The values se-
lected for epoch size are 500 and 1000, while all values used for the detection
threshold during the experiments are present in the chart.
The analysis of Figure 5.10 shows that the evolution of the recall-precision curves
is similar regardless of the epoch size, i.e., higher detection rates lead to lower
precision values. Here, a trade-off must be made when deciding whether to en-
force higher or lower values for the epoch size. Nevertheless, the analysis of the
epoch size shows a clear difference, since smaller values are able to maximize
both metrics more easily. For both algorithms (BoSC and STIDE) and deploy-
ments (Docker and OS), the smallest epoch size ( 500) produced a more fitting
recall-precision curve, meaning that this value produces results closer to the de-
sired ones, and thus can be considered better than the alternative. Additionally,
despite similar behavior, there are small differences between BoSC and STIDE in
both deployments; BoSC performs slightly better than STIDE, achieving values
of higher recall with higher precision for the same configurations.
The reason why smaller epochs perform better is due to the level of granularity.
As the number of events in an epoch is larger, there is a higher probability of
crossing the detection threshold, resulting in higher false positive rates for the
same detection rate.

In short, from the analysis of the results and the impact of these three parame-
ters, we can conclude that window sizes of 3 or 4 and an epoch size of 500 gener-
ally achieve higher detection rates. Therefore, these are the prevailing values to
achieve better results in this context.
5.3.4 Using different workload for training and detection phases
We then perform an analysis to verify the ability of the detection models to gener-
alize the application of the learned knowledge. To do this, we detail the results of
the detection models trained with all three workloads and tested with all work-
loads and command combinations, with the goal of understanding which work-
load generates a broader and more complete profile of the system. Figure 5.11
presents the results filtered by training time ( 24H) and window size using win-
dows 3 and 4. In addition, we focus our attention only on Docker and LXC and
on BoSC and STIDE due to their better performance.
The results are generally similar between the recognition models for the same
deployment, demonstrating good generalization of the recognition models.
However, there is a noticeable increase in precision in LXC when wlSteady is
used during training. This fact contradicts what one might intuitively expect,
since wlVariable consists of an unstable customer behavior and holds more
connections; nevertheless, LXC produces higher amounts of data [Flora and
Antunes, 2019] and the use of wlSteady might generate cleaner profiles. Overall,
the Docker detection models prevail in terms of detection rate at the expense of
producing a slightly higher FPR. In this case, the differences between the two
workloads used during training are in the opposite direction, with detection
models trained with wlVariable generating alerts with slightly higher precision
but significantly lower FPR. Thus, the use of wlVariable during training for
Docker tends to ensure better overall results at low cost to detection rates, which
remain significantly higher than for LXC.
96

Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
Figure 5.11: Generalization capacity of the detection models.
The horizontal axis has on the lower line the platforms, on the middle lines the
algorithms and the training workloads and on the highest line the testing workloads.
As far as the use of the wlOperator-1 workload is concerned, it leads to clear
gains in terms of precision for Docker and, in general, for LXC as well. This
workload can be described as very complete and effective for use during train-
ing, not only providing detection models with the ability to generalize informa-
tion regarding the commands used ( wlOperator-2 and wlOperator-3), but also
contributing with the ability to maintain high detection rate and precision values
for wlSteady and wlVariable. This workload proves its generalization capacity
to other sets of commands as well.
In short, we can observe that the results are satisfactory with both workloads;
however, in DockerwlVariable contributes with lower false positives and higher
precision, while in LXC the use of wlSteady increases the precision obtained.
However, the most significant variations occur when wlOperator-1 is used to
train the detection models; it obtains a high detection rate and precision for each
test workload. This means that the detection models are able to generalize the
knowledge and the learned profiles are more complete and effective, demonstrat-
ing the importance of the quality of the selected workloads.
5.3.5 Distinguishing Benign Administration Operation from
Malicious Events
In this section, we take the generalization of knowledge a step further. In addi-
tion to the detection models from previous analyses, we also focus on additional
detection models trained with wlVariable and data resulting from management
commands, which are benign operations that a system administrator might per-
form. For training purposes, only Commands Set 1 from Figure 5.5 was used,
while during the testing phase, each test case was performed with all three pat-
terns present in the referenced figure. This experiment was aimed at understand-
ing two main points:

Figure 5.12: Performance of detection models faced with management tasks and
attacks.
1) are detection models without prior knowledge of management commands
able to distinguish these operations from malicious events; and
2) are detection models trained with some commands able to generalize their
knowledge and distinguish new commands from attacks.
For this purpose, we conducted the testing phase with three patterns executed
before and after the attack. In addition, we tested the existing detection models
(without knowledge of any commands) and the detection models trained with
Commands Set 1 and wlVariable, whose results are presented in Figure 5.12.
The overall analysis of Figure 5.12 confirms initial expectations that detection
models without prior knowledge of management commands have difficulty dis-
tinguishing non-malicious maintenance operations from malicious events. Al-
though in the case of Docker the recall values are always close to 100%, the de-
tection models trained with either wlSteady or wlVariable are unable to achieve
precision values above 0.400, while in some cases generating FPR as high as
0.180. On the other hand, regardless of the presence of commands during train-
ing, LXC detection models do not produce precision values lower than 0.300;
however, these results come at the expense of attack detection rates, since recall
values do not exceed the threshold of 0.800, which is significantly lower than in
Docker.
The detection models trained on datasets containing maintenance tasks have
higher precision but similar recall values, demonstrating the importance of
knowing how maintenance tasks are viewed in order to filter them and clearly
distinguish them from attacks. Furthermore, there was a noticeable capac-
ity to generalize known maintenance patterns, as for Commands Set 2 and
Commands Set 3 the results were similar, maintaining high precision and low
FPR while detecting the attacks.
In short, the detection models without prior knowledge of management com-
mands have difficulty distinguishing non-malicious maintenance tasks from at-
tacks, while the detection models with task pattern understanding are able to
generalize to other previously unknown commands. Overall attack detection was
high, but the inclusion of management commands in the training is paramount to
increase the precision of alarms and reduce false positives in environments where
this behavior is common.
98

Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
5.3.6 Most representative and promising configurations
In this analysis, we focus on the configurations that perform better in the most rel-
evant scenarios, both in terms of high detection rates and precision. The most di-
verse attackloads are the most challenging in terms of detection (harder to avoid
false positives while correctly reporting the attacks). This means that the test
datasets based on wlSteady and wlVariable were avoided. We did the same for
Docker and LXC and sorted the results in descending order using the F-Measure
and selecting the top 10. These can be seen in Table 5.2 for Docker and in Table 5.3
for LXC.
Analyzing the values present in Table 5.2 we can conclude that the top 10 detec-
tion models were all trained with the wlOperator-1 workload and used e=500
with d=100; resulting in recall values ranging from 0. 937 to 0.971 and pre-
cision between 0.858 and 0.903, while not exceeding 0.002 of FPR. The pre-
sented detection models reach their full potential mainly when tested with the
wlOperator-2 workload and with w=3. The BoSC algorithms take the top two
positions, achieving F-Measure values of 0.936 and 0.931.
These results clearly show that richer and more complex training workloads do
indeed make a significant difference, both in the representativeness of the exper-
iments and in the quality of the results obtained.
Of the ten detection models present in Table 5.2, the majority (6) use BoSC as the
algorithm, while the remaining 4 use STIDE, showing a prevalence of frequency-
based techniques in obtaining better results. In fact, the only detection model that
uses w=4 uses BoSC, indicating that this method is able to achieve better results
even when using higher granularity. However, the overall preferred configura-
tions are the use of w=3, e=500, and d=100.
Regarding LXC, the analysis of Table 5.3 shows that recall values are in the range
of 0.806 to 0.895, precision between 0.877 and 0.980, with very low FPR. LXC
has some similarity to Docker’s results, but the differences are very interesting.
For instance, in LXC, all the training workloads are in the top 10 detection models,
although the majority of them are due to the use of wlOperator-1. Although the
best performing detection model uses w=3, the majority of the best results come
from w=4, and despite the larger number using d=100, the best result uses d=20.
The reason why a larger window appears more often in these results is related

to the larger amounts of data that must be processed in LXC traces. Although
LXC has more unique system calls on average, there is also a small fraction of
them that are issued more frequently; thus, the diversity is distributed among the
most frequent system calls, which require larger windows to accommodate the
repetitions.
Another interesting insight from these results is that the best performance is
achieved with 12H of training, which contradicts the general observation in Sec-
tion 5.3.2 that 24H results in more effective detection models; in fact, the same
configuration with 24H of training does not show up in the top 10 and results in
recall=0.773 and precision=0.954. This shows that despite achieving better
precision, the loss in recall is much higher.
In short, the detection models have better results when trained with
wlOperator-1, demonstrating the impact of representative and rich work-
loads on their performance. Another important observation is that smaller
epochs perform better, implying that lower granularity is required to achieve
better discrimination between malicious and normal events; in fact, most require
a high detection rate, ensuring that at lower granularity it takes a significant
percentage of anomalous events to produce satisfactory results. For instance,
if e=500 and d=100, this means that at least 20% of the events within an epoch
are considered anomalous. In addition, the use of smaller windows seems to
work correctly with more diverse traces, while larger windows work better
with less diversity or larger amounts of data. Related to this, the use of smaller
epochs with the need for a high percentage of anomalous events within them
is appropriate for high diversity datasets, while lower percentages, such as 4%
with e=500 and d=20, work better with lower diversity or larger amounts of data.
5.4 Threats to Validity
The experiments conducted using the proposed methodology allowed us to gain
important insights into the effectiveness and applicability of intrusion detection
in container-based environments. The use of detection algorithms shows promis-
ing results for increasing the security level of applications deployed using this
technology. Nevertheless, it is worth mentioning that some validity threats were
identified during the execution of the experiments.
100

Studying and Evaluating Intrusion Detection in Containers
Regarding internal validity, no real threats can be raised since each experiment
was performed independently and different metrics were used.
Some external validity threats should be mentioned, mainly related to some lim-
itations that we recognized; however, we are confident in the overall significance
of the work and the results presented. In this section, we highlight the threats to
the validity of our study.
Vulnerabilities are not specific for containers. All the vulnerabilities used in
this experimental campaign are specific to the MariaDB service. This was moti-
vated by the need to port the experiments across three different deployments in
order to fairly apply the methodology to each setup. However, the primary goal
of the experiment was to demonstrate the applicability of intrusion detection to
container-based environments and to validate the effectiveness of the detection
models when applied. For these reasons, the use of MariaDB vulnerabilities does
not compromise the conclusions of the study.
Reduced number of exploits. While the number of exploits may be small (five) to
present statistically significant results, the vulnerabilities selected are diverse in
both the type of attack and the attack methodology examined, and the resulting
data provides important analysis. This initial study allows us to understand the
specifics of training detection models using data from containers and how they
differ from typical OS deployments. It also provides important insights into the
impact of a well-defined monitoring surface that containers provide versus OS
deployments.
Reduced number of algorithms. In this experimental campaign, only three state-
of-the-art algorithms have been selected and analyzed. These are state-of-the-art
lightweight algorithms that achieve satisfactory results, factors that contribute to
their applicability in practice. Other algorithms could be used, but they would
not have the applicability of the ones used, and we believe that there would not
be much to gain in terms of meaning and significance to include other methods.
Nevertheless, the methodology is general, and it would be possible to apply it to
other types of intrusion detection algorithms.
5.5 Conclusion
In this section, we summarize the lessons learned from the presented study. Be-
sides demonstrating the applicability of the proposed methodology, the exper-
imental campaign allows us to draw some insights regarding the effectiveness
of applying intrusion detection techniques to container-based systems; and how
they can be improved and applied in real-world scenarios.
Window-based algorithms perform better.Our experiments show that window-
based algorithms outperform statistical approaches. Although HMM could not
be applied to LXC datasets, the overall picture is that STIDE and BoSC result in
higher detection rates on each platform, while maintaining high precision and
low false positive rates. Extending these experiments to other types of algorithms
101

Chapter 5
(e.g., Machine Learning (ML)) may show that window-based algorithms have
shortcomings; however, applying ML approaches would potentially increase
training overhead.
Achieved better results in container deployments. Comparing the results ob-
tained in the OS deployment and the container deployments (Docker and LXC),
the detection models performed better in the latter. The main reason is related to
the very well-defined monitoring surface in the case of containers, which reduces
the need for user interaction in this step. The well-defined monitoring surface re-
moves the subjectivity introduced by the manual selection of relevant processes,
which in turn increases the accuracy of the detection models.
Algorithms can distinguish between attacks and management operations. In
general, the detection models are able to clearly distinguish between malicious
events and those resulting from administrative operations. This is an interesting
finding, which means that the execution of maintenance tasks by system admin-
istrators cannot affect the results produced by the detection models. This can be
verified by the rate of alerts raised in each of the three sections defined during the
testing phase. The alerts were generally 5 times higher during the attack section,
except for the CVE-2016-6662 exploit.
Based on the lessons learned during the study and evaluation of intrusion de-
tection for container-based systems, we understand that intrusion detection is a
promising approach to improve the security of microservice applications. Intru-
sion detection approaches are actually able to define detection models that are
clearer in container deployments than they are in traditional OS-level deploy-
ments. The techniques achieve even higher detection rates with Docker deploy-
ments, which is the most common container platform used with microservice
applications.
There are challenges that need to be addressed, so intrusion detection can deal
with the dynamicity, scalability, and elasticity of microservices. Thus, Chapter 6
proposes improvements to the intrusion detection pipeline so that the mechanism
can effectively improve the security of microservice applications.
102

CHAPTER 6
Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
The growing complexity and dynamicity of microservices, combined with their
ability to scale, present significant challenges to security monitoring tools. Inte-
grating these tools into a DevSecOps pipeline is currently impractical, necessi-
tating research into adaptive intrusion detection approaches. Nevertheless, it is
imperative to incorporate security into this paradigm and ensure its continuous
adaptation to the evolving landscape. Intrusion detection has been used to in-
crease the security level of these systems but faces significant challenges: namely,
effectively processing all the information produced by the services and keeping
up with constant modifications and releases. We explore host-based intrusion
detection by capturing microservices’ behavior, which encounters a significant
challenge when dealing with the dynamicity of microservices that causes detec-
tion models to become outdated.
In this chapter, we propose a set of components that aim to improve the effective-
ness of intrusion detection in scalable, elastic, and dynamic microservice applica-
tions. First, we introduce three data processing techniques that enable intrusion
detection in scalable and elastic microservice applications utilizing Continuous
Integration and Continuous Delivery (CI/CD) approaches. These techniques ma-
nipulate data collected from active microservice replicas and feed it to algorithms,
resulting in reliable intrusion detection even after scaling operations. Second, we
propose three techniques that allow to obtain the core behavior of a microser-
vice into intrusion detection models. The techniques capture a stable model of
a microservice core and can be applied across service releases. Finally, we pro-
pose a detection model reuse metric and conduct a study to assess its feasibility.
The metric leverages data from the service’s source code, its runtime data and
algorithm patterns to understand whether models from prior versions can be re-
purposed to newer versions.
To evaluate these techniques and metric, we integrate them into an evaluation
103

Chapter 6
methodology in which the detection models are evaluated using datasets gen-
erated in a representative system with known vulnerabilities through an attack
injection methodology with a diverse set of representative attacks, and using two
lightweight algorithms: Sequence Time-Delaying Embedding (STIDE) and Bags
of System Calls (BoSC). The results show that: i) the techniques improve detec-
tion effectiveness in scalable scenarios; ii) obtaining the core of a service helps
with reusing detection models across several releases; and iii) the metric portrays
the impacts of modifications across releases.
This chapter covers the different improvements that integrate with intrusion de-
tection (Section 6.1), starting with techniques to deal with scalability and elastic-
ity (Section 6.2), address detection model reuse across microservice releases (Sec-
tion 6.3), and a metric to compute the likelihood of a detection model to be reused
(Section 6.4). The chapter also covers the evaluation of these techniques, with the
experimental approach (Section 6.5), the results for scalable environments (Sec-
tion 6.6), the results for detection model reuse (Section 6.7), and the results for
use of the metric (MRI) (Section 6.8). It closes with threats to validity (Section 6.9)
and a conclusion (Section 6.10).
6.1 Integration with Intrusion Detection
The proposed approaches are designed to integrate seamlessly with a typical
anomaly-based intrusion detection methodology, as shown in Figure 6.1, which
traces

An automated approach to collect data traces from microservices and produce
detection models that generalize knowledge and detect deviations during runtime,
raising alarms.
104

Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
operates in two main phases: the training phase for learning operational profiles,
and the detection phase for analyzing events to detect intrusions.
During the training phase, the algorithms use benign system information to cre-
ate benign detection models. These profiles serve as references during the de-
tection phase, where they identify deviations from the learned normal behavior
and raise alarms when potential intrusions are detected. The data processing
approaches are responsible for processing the collected data and feeding it to
the intrusion detection algorithms. This step is easily accomplished in an intru-
sion detection pipeline, as was done during the design and implementation of
µSherlock (see Chapter 8).
Regardless of the number of active replicas, the detection models can be used
continuously to effectively detect security breaches and ensure the uninterrupted
security of service replicas (see Section 6.2). This capability eliminates the need
for multiple training conditions to cover all deployment scenarios, promoting
profile reusability and generalization of acquired knowledge. In addition, tech-
niques for capturing the core behavior of a microservice work in a similar way by
processing the collected information to provide improved detection rates across
service releases (see Section 6.3).
6.2 Dealing with Scalable and Elastic Microservice
Environments
In this section, we propose three data processing techniques to enable effective
intrusion detection in scalable and elastic microservices, suitable for integration
into DevSecOps pipelines as Runtime Application Self-Protection (RASP) [Mad-
den, 2020]. The techniques group events, generalize knowledge, and handle large
amounts of data, allowing algorithms to capture reusable profiles of replicated
services and maintain security levels as scaling events occur. We assume that each
service is deployed in its own container, allowing for non-intrusive black-box
monitoring. The focus is on host-based intrusion detection by analyzing system
call data collected from each service. The proposed data processing techniques
make detection models more effective in scalable and elastic environments. The
Figure 6.2 illustrates each approach using the STIDE algorithm as an example.
Independent Replica Aggregation (η)
Algorithms with this approach store information from all service replicas, which
intuitively leads to more comprehensive profiles resulting from a combination of
all independent patterns. Instead of creating separate profiles for each replica,
they are combined into a single detection model. This approach increases the
number of patterns for event classification during the detection phase.
This technique can be formalized as follows. Let R be the set of all active service
replicas in a microservice application. For each replica r in R, let Pr be the set of
behavior patterns collected from replica r. Then, the detection model B, used by
the detection model, is defined as the union of all patterns from all replicas:

Trace Events in Total Order (τ)
This technique combines the information from all service replicas using times-
tamps to establish chronological order. By combining the information from all
replicas into a chronologically ordered stream, we intuitively construct a detec-
tion model that targets the replica set. As shown in Figure 6.2, this approach es-
tablishes a connection between the information produced in a set of service repli-
cas. The technique can be formalized as follows. Let S be the set of all system call
data collected from active service replicas, and let T be the set of corresponding
timestamps. The technique combines this information into a single data stream
based on the timestamp to form a chronologically ordered stream D:
D ={(s, t)| s∈ S, t∈ T}
(s, t) corresponds to the same event
This ordered stream D is then used to construct the detection model B represent-
ing the operational profile of the replica set: B = f (D). Where f is the algorithm
that processes the ordered stream D and combines the information from all repli-
cas to create the detection model B.
106

Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
Replica Periodic-Interleaving (θ)
This approach extends trace events in total orderand sequentially extracts intervals
of data from alternating replicas, each with a duration of t units of time. That
is, it appends the events from each replica within an interval to the stream. In
this way, this approach reduces breaks in the sequence of system calls, aiming
to preserve the relevance of system call sequences and minimize interruptions
caused by the aggregation process. It allows to preserve the proximity of certain
calls occurring in the same replica. The approach can be formalized as follows.
Let R ={R1, R2, . . . ,Rn} be the set of replicas, where n is its total number. Let t
be the interval length in units of time for sequential extraction. The event stream
D is created by sequentially extracting events from alternating replicas within the
interval t and appending them to the stream, and can be defined as:
D =

where:
• Rn[(i− 1)t : it[ represents the events within the interval of lengtht extracted
from replica Rn,
• k is the number of intervals required to cover all events in Rn, i.e., k =

• the union operator∪ combines events from all intervals of each replica into
the event stream D.
The event stream D is then used to construct the detection model B representing
the operational profile of the replica set: B = f (D). Where f is the algorithm that
processes the event stream D and combines the information from all replicas to
create the detection model B.
For example, Figure 6.2 illustrates a possible application. First it extracts three
seconds of information from replica r1, then three seconds of data from replica
r2, followed by another extraction of three seconds of information from r1, and
finally it completes the stream with the remaining information from r2. The re-
sulting stream is then fed into the algorithm.
6.3 Detection Model Reuse for Microservice Environ-
ments
In this section, we propose three techniques that derive the core information from
the microservice’s behavior, which in turn generalizes these models, enabling
their reusability across different versions of a service. Intuitively, the core be-
havior of a microservice contributes to the majority of patterns collected from a
107

Chapter 6
microservice, and would allow, for example, improved reuse of detection models
and the ability to protect different versions of a microservice without requiring
new data collection and training phases. These proposed approaches operate at
the level of the system call alphabet, in two different variants, used to construct
patterns (pre-processing) and at the level of the stored patterns (post-processing).
Pre-processing Techniques (f t & qu)
Host-based detection models are based on patterns constructed from the execu-
tion of system calls. Applications make some system calls more frequently than
others. Intuitively, the more frequent system calls will contribute to the major-
ity of the patterns used as a baseline for the detection model’s benign behavior
database. This set of system calls represents the core behavior of the microser-
vice. The core of microservices is defined early on and, despite changes across
releases, remains largely unchanged unless a major refactoring takes place. That
is, the core of microservices should be very similar across different versions of the
same microservice.
Taking this into account, this technique intends to pre-process the traces of system
call data used during training and select the system calls that are executed more
frequently. This would allow for fewer patterns to be stored during training, but
would also emphasize the core of the executions. That is, the most frequently
observed calls are assumed to be more important to the service and thus have a
higher ability to generalize from the resulting knowledge.
Based on this intuition, the first variant (f t) of this technique is based on selecting
the most frequent system calls from the entire microservice system call alphabet.
The second variant ( qu) of this technique is based on the cumulative frequency
function for each system call.
For both variants, let Σ be the alphabet of unique system calls seen during the
data collection for training, and s a system call of the alphabet, such that s∈ Σ.
Furthermore, Σ contains information about the frequency of each system call and
is ordered in descending order of frequency. Let Θ be the core set of system calls
resulting from the application of each technique, which will be used during train-
ing to construct the patterns. Also, 0 < α < 1 is a parameter which determines
the size of the core set and can be configured by the user.
For the first variant ( f t), the selection of s is based on the following criteria:
Θ ={si|si∈ Σ, and i≤ round (α×| Σ|)}
For the second variant ( qu), let c f(s) be the cumulative frequency of the system
call s in the training trace data. Then, the core set Θ is defined as:
Θ ={si|si∈ Σ, and c f(si)≤ α}
In this manner, the first variant selects based on the size of the initial system call
alphabet and the frequency of the system call, while the second variant selects
based on the cumulative frequency of the system call. When the first variant is
used, algorithms are prefixed with f t (e.g., f t_stide); while for the second variant,
the algorithms are prefixed with qu (e.g., qu_stide).
108

Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
Post-processing Technique (p)
The patterns stored during the training process have a direct impact on the effec-
tiveness of the model. Similar to system calls, the service may provide multiple
endpoints, but user requests may interact with one endpoint more often than an-
other. Also, some patterns that appear seldom in the training data may not be as
important to the service. Based on this assumption, we developed a technique to
reduce the number of patterns stored in the model, keeping only the most impor-
tant core patterns.
This technique ( p) leverages this factor to trim the detection models of less fre-
quent patterns. As in the pre-processing techniques, let Σ be the alphabet of
unique system calls seen during the data collection for training, and s a system
call of the alphabet, such that s∈ Σ. Let Ψ be the set of system calls patterns ν
captured during the training phase, such that ν∈ Ψ, which will be used during
detection to identify intrusions. Also, 0 < α < 1 is a parameter which determines
the size of patterns kept and can be configured by the user; let c f(ν) be the cu-
mulative frequency of the pattern ν in the benign behavior database. Then, the
patterns core set K is defined as:
K ={νi|νi∈ Ψ, and c f(νi)≤ α}
In this way, the post-processing technique selects the patterns based on their cu-
mulative frequency. When this approach is used the algorithms are prefixed with
p (e.g., p_stide).
6.4 Model Reusability Index (MRI)
In this section, we propose Model Reusability Index (MRI): a metric designed
to provide insight into the reusability of intrusion detection models for microser-
vices. This metric can be used to make decisions about whether to reuse older de-
tection models when new microservice releases occur, or when retraining should
take place. Furthermore, it can be used in benchmarking procedures with the
appropriate workload to evaluate whether an algorithm is suitable for producing
detection models that are more likely to be reusable. To do this, we use source
code, runtime data, and algorithm patterns from the microservice to generate a
meaningful and useful numerical value representing model reusability.
The metric quantifies the similarity between detection models trained on different
microservice releases based on source code similarity, system call alphabet, and
algorithm patterns [Flora and Antunes, 2024c]. The system call alphabet and al-
gorithm pattern analysis is based on runtime data collected during the execution
of the microservice. The system call alphabet corresponds to the set of unique
system calls invoked by the microservice, while the algorithm patterns refer to
the patterns built on top of this system call sequence. The definition of MRI is as
follows:
109

MRI =
n
∑
i=1
ωiCi = ω1CD + ω2Al + ω3RI
where
C components contributing to the metric (CD: source code similarity, Al: sys-
tem call alphabet, and RI : detection model reusability)
ω the weights for each of the components in C, where Σn
i=1ωi = 1
n the number of components in C and ω, as|ω| =|C|
For the source code , we apply a similarity metric based on a string matching
Greedy String Tiling and Running Karp-Rabin Matching algorithms, which is
a simple and effective way to obtain the similarity between two releases of the
microservice [Wise, 1993].
For the components based on the system call alphabet and algorithm patterns,
the similarity level is measured using the Tversky index, a measure of the sim-
ilarity between two sets [Tversky, 1977], by applying it to the two microservice
versions (see Definition 2). We use α = 0.1 and β = 0.9 because, for the purpose
of reusing detection models, the information in the new microservice release that
is not in the old one is more relevant.
Definition 2. The Tversky index is defined as:
S(A, B) = |A∩ B|
|A∩ B| + α×| A− B| + β×| B− A|
where in this context
A is the set from the older microservice release
B is the set from the new microservice release
α, β are the parameters of the Tversky index.
(Applied to system call alphabets and algorithm patterns.)
6.5 Evaluation Experimental Approach
To perform the experimental evaluation of the different techniques and metrics,
we adapted the methodology proposed in Section 5.1 to evaluate each set of tech-
niques with host-based intrusion detection algorithms in microservice applica-
tions. For each evaluation case, scalability and reusability, we developed a varia-
tion of the approach as shown in Figure 6.3 and Figure 6.4.
110

Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
The methodologies cover the data generation process, using attack injection to
generate data for testing, and are designed for microservice applications with
different states of the system being monitored. Training traces are used to train
detection models, while test traces are used to evaluate their effectiveness. We
collect measurements and calculate metrics to evaluate performance.
6.5.1 Microservices Scalability and Elasticity
To evaluate the effectiveness of data processing algorithms in handling scaling
events, we constructed multiple scenarios for both the training and detection
phases, including 1, 2, 3, 5, and 7 active replicas of the WebUI service. These
variations in the number of replicas, with scale-down and scale-up of the target
microservice, allow us to collect data traces in multiple scenarios, as shown in
Figure 6.3. This resulted in a total of 20 evaluation scenarios.
This allows us to directly compare the different data processing approaches to
each other and to the simple algorithms. This comparison allows us to determine
whether the algorithms that use data processing techniques are more effective.

6.5.2 Microservices Continuous Integration and Continuous De-
livery
To evaluate the effectiveness of the impact of the reuse techniques along with the
algorithms on the detection models built, we constructed different scenarios for
both the training and detection phases based on the variations of the monitored
releases (see Figure 6.4). We used two versions of TeaStore, v1.2.0 and v1.3.8,
obtained from the official GitHub repository 1. To create a variety of releases for
the service, we also used three versions of Tomcat (v7.0.57, v8.5.0, v9.0.0) and
combined them, resulting in a total of six different configurations.
The selection of the referenced versions followed a combination of major and
minor version changes. This combination allows building different releases of the
microservice with different depths of change. It can vary either on the application
source code or on the deployment server, or a combination of both. This allows
for a wide range of detection effectiveness tests.

6.5.3 Algorithms and Configurations
The algorithms examined include BoSC and STIDE, both of which use a sliding
window over a system call trace. These algorithms build a baseline behavioral
database containing patterns of benign system calls, which are then used to detect
deviations (i.e., intrusions). While BoSC stores windows as "bags" containing the
frequency of each system call within the window, STIDE preserves the original
order of system calls. During the evaluation, we chose window lengths of 3 and
4. For more details on each algorithm, see the Section 2.5.3.
For the methodology parameters, we explored epoch sizes of500, 1000, and 5000,
and detection thresholds of 5, 10, 20, 50, and 100.
6.5.4 Environment Setup
Experimental Settings. We deployed the application on a machine with an In-
tel Core i5 CPU and 32GB of RAM. For load generation and attacks, we used
another machine with an Intel Core i5 CPU and 16GB of RAM. Both machines
were running Ubuntu 18.04. The application was deployed using three versions
of Tomcat: 7.0.57, 8.5.0, and 9.0.0 within Docker 18.06.3. While these versions are
somewhat outdated, they are still widely used today and represent technologies
commonly found in production software.
Workloads and Attacks. We used four different workloads resulting from two
intensity profiles, consisting of the number of requests and two user behavior
profiles, and two user behavior profiles detailing the requests made by users.
One intensity profile maintained a constant number of 1000 requests, while the
other varied between200-1800. One behavior profile includes users browsing the
store, adding and removing items from the cart (with 11 requests), and the other
also includes purchase actions (with 13 requests), resulting in a richer operational
profile.
We selected known vulnerabilities to attack multiple levels of the system. The
vulnerabilities are detailed in Table 6.1, including the Common Vulnerabilities
and Exposures (CVE) IDs, affected components, class, type of exploitation and
impact, and the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) score. We chose
these vulnerabilities because they are representative of real security problems.
