2017-16995 Corruption DoS, Overflow, MC 7.2
2017-7308 Access-Control DoS, Overflow, PE 7.2
Legend - DoS: Denial of Service; PE: Privilege Escalation;
MC: Memory Corruption; XSS: Cross Site Scripting
Data Collection Method. We collected system call data using µDetector2, which
uses sysdig [Sysdig, Inc, 2019]. The data is processed according to the specificity
of each proposed approaches and the algorithms.
6.5.5 Ground Truth and Alert Frequency
The proposed approach is designed for systems supported by containers, which
are lightweight and contain only the components necessary to run the service. To
ensure that intrusions are detected by the detectors, the algorithms must capture
a stable and comprehensive behavior profile.
We have divided the evaluation process into three phases: pre-attack, attack (cor-
responding to the duration of the attack), and post-attack. These phases are de-
fined around the injection of the attack and serve the purpose of establishing three
perspectives of analysis. The ground truth for evaluation is built around the at-
tack injected in each experiment. Based on the attacks, reported time intervals are
compared, TP and FN are counted.
6.6 Results for Detection in Scalable Environments
This section presents and analyzes the results obtained from the evaluation of the
proposed data processing approaches to address scalable and elastic microservice
environments. The goal of the evaluation is to demonstrate their performance in
different scenarios, which are detailed and analyzed here. In this evaluation, we
train several behavioral profiles using different algorithms, both with and with-
out the proposed data processing techniques. In order to perform a fair evalu-
2µDetector was developed in the context of a master thesis work by Miguel Teixeira. See
Appendix A for source code and context.
113

Chapter 6
ation of the approaches, we focused our analyses on the pre-attack and attack
phases, as they allow us to determine whether the classifiers are effective. The
complete evaluation results are available online (see Appendix A).
We train profiles using algorithms with and without data processing techniques.
For a fair evaluation, we analyze the pre-attack and attack phases. To clarify the
purpose of this evaluation and the usefulness of the data processing techniques,
we address the following research questions:
RQ1: Which configurations for the algorithms and reporting mechanism achieve
higher detection effectiveness?
RQ2: What are the impacts of the techniques on detection effectiveness for both
stable and scalable deployments?
RQ3: How effective are data processing approaches in detecting different classes
of attacks?
6.6.1 Configuration T uning
We conducted a case study with three active replicas during training and detec-
tion, a variable intensity load, and user browsing behavior. We selected the top
25 configurations based on two scenarios: i) Business Critical (BC) and ii) Best
Effort (BE). For BC, we prioritized recall as the selection metric, with precision
as the tiebreaker, while for BE, we used F-Measure as the selection metric, with
recall as the tiebreaker [Antunes and Vieira, 2015b].
The results (see Figure 6.5) show a similar pattern for both scenarios. The combi-
nation of a window size of3, an epoch size of 500, and a detection threshold of50
consistently produces better results for both the BC (26 times, 7% of the total) and
BE (40 times, 11% of the total) scenarios. Other triplets such as w=3;e=1000;d=50
(6% of total for BC and 8% for BE) andw=4;e=500;d=50 (6% of total for BC and 8%
for BE) also appear frequently. Further analysis in this section uses these triplets
for algorithm configuration.

6.6.2 Effectiveness with stable and scalable scenarios
To understand the impact of the techniques on stable and scalable deployments,
we compared the results of simple algorithms with those of data processing
techniques. We used four metrics: recall, precision (prec.), F-measure (F-M),
and markedness (mark.) to evaluate the results under three different scenarios:
Business Critical (BC), Best Effort (BE), and Minimum Effort (ME) [Antunes and
Vieira, 2015b]. Table 6.2 presents results for stable deployments and Table 6.3
presents results for scalable deployments.
In stable deployments with three replicas, the results consistently show a recall
above 0.64, while with five replicas the recall remains above0.52. In the majority
of cases, the techniques allow for better results in all scenarios (5 / 6).
For scalable deployments, recall was always above 0.52, and with five replicas,
it was always above 0.53. As with stable deployments, simple algorithms had
higher recall but lower precision. The use of data processing techniques resulted
in profiles that performed better in the three analysis scenarios (8 / 12). Better
effectiveness was observed when training with more replicas than those used in
detection.
To address RQ2, we considered two stable and four scalable scenarios. The re-
sults in Table 6.2 and Table 6.3 show thatin 72% of cases, algorithms using data
processing techniques outperformed simple algorithms(STIDE and BoSC). This
means that in almost 3 out of 4 cases, data processing approaches resulted in bet-
ter effectiveness. For a BC scenario, data processing approaches were better in
50% of the cases, with slightly lower recall values but consistently higher preci-
sion. For BE and Minimum Effort (ME) scenarios, data processing approaches
showed better results 83% of the time.

Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
Overall, the classifiers performed well, demonstrating high precision ( ≥0.88)
and satisfactory recall with a range of [0.33, 0.77] . However, they struggled to
detect bypass vulnerabilities, where recall was at most 0.50. On the other hand,
memory corruption was effectively detected with recall values always above
0.60, and access control showed a recall of at least 0.71.
Among the different techniques, theη-stide-3 profile consistently produced better
results, as expected based on observations from Table 6.2. For the access control
and bypass attack classes, it ranked first in every scenario. For memory corrup-
tion, the replica periodic-interleaving technique ranked first every time, twice
with θ-stide-3 and once with θ-bosc-3.
So, to answer RQ3, we find that the techniques are able to detect attacks from
all three classes. However, they are more effective at detecting access control
and memory corruption, achieving recall values of 0.77 and 0.78, respectively,
with precision values of 1.00. Bypass attacks are more difficult to detect, with a
maximum recall of 0.50 and precision of 1.00.
6.7 Results for Detection Model Reuse
This section presents and analyzes the results obtained from the effectiveness
evaluation of the proposed techniques to reuse detection models across different
versions and to address the dynamic microservice environments using CI/CD.
The goal of the evaluation is to demonstrate their performance in different sce-
narios, which are detailed and analyzed here.
In this evaluation, the algorithms and proposed techniques processed the training
data traces according to their definition and generated the detection models con-
taining the benign behavior databases. Subsequently, the test traces produced
with the mixed loads of benign interactions and attack injections are processed
and classified. The classifications were analyzed with the epoch-based approach
and the measurements were collected to compute the mentioned metrics.
For the analysis of the results, we first provide an overview of all evaluated mech-
anisms in order to gain general insights. We present more detailed results, focus-
ing on the configurations that yielded higher effectiveness. We perform a thor-
ough analysis of the impact of different values of α for the proposed techniques.

Chapter 6
In addition, we analyze the behavioral patterns stored by the detection mod-
els using the different techniques and compare them to the baseline algorithms.
Given the large number of configurations tested, we focus our analysis on a sub-
set of the results obtained, focusing on a configuration that includes a window
size of 3, an epoch size of 500, and a detection threshold of 50.
6.7.1 Effectiveness Overview
To get an overview of the effectiveness of the proposed techniques and algo-
rithms, we analyze the results produced by each mechanism using the same mi-
croservice release during training and testing. This configuration serves as the
base case for our study. A mechanism that does not provide an acceptable level
of effectiveness in this case does not qualify for deeper analysis (i.e., training on
one release and testing on a future release). Figure 6.6 shows the results for all
metrics: recall, precision (prec.), F-measure (F-M), and False Positive Rate (FPR).
An obvious observation from the chart is that the second variant of the pre-
processing technique ( qu) clearly produces extensive False Positives (FPs) and
causes the detection models to significantly underperform compared to the base
algorithms and other techniques. This technique (qu) produces a high number of
FPs, with the lowest False Positive Rate (FPR) value being0.873. This fact implies
that the second variant of the pre-processing technique (qu) is not suitable for the
base case, which logically leads to very low effectiveness in other microservice
versions as well.
Conversely, the post-processing and the first-variant pre-processing ( f t) tech-
niques show positive results, with high precision ( >=0.779) and recall values
(>=0.666). These mechanisms consistently show increased effectiveness across
the six microservice version base cases, providing a good starting point for ef-
fective application on different microservice releases. The STIDE and BoSC algo-
rithms also show very good effectiveness, with precision at least0.779 and recall
always >=0.666.
The second variant of the pre-processing technique ( qu) proves to be ineffective
in producing usable profiles, as the results obtained show extremely high FPR
and low precision. Therefore, this technique ( qu) will not be further considered
during the analysis performed.
6.7.2 Proposed Techniques Configuration Analysis
To understand the impact of theα value on the performance of the proposed tech-
niques, we examined the results for its different configurations. Figure 6.7 shows
the metrics obtained for a scenario where the release version of the microservice
is the same during training and testing. For this analysis, we used the release
with TeaStore 1.2.0 and Tomcat 7.0.57. Both techniques were tested with six val-
ues for α: 0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8, 0.9, and 0.95. We will focus on the modifications of
BoSC, with STIDE following a similar pattern.

Figure 6.7: Impacts of techniques’ configuration on detection effectiveness.
The results show that the configuration of the techniques is important for the
detection effectiveness. For the f t technique, the results with α =0.5 are particu-
larly poor, with very low precision and a slight FPR. The results start to improve
with α =0.6 and peak with α >=0.7. In the case of the post-processing tech-
nique (p), the results are very unsatisfactory with α∈ [0.5, 0.6, 0.7 ], with the
results only beginning to improve and peaking at α >=0.8.
The findings of this analysis are taken into account for the following analysis
perspectives. We will focus on theα values of 0.6 for f t and 0.8 for p techniques.
6.7.3 Impacts of Proposed Techniques on Detection Models
To have a clear idea of the impact of the techniques on the detection models
produced, we analyzed the patterns in the behavior database after training was
complete. We performed this comparison using Venn diagrams, which provide
a visual representation of the patterns present in each model and their overlap.
For this analysis, we considered the configurations with better results. Figure 6.8
shows the diagrams obtained for versions v1.3.8 of TeaStore and versions v7.0.57
and v8.5.0 of Tomcat.
A clear observation from the diagrams is that p_stide and p_bosc produce sub-
sets of the remaining detection models. The post-processing technique ( p) sig-
Figure 6.8: Overview of proposed techniques impacts on detection models
patterns. TeaStore (v1.3.8) and Tomcat version diagram label.
119

Chapter 6
nificantly reduces the model. However, they maintain the level of effectiveness,
which means that the majority of patterns contributing to the results are a very
small set. Also, f t_stide and f t_bosc learn most of the patterns that stide and bosc
acquire during training.
In fact, there are some new patterns that can help improve the results, and others
that are left out of the final model. The reason for this is that the pre-processing
technique only considers the set of core system calls during training, and when
others are ignored, some new patterns emerge. This can be a valuable approach
to obtain some patterns that are not stored by simple algorithms.
6.7.4 Reuse for Different Microservice Versions
To investigate the ability of the algorithms and techniques to reuse models from
previous releases, a series of experiments were conducted. Detection models
were trained using one release of the microservice and then tested against other
releases. Four releases of the microservice were used for training, including two
TeaStore versions (v1.2.0; v1.3.8) and two Tomcat versions (v7.0.57; v8.5.0). The
models generated from these versions were tested in six versions of the microser-
vice, the four from the training and two from the combination of the two TeaStore
versions and Tomcat v9.0.0. In terms of configurations, the post-processing tech-
nique (p) uses α = 0.8 and the pre-processing technique (f t) uses α = 0.6.

Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
The results obtained are presented in Table 6.5. Each column in the table corre-
sponds to the microservice releases used during testing, while each block row
corresponds to the microservice releases used during training, providing results
for each algorithm and technique. There are several data points in the table, so
we highlight those that provide more relevant information and report several key
findings that emerged from the analysis.
Models trained on releases with Tomcat v7.0.57 are effective at detection on that
release, but perform poorly on other releases. This discrepancy is due to the
significant changes introduced in later Tomcat versions, which significantly alter
the behavior of the application. This can be seen in the Section 6.7.3, where the
total number of patterns in Tomcat v8.5.0 is much higher than in v7.0.57.
STIDE/BoSC models perform with high effectiveness (recall >=0.655; precision
>=0.925) when trained and tested on the same release. Even when tested on other
versions, STIDE/BoSC can deliver significant effectiveness, for example when
trained on Tomcat v8.5.0 and tested on Tomcat v9.0.0 (recall >=0.728; precision
>=0.967), using TeaStore v1.3.8 in both cases.
For the pre-processing technique ( f t) there are mixed results. While it slightly
improves effectiveness in cases such as when trained and tested on Tomcat 7.0.57
releases, it also reduces effectiveness on Tomcat 8.5.0 releases. However, when
trained on Tomcat v8.5.0 and tested on Tomcat v9.0.0 (recall >=0.744; precision
>=0.813), the detection rate improves, but at the expense of precision.
As a key takeaway, it is evident that the post-processing technique ( p) produces
models with results very close to the original models (i.e., STIDE/BoSC). The
pre-processing technique ( f t), on the other hand, requires more careful tuning,
but can provide better results in certain scenarios.
6.8 Results for Model Reusability Index
This section presents and analyzes the results obtained from the validation and
evaluation of the application of MRI. The goal of the evaluation is to demonstrate
the use of the metric by analyzing the source code of the application, system
call data was collected during the test runs, and after calculating the metric, we
trained STIDE and BoSC with windows of size 3 and 4 to compare the insights
from the metric with actual detection results.
To compute the release metric, we processed the application source code and col-
lected runtime data. The source code of the releases was processed by JPlag3 and
the similarity metric was obtained. We then applied the Tversky index 4 using a
small Python script with the package py_stringmatching5, which contains imple-
mentations of several similarity metrics.
3JPlag is a system that finds similarities among multiple sets of source code files. It applies
similarity algorithms and is open-source and available on GitHub (github.com/jplag/JPlag).

The results (see Table 6.6) obtained by JPlag show that the changes in the source
code are minor. The r_12_* and r_13_* releases differ only slightly, with the
similarity decreasing from 1.000 to 0.994.
For the runtime system call alphabets, we have more variation. The largest differ-
ence between releases is betweenr_12_7 and r_12_8, where the alphabet Tversky
index is 0.936. For the same case, the algorithm patterns Tversky index varies
between 0.582 and 0.696. This is reflected in the MRI metric, which results in a
range between 0.712 and 0.786.
Another analysis case is r_13_7 and r_13_8. Here we have no difference in the
source code, because the TeaStore version is the same. The alphabet Tversky
index is 0.952 and the algorithm patterns Tversky index is between 0.310 and
0.456. The MRI metric reflects this with values between 0.540 and 0.634.

6.8.2 Detection Results in Reuse Scenarios
After computing the MRI metric values, we can observe the detection results for
the models of both algorithms. We trained the algorithms with benign data col-
lected at runtime and injected the attacks against the vulnerabilities presented in
Table 6.7 (a subset of the ones in 6.1) to collect detection metrics such as recall,
precision (prec), F-Measure (F-M), and FPR [Antunes and Vieira, 2015a].
Table 6.7: Subset of vulnerabilities used in MRI experiments.
CVE ID Vulnerability Type(s) CVSS Score
2016-6816 Cross Site Scripting, Gain Information 6.8
2019-5736 Code Execution 9.3
2016-5195 Privilege Escalation 7.2
2017-16939 DoS, Privilege Escalation 7.2
2017-7308 DoS, Overflow, Privilege Escalation 7.2
The results (see Table 6.6) show that the detection of the attacks is performed with
relatively high effectiveness for base cases, i.e., where the releases are the same
during training and detection. For these cases, the recall values are above 0.706
and the F-Measure is not inferior to 0.828.
For the cases where the releases are different (reuse scenarios), the effective-
ness is lower, although the recall values are generally higher >=0.714, but the
F-Measure values are much worse <=0.022, as the false positive rates are much
higher >=0.668. We can observe slight variations in the MRI metric values for
different algorithm configurations. These variations are reflected in the FPR ob-
tained by the models, indicating that the RI component is able to provide an in-
dication of the reuse probability present in MRI.
The MRI values seem to indicate that models with a score above 0.8 are suitable
for reuse, as is the case for scenarios r_12_7 and r_13_7, where the detection
results maintain high values, in contrast to what is observed in the remaining
scenarios.
6.9 Threats to Validity
The experiments conducted to validate, evaluate, and compare the proposed
techniques and metrics allowed us to gain important insights into improving the
effectiveness of intrusion detection in scalable, elastic, and continuously changing
microservice environments. The use of our techniques along with state-of-the-art
intrusion detection algorithms shows promising results for increasing the secu-
rity of complex and dynamic microservice applications. However, it is worth
mentioning that some validity threats were identified during the experiments
conducted.
In terms of internal validity, our experiments were carefully designed and con-
ducted in a controlled laboratory environment. We ensured that no external fac-
123

Chapter 6
tors could influence the results. However, it is important to acknowledge the
presence of certain threats to external validity.
Single testbed used for data collection. Our experiments were conducted exclu-
sively with the TeaStore application. However, it is worth noting that TeaStore is
widely used and is considered a representative testbed in the literature. To main-
tain the validity of our results while minimizing variability, we conducted tests
on a significant number of configurations and repeated each experiment thrice.
Reduced set of microservice releases and configuration diversity. The number
of versions of the web server and TeaStore can be small. We present only three
different versions of Tomcat and two versions of the application. However, the
combination of both sets of versions results in six different service versions, which
is large enough for the exploratory study presented here.
Reduced variety of workloads. The workloads could have been further diver-
sified to challenge the classifiers with a wider range of non-malicious behavior,
such as operator commands. Nevertheless, they included different users and dif-
ferent behaviors with different impacts on the system, reflecting different opera-
tions performed by the application.
Majority of attacks target Kernel vulnerabilities. The selection of attacks used
in our experiments may appear to be biased towards kernel-level vulnerabilities.
However, it is important to emphasize that our selection considers a wide range
of real-world vulnerabilities, representing different known attacks targeting dif-
ferent classes of vulnerabilities.
6.10 Conclusion
In this section, we summarize the contributions and lessons learned from pro-
posed techniques and metrics. First, we propose data processing approaches for
intrusion detection in scalable and elastic microservices. Second, we propose
three techniques to obtain core information of microservices to allow the reuse
of detection models. Finally, we propose MRI: a model reusability index metric
that leverages source code, runtime data, and algorithm data to provide detection
models reuse potential.
Data processing approaches deliver improved detection effectiveness in scal-
able microservice environments. The combination of algorithms with our data
processing approaches allows us to create generalized profiles for sets of service
replicas that can be effectively applied in different scenarios, as demonstrated in
our evaluation. Their integration into a DevSecOps pipeline would ensure run-
time protection in an effective and timely manner. Our detection models achieve
improved performance, outperforming their usage without techniques and suc-
cessfully detecting most attacks across different deployments.
Extend the reusability of detection models by using techniques that capture
core information about the behavior of microservices. Capturing the core of
the detection model was well achieved, which allows reducing the number of
124

Improved Components for Microservices Intrusion Detection
stored patterns while ensuring high detection rates without compromising pre-
cision results. The evaluation results show that our techniques can capture the
core behavior of microservices, with an 80% reduction in patterns used to detect
anomalies, while maintaining similar detection effectiveness. The results also
show that model reuse is possible without compromising detection effectiveness.
Model Reusability Index (MRI) provides up-front insight into the reusability
of detection models. This metric can be used to support decisions about whether
to reuse older detection models when new microservice releases occur or when
retraining should take place. It can also be used in evaluation procedures with
the appropriate workload to assess whether an algorithm is suitable for produc-
ing detection models that are more likely to be reusable. The metric also repre-
sents the impact of changes in source code, system call alphabets, and algorithm
patterns across releases.
Over the course of the work presented in this chapter we accumulated significant
understanding of the application of intrusion detection to microservices. The
improvement of the security level of microservice applications through the use
of intrusion detection is quite possible and applicable, even more so with the
techniques we proposed and described in this chapter.
Nevertheless, the research and development work of novel techniques is lim-
ited by a major shortcoming that is the public availability of frameworks for the
evaluation and comparison of different techniques. The work conducted by re-
searchers that propose different algorithms or pipelines to detect intrusions in
microservices cannot be easily compared as a benchmarking approach and an
updated intrusion dataset are not available. Chapter 7 focuses on addressing this
issue, we propose a benchmarking approach and a dataset generation methodol-
ogy, which is applied to generate a 900-hour publicly available dataset.
125

CHAPTER 7
Evaluating Intrusion Detection for Microservice Applications
Microservices are prevalent in cloud-based applications that serve millions of
customers every day, typically running mission-critical systems in software con-
tainers and multi-tenant environments, making it critical to secure these systems.
Intrusion detection is a widely used technique that is now being used in microser-
vices to build behavior detection models and report possible attacks at runtime.
However, it is difficult to evaluate and compare the effectiveness of different ap-
proaches. Standardized frameworks do not exist, and without a fair comparison
of new techniques to the state-of-the-art, it is difficult to understand their pros
and cons.
This chapter presents a comprehensive approach to evaluating and comparing
different intrusion detection approaches for microservice applications. A bench-
marking methodology is proposed to allow users to standardize the process for
a representative and reproducible evaluation. We also present a dataset that uses
representative workloads and technologies based on state-of-the-art microservice
applications. The benchmark and dataset are used in three case studies charac-
terized by dynamism, scalability, and continuous deployment to evaluate and
compare state-of-the-art algorithms for intrusion detection in microservices. Ex-
periments demonstrate the usefulness and broad applicability of the benchmark,
while showing the capacity of intrusion detection algorithms in different appli-
cations and deployments.
This chapter covers the benchmarking approach proposed for microservices (Sec-
tion 7.1), the dataset generation methodology (Section 7.2), the instantiation of the
benchmark to evaluate and compare intrusion detection in microservices (Sec-
tion 7.3), a 900-hour dataset for intrusion detection (Section 7.4), and the results
from the application of the benchmark (Section 7.6). It closes with threats to va-
lidity (Section 7.7) and a conclusion (Section 7.8).
127

Chapter 7
7.1 Benchmarking Approach for Intrusion Detection
in Microservice Applications
This section proposes a benchmarking approach that follows a specification-
based style, describing the functions to be achieved by the detection models, the
required inputs (workload), and the outcomes [v. Kistowski et al., 2015; Nunes
et al., 2018]. The implementation of the benchmark uses measurement-based
techniques, in which the user exercises the tools under benchmarking using to
identify security intrusions in a dataset of host data with mixed data, benign and
malicious, to obtain a small set of measures that represent the intrusion detection
capabilities of the tools.
The objective is to evaluate and compare a set of Intrusion Detection Systems
(IDSs) on a representative dataset (workload), containing benign and malicious
data, and verify their effectiveness in detecting the attacks; then, rank them using
a set of metrics to understand which are more applicable to each scenario.
The general architecture of the proposed benchmark for microservice intrusion
detection models is depicted in Figure 7.1. It consists of four components, which
are introduced here and described in the following subsections.
Procedure
Complexity
Scenarios
Risk Tolerance
Scenarios
Workload
(Dataset)
Algorithms
Algorithms
Ranking
Metrics
Figure 7.1: General Architecture of the Benchmark.
The benchmarking approach requires a representative workload (dataset) to present
representative results, which should be applied in a group of different complexity
scenarios to cover the real operation of the target system. The approach makes use of
well-known and accepted metrics that are used to rank the detection models according
to different tolerance scenarios, following the defined procedure and rules that ensure
standardized evaluation and comparison.
(1) Workload (Dataset): data required to train and test the detection models.
It should be representative of the normal environment and operation of the
target system and contain malicious data that is representative of the mali-
cious attacks that the system may face.
(2) Scenarios: represent the different levels of risk tolerance for the system to
which the detection models are to be applied. The risk tolerance is lower
128

Evaluating Intrusion Detection for Microservice Applications
for a system with a higher probability of attack and higher criticality, thus
requiring the detection models to provide higher detection rates.
(3) Metrics: an easy-to-understand and meaningful way to evaluate and com-
pare the IDSs. The metrics define the effectiveness of the detection models
in identifying malicious data in the workload.
(4) Procedure: rules to be followed when running the benchmark. It consists
of the steps to be taken to prepare for the execution of the benchmark and
the process to be followed during its execution. If followed, the benchmark
will produce the measurements and the ranking for the desired scenarios in
a reproducible and representative manner.
7.1.1 Workload
The workload used to benchmark detection models is critical to the tests per-
formed and the results obtained. The quality of the information and its repre-
sentativeness will strongly influence the validity of the results. In the case pre-
sented, the dataset defines the events processed by the algorithms/techniques
used to build the detection models. To increase the usefulness of the results, the
workload should be as close as possible to the real deployment.
To ensure a valid picture of microservice environments and their typical behavior,
the dataset should include the following information:
– Data from a stable deployment, which represents scenarios where the ser-
vices are not changed and remain with the same configuration.
– Data from a dynamic deployment where dynamicity is present. The scaling
events take place to accommodate modifications in user demand.
– Data from a continuous deployment that is characterized by the continuous
delivery of new versions of the software.
There are different types of workloads that can be used in a benchmark: real,
realistic, and synthetic [Gray, 1993; Antunes and Vieira, 2015a].
– Real workloads are collected while real systems are running in production
environments. These workloads tend to be more representative, but are
difficult to obtain because it would mean having a live system to monitor
and information about malicious events taking place within it.
– Realistic workloads are artificial workloads based on real systems, vulnerabil-
ities, and attacks in the domain of the benchmark. When the methodology
for generating realistic workloads is well-defined, the results obtained en-
sure representativeness of the real system, and even have the advantage of
depicting some edge cases that are rare in real systems but may be impor-
tant in highly critical scenarios. A common approach to generating realistic
datasets is attack injection [Milenkoski et al., 2015b; Flora et al., 2020].
– Synthetic workloads are generated according to a set of guidelines and
premises that facilitate data collection but raise concerns regarding
representativeness.
129

Chapter 7
7.1.2 Scenarios
For our security benchmark, a scenario is based on the level of risk tolerance that
an organization is willing to accept in pursuit of the system’s objective [Dempsey
et al., 2011]. In this way, this approach considers not only the criticality of the
system, but also the likelihood of attacks and their business impact. In the case
of an air traffic control system (critical scenario), the detection models must de-
tect as many possible intrusions as possible, even if some are misclassified at a
tolerable level. Conversely, at the other end of the criticality scale, a public bul-
letin board (non-critical scenario) is concerned with minimizing the number of
false positives, since they are costly to investigate, and resources are scarce. This
benchmark takes into account three levels of risk tolerance, established as realistic
scenarios and defined based on previous research [Antunes and Vieira, 2015b]:
• Low-tolerance: the system/organization has a low risk tolerance, so it re-
quires security intrusions to be detected at the highest rate.
• Moderate-tolerance: security intrusions may go undetected at the expense
of reducing false positives. The system’s tolerance level allows for some
risk.
• High-tolerance: it is essential that the detection model minimize false pos-
itives as much as possible. The objective is to identify security problems
without wasting resources.
In short, the acceptable level of false positives and false negatives is defined by
the risk tolerance of the system/organization. At lower tolerance levels, the ac-
ceptable level of false positives is higher than at higher tolerance levels, while at
higher risk tolerance levels the main goal is to reduce false positives even at the
expense of reporting intrusions.
7.1.3 Metrics
To compare and rank intrusion detection models, we use appropriate metrics
commonly used in intrusion detection evaluation [Milenkoski et al., 2015b; Flora
et al., 2020]. For classification problems like this, the events that are correctly
classified by the detection models are known as True Positive (TP) (for intru-
sions) and True Negative (TN) (for non-intrusions). The intrusion events that are
misclassified as non-intrusions are called False Negative (FN), while the symmet-
ric misclassification is called False Positive (FP). For more details on metrics and
evaluation of security measures, see Section 2.6.3.
Table 7.1: Metrics by Risk Tolerance Level.

For each risk tolerance scenario, we select a primary metric to rank the IDSs and a
secondary metric to act as a tiebreaker (see Table 7.1). These metrics are adapted
from other work [Antunes and Vieira, 2015a]. The choice of metrics depends on
the tolerance level that the system can withstand, which is closely related to the
criticality of the system and the resources available to ensure its security.
In scenarios where the risk tolerance is low, we choose the recall metric. This
metric aims to identify the maximum number of intrusions at any cost, even if it
means accepting some false positives. In the event of a tie, we turn to precision to
prioritize the detection model that produces fewer false positives. These evalua-
tion metrics, which have different focuses, effectively represent the performance
of detection models.
• Model Reusability Index (MRI): evaluate whether a detection models is more
likely to be reused for different releases of a microservice [Flora and Antunes,
2024c].

The Model Reusability Index (MRI) metric is a special case. The formal definition
of the metric has been originally published in a conference paper [Flora and An-
tunes, 2024c], and is detailed in Section 8.3.2. This metric is intended to be used
optionally for cases where the selected workload (dataset) contains information
on different releases of a microservice. The metric requires information about the
source code of the different releases and core data regarding the events used for
training and testing of the detection models. Originally applied using system call
data, it is not included in the scenarios defined in Table 7.1. Rather it can be used
as an optional configuration by the benchmark user.

7.1.4 Procedure
The execution of the benchmark follows a simple procedure consisting of four
high-level steps, as shown in Figure 7.2, and described next.

Figure 7.2: Benchmark Procedure Diagram.
(1) Preparation: comprises the selection of the set of algorithms/techniques to
be benchmarked. The algorithms or techniques may allow for different con-
figurations, and thus it is possible to benchmark the same one with different
parameters to tune it and obtain the most effective.
(2) Execution: consists of training and testing the detection models on the
workload according to the selected scenarios and measures. This step is the
one that allows to compare the performance of the algorithms/techniques.
Details below.
(3) Classification Normalization: normalization of the results produced by the
algorithms/techniques. Details below.
(4) Ranking: ranks the detection models used in the evaluation according to
the benchmark scenarios and metrics used.
Execution
The execution step of the procedure must prepare the workload to be processed by
the algorithms and techniques to be benchmarked. The training dataset should
be cleaned and prepared to fit the operation of the algorithm, for example, some
Machine Learning (ML) algorithms require the data be organized into feature
vectors, so a data transformation step is required.
Once the workload has been prepared to meet the requirements of the algorithms,
training takes place. Typical ML pipelines include parameter tuning to achieve
a stable and usable model, for which a validation dataset should be used. For
other algorithms that do not follow an ML approach, but require a training step
and process the information to obtain patterns of behavior from the data, we
propose a tuning mechanism that allows to identify whether the detection model
has reached a stable and usable state, or steady-state.
132

Evaluating Intrusion Detection for Microservice Applications
For this, we use the formula 0 ⩽
Sts2−Sts1
ts2−ts1
⩽ σ [Milenkoski et al., 2015a] to de-
cide whether an interval is at learning steady-state. A detection model reaches
steady state when this inequality is satisfied five consecutive times for σ = 0.15,
based on previous work detailed in Section 5.1.1. This is a simple but effective
approach that allows benchmark users to identify when detection models reach
steady state.
Classification Normalization
Different detection models can produce different types of outputs. According to
the output they produce, they can be classified as soft or crisp detectors [Islam
et al., 2018a]. Models that produce a score (e.g., intrusion probability) instead
of a decision (i.e., anomalous or normal) are called soft detectors. Crisp detectors
produce a decision. Soft detectors can be converted to crisp detectors by applying
a threshold on the output score [Islam et al., 2018a].
To compare the detection models, we need to ensure that they produce the same
type of output, which is a final decision. This can be either a binary decision
(i.e., anomalous or normal) or a class decision (e.g., normal, user2root, etc.). For
a class-based decision to be comparable, all detection models should be able to
produce the same set of classes. If some detection models produce a binary de-
cision and others do not, the ones that produce classes can be converted to an
“anomalous” decision by generalizing the attack classes. To convert soft detec-
tors into crisp binary detectors, the benchmark user must define a threshold that
separates anomalous from normal decisions [Islam et al., 2018a].
Second, detection models may process information differently, and so the output
may refer to different events (e.g., a system call or a window). To allow a fair
comparison between detection models, the classifications should be translated
into meaningful and comparable data points. For this purpose, we propose a
mechanism based on a time interval that can aggregate different outputs of each
model. This implies that detected anomalies are reported for a user-defined time
interval, which allows for fair comparisons.
7.2 Dataset Generation Methodology
This section outlines the methodology designed to generate datasets for evalu-
ating intrusion detection techniques in microservice applications. The approach
consists of two main phases: planning and execution, as illustrated in Figure 7.3,
and explained in the following sections.
7.2.1 Planning Phase (P)
The planning phase encompasses 3 steps to establish the components of the
dataset generation environment.

Environment Definition (P1)
To ensure the representativeness of the dataset, the selected systems and deploy-
ment environment must be representative of real-world applications. In the sys-
tems selection step, applications should resemble actual scenarios, such as e-
commerce or video streaming, exhibiting complexity and rich feature sets for
users to interact with. That is, when a user makes a request the flows to solve
it should have different interactions among the microservices of the testbed. To
facilitate representativeness, the selection should be based on previous research
use, using applications with broad community acceptance.
Next, the deployment and workloads definition phases establish user behavior
profiles and workload intensities for the load generators. User behavior profiles
detail the set of operations that users perform on the application, that is, the set
of requests to which the application responds. This set of requests should apply a
high coverage (e.g., >80%) of the features provided by the application, while hav-
ing a similar distribution to that of real users. The workload intensity describes
the number of active users over the duration of the experiments. The work-
load intensity should be derived using practical application profiling, and then
defined according to the different patterns of performance testing [v. Kistowski
et al., 2014]. A load generator is an automated tool that takes the user behavior
profiles and the workload intensity definition, and conducts the requests to the
application endpoints at the rates defined by the workload intensity. Examples
are JMeter1 and Locust2.
To improve the quality of the dataset, we propose to introduce benign operations
that are neither malicious nor the result of user requests. During the data en-
richment step, we conduct maintenance tasks that provide varied data beyond
standard client interactions. These operations result in unseen behavior from the
system without being malicious. The data produced allows techniques and algo-
rithms to learn more broadly, covering more than the typical response to client
requests. In this way, detectors can prevent false positives resulting from com-
1JMeter is an Apache project for load testing and measuring the performance of applications,
with special emphasis on web applications (jmeter.apache.org)
2Locust is a load testing framework that simulates user interacting with applications based on
scripted endpoint calls (locust.io).
134

Evaluating Intrusion Detection for Microservice Applications
mon system administrator operations. It can also be used to identify whether the
algorithms can generalize the knowledge acquired during training and expand
it to other system administrator operations. The operations should cover typical
administrative actions, such as memory, process, or storage related.
Malicious Actor Behavior (P2)
Simulating realistic attackers involves defining athreat model that outlines attack
points and mechanisms using frameworks like MITRE ATT&CK [Strom et al.,
2018]. The definition of the threat model includes the attack vectors against
the system and how those attacks are executed. For vulnerability selection ,
databases like NVD and CVE Mitre are extremely useful, while attack selection
identifies exploits that target the vulnerabilities. The former is especially impor-
tant when it comes to the vulnerability injection phase of the process, where
vulnerabilities are added to the application. Using known vulnerabilities also
increases the representativeness of the attacks performed. For the attack selec-
tion, Proof of Concept (PoC) code (exploits) is available for download online, in
databases such as exploit-db or in online GitHub repositories, and are recom-
mended to ease the implementation of the attack injection procedure.
Experimental Methodologies (P3)
Datasets include different types of data used by intrusion detection algorithm-
s/techniques. These mechanisms are different in nature and operation, and re-
quire different information during the training and testing phases.
First, it is necessary to define what data should be collected from the environment
in which the application is deployed. Host information (system calls, file system
data), network information (connection flows, traffic), and application data (per-
formance data from load generators) can be collected.
Second, the type of data files that make up the dataset should be defined.
The dataset files can contain benign, purely malicious, or mixed informa-
tion [Milenkoski et al., 2015b]. Benign data includes non-malicious client
interactions, purely malicious data records only attacker operations on the
application, and mixed data combines both. Each file type requires a specific
experimental methodology detailing data collection steps, timing, workload
initiation, and attack injection (if applicable).
There should be three types of experimental methodologies for generating data
files. This increases the usefulness and representativeness of the dataset, as it
allows test data files to be completely independent of the training and validation
used to construct the model, and avoids the typical data splitting that occurs
when training machine learning models [Russell, 2010]. Atraining methodology
that clearly defines how the duration of the data collection procedure and the
steps taken to obtain data are used to train algorithms/techniques. A validation
methodology that can be used as a data source independent of training to allow
evaluation of the model during the training phase, so that it is possible to tune
135

Chapter 7
the parameters [Russell, 2010]. A testing methodology clarifies how to collect the
data used to test the model, with details on injection and workloads.
7.2.2 Execution Phase (E)
Once planning is complete, the framework and environment for data generation
should be developed. A framework reduces human interaction in the generation
of datasets and increases the repeatability of the experiments conducted.
Experiments Preparation (E1)
To prepare the experimental campaigns, it is important to perform asetup prepa-
ration: define the architecture and set up the environment; a scripting automa-
tion (the recipes to deploy the environment and the application) and execute the
framework responsible for controlling the experiments. Infrastructure automa-
tion tools that support multiple systems, such as Ansible 3, can streamline setup.
A common programming language can be used to implement the different meth-
ods for generating the different data files.
Experiments Execution (E2)
All selected configurations (e.g., scenarios, attacks, testbeds) are combined into
produce the execution queue and executed. The experiments are conducted
using the developed framework. The data files are collected according to the
methodologies and should be validated for accuracy and completeness.
7.3 A Benchmark for Host-based Intrusion Detection
in Microservices
The benchmarking approach presented (Section 7.1) aims to be a general ap-
proach that can be applied to different intrusion detection mechanisms, with dif-
ferent targets, and operation modes, that rely on different data types to detect
attacks and intrusions. In this section, we present an instantiation of the pro-
posed benchmark approach for host-based intrusion detection in microservice
applications. The instantiation of the benchmark uses measurement-based tech-
niques where the selected IDSs are executed to detect security intrusions on the
workload (dataset) containing different types of attacks to obtain the measures
and calculate the metrics to rank the tools.
3Ansible is a suite of software tools that enables infrastructure as code (ansible.com).

7.3.1 Workload
As mentioned earlier, the workload used in the benchmark should be represen-
tative of the environments in which the IDSs may operate. To ensure this charac-
teristic, one needs to provide a wide range of applications that are dynamically
exercised to expose the IDSs to different operational profiles and test them for
different possible uses. Since no microservice intrusion detection dataset exists,
we created our own workload for this instantiation of the benchmark. We ap-
plied the dataset generation methodology presented in Section 7.2 to generate
900 hours of recording data, using three different testbeds with multiple config-
urations to obtain data for the three complexity scenarios described. The details
of the datasets produced using host data from microservice applications are pro-
vided in Section 7.4.
7.3.2 Scenarios
When instantiating the benchmark, one should aim for a comprehensive coverage
of the real-world scenarios in which an IDS would operate and try to cover them.
The definition of the complexity scenarios should follow an increasing approach,
starting with the baseline case and ending with the most relevant and challeng-
ing case the IDS may face. For our instantiation, we defined three complexity
scenarios for this instantiation of the benchmark based on its specification. In
the first scenario, the algorithms are trained and applied in the same operational
environment, where the testbeds have the same deployment scenario. The sec-
ond considers a scalability environment where the monitored microservice scales
and has a varying number of active service replicas. The third considers a more
complex scenario where the microservice is continuously deployed across its dif-
ferent releases. This means that as the microservice evolves, new features and
bug fixes are added and its behavior may change. The benchmark for the first
two case studies uses data from all three testbeds, while the third case study uses
data from the TeaStore testbed only.
The three risk tolerance scenarios defined in the benchmark guidelines are ap-
plied in this instantiation as a way to rank the different detection models accord-

Figure 7.5: Benchmark Instantiation Procedure.
ing to the criticality of the system in which they might be applied. Hence, we
adopt the low, moderate, and high risk tolerance scenarios, which are defined
and detailed in Section 7.1.2. The adoption of these risk tolerance scenarios also
defines the ranking metrics for each scenario (presented in Table 7.1).
7.3.3 Procedure
For the procedure of the instantiation of the benchmarking approach, we start by
selecting the algorithms and then define the concrete steps based on the specifi-
cation.
The selection of algorithms depends on the goals of the benchmark user. The al-
gorithms used should be those that are most relevant to the intended goal and
are the final candidates after an initial study and selection phase. For our instan-
tiation, we intend to demonstrate the breadth of the benchmark and therefore se-
lected several algorithms used in previously published work to benchmark based
on the literature review conducted in Section 2.5.3. The selected algorithms have
different approaches and characteristics, demonstrating the range of applicability
of the benchmark. In the evaluation, the use of the Sequence Time-Delaying Em-
bedding (STIDE) and Bags of System Calls (BoSC) algorithms is based on previ-
ously work along with the Local Outlier Factor (LOF), One Class Support Vector
Machines (OCSVM), Isolation Forest (IF), and Hidden Markov Models (HMM).
The algorithms are benchmarked using a segment of the datasets generated in
Section 7.4. To train the detection models, we used the initial 24-hour segment of
each data collection for each testbed, with the validation data used to fine tune
the parameters, and for testing we excluded the data samples that had Common
Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) IDs from 2014.
For the STIDE and BoSC algorithms we followed the approach of previous work
(see Chapter 5), where the sequence of system calls is processed into windows
of configurable size and reports are based on those windows. For the remaining
algorithms, we followed the approach of Araujo et al. [2023], where the sequences
of system calls are converted into feature vectors using ngrams of 50 system calls
for training and testing.
The procedure implemented is detailed in Figure 7.5 and is based on the specifi-
138

Evaluating Intrusion Detection for Microservice Applications
cation proposed in Section 7.1.4. It initiates with the selection, and preparation, of
the training datasets according to the applied complexity scenario. The prepared
training data is fed to the algorithms for the training phase, where the valida-
tion datasets are included to fine tune the algorithms. The convergence analysis
dictates when to stop the training phase and the detection models are ready for
testing. The models are then evaluated using the test datasets, with different con-
figurations tested depending on the complexity scenario. The measures are col-
lected and processed to normalize the output of the different detection models.
Finally, the metrics for the three risk tolerance levels are computed, and the de-
tection models are ranked according to the benchmark guidelines (see Table 7.1).
The experimental results are analyzed in Section 7.6.
7.4 Dataset for Host-based Intrusion Detection in Mi-
croservices
This section demonstrates the application of the outlined methodology (Sec-
tion 7.2), detailing the concrete techniques, artifacts, and experiments carried
out to generate datasets for host-based intrusion detection in microservice-based
applications. A sample of the dataset is available online (see Appendix A).
7.4.1 Planning Phase
The design phase of the experiments involves several steps to determine the
methodology for each type of data that makes up the datasets. These steps are
described below.
Systems and Deployment Definition
To ensure the representativeness of the generated datasets, we analyzed state-
of-the-art open-source and publicly available microservice applications [von
Kistowski et al., 2018; Weaveworks, 2017; Community, 2016; Zhou et al., 2018b;
Gan et al., 2019]. From this pool, we selected TeaStore, SockShop, and the Social-
Network from DeathStarBench based on the number of services, development
technologies, and prior research utilization. TeaStore and SockShop architectures
are presented in Figure 2.2 and Figure 2.3, respectively, while SocialNetwork
architecture is presented in Figure 2.5a.
TeaStore simulates an e-commerce tea store with five services sharing a base im-
age and Java for development [von Kistowski et al., 2018]. During the experi-
ments, we use two versions of TeaStore, v1.3.0 and v1.3.8. SockShop portrays an
e-commerce store selling socks with eight microservices [Weaveworks, 2017]. It
includes diverse programming languages for the services, such as NodeJS, Java,
and Golang; and different databases like MySQL and MongDB. The services in-
teract using REST. For more details on both testbeds see Section 2.2.3.
139

Chapter 7
The Social Network application portrays a social network with unidirectional
follow relationships that is implemented using microservices that communicate
through Thrift RPCs [Weaveworks, 2017]. The application is part of a benchmark
suite of microservice applications used for research purposes [Gan et al., 2019].
Nine scaling scenarios were defined for each system, influencing deployment and
active service replicas. Deployments vary from 7 to 31 containers (active service
replicas) for TeaStore, from 15 to 59 for SockShop, and from 27 and 70 to Social-
Network. Figure 7.2a was taken from the original paper that presented TeaSt-
ore [von Kistowski et al., 2018], with a slight adaptation for scenario #9, where
in our case the WebUI service has seven active replicas, to have a larger sample
of active service replicas. To derive the scenarios for SockShop and SocialNet-
work, we subjected them to different intensity loads and used HPA 4 to observe
the auto-scaling behavior of the application. Figure 7.2b (SockShop) and Fig-
ure 7.2c (SocialNetwork) describe the number of active service replicas for the
nine scenarios.

Workloads and Operator Commands
For representativeness, we adopted recommended workload profiles for each ap-
plication with different intensities. An eight-day training phase (see Figure 7.6)
collected diverse data by applying diverse user behaviors, complemented by be-
nign operator commands for data enrichment. We selected eight days for the

sources to match with demand, according to user-defined rules on observed metrics (e.g.,
CPU) [Kubernetes, 2023].

Figure 7.6: Long series characterization for the training phase. Eight days of

duration of the training data collection as it is extensive enough to have sufficient
time to record a significant amount of data from a system that is continuously
operating in a dynamic environment with several modifications. The user behav-
ior profiles are composed of different requests that reflect the e-commerce and
social network activities referred. The writing operations (e.g., creating a post or
making a purchase) have varying execution rates (10% in general, with days 4
and 7 having 20%). This distribution is consistent with the common behavior of
e-commerce users who browse pages for short periods of time [Zenetti, 2022] and
other of read/write workload experiments. The load intensities included vari-
ous curves to increase variability: constant (e.g., [26, 34[ hours); quadratic (e.g.,
[34, 38[ hours); sinusoidal with different periods (e.g., [16, 18[ and [38, 72[ hours);
cubic (e.g., [80, 85[ hours); and reciprocal (e.g., [96, 100[ hours). Six scaling events
occurred during the 8-day period, incorporating scale-up and scale-down shifts
among four different deployment scenarios.
Benign operator commands, that emulate system administrators’ actions, were
introduced as data enrichment steps. These operations were derived from real-
world scenarios and covered resource usage checks, network verification, and
file handling. Figure 7.7 presents the four sets of data enrichment commands
used in the experiments performed. They were selected after researching several
reference blogs and forums for system administrators [Ran, 2023; Brown, 2022;
DevOpsArticle, 2021].
Threat Model
To have a representative experimental campaign, using attack injection, we de-
fined different layers that could be targeted by a malicious agent in such envi-
ronments, adopting the threat model defined in Section 3.1. The shared infras-
tructure of microservices allows attackers to compromise multiple layers. Targets
include services, the virtualization engine, and the host operating system. An at-
tacker can try to compromise other services (or tenants) to obtain access to it or
endanger its operation. An attacker can target the virtualization engine where the
microservices are running. This could be through attempts at escaping the isola-
tion of its container or gaining access to or control of the engine to manipulate the
other services sharing the infrastructure. Because container-based solutions use
kernel cgroups and namespaces to achieve isolation and data separation, mali-
141

Chapter 7
Figure 7.7: Data Enrichment command sets used.
These sets are representative of common system administrator tasks for system
operation assessment.
cious services may also attempt to target the host operating system. These attacks
allow agents to escalate privileges and gain control of the host machine, allowing
them to perform a range of activities.
The Mitre Att&ck framework informed attacker tactics such as reconnaissance,
code execution, privilege escalation, and lateral movement [Strom et al., 2018].
An attacker applies reconnaissance to gather information, execution of malicious
code to establish a persistent connection, and gain continuous access. It is also
common to perform privilege escalation to increase the level of access in the
system. A common operation in microservices is lateral movement, where after
gaining control of one service, the attacker attempts to move to another related
service. In short, we have a malicious service that shares infrastructure with the
application and attempts three types of attacks against: i) an application service;
ii) the virtualization engine; and iii) the host Operating System (OS).
Vulnerabilities and Attacks
Several real-world vulnerabilities at multiple levels of the stack were collected to
increase the representativeness of the attacks executed. These weaknesses have
been reported by security researchers over the past decade, with CVE IDs as-
signed ranging from 2014 to 2022, an eight-year period. Although the 2014 vul-
nerabilities are a bit old, they are representative of a class of vulnerabilities that
are still being found in current software and exploited in the wild. For exam-
ple, the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency’s (CISA) top 2022 rou-
tinely exploited vulnerabilities include vulnerabilities with CVE IDs from 2018
and 2017 [CISA, 2023].

The vulnerabilities are detailed in Table 7.3, with severity data, with Common
Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, the category assigned based on
the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) categorization system, and the map-
ping to the threats defined in Section 3.1, which allow us to understand the ap-
proach of the attacker. It is possible for a vulnerability to be mapped to multiple
threats in the threat model because it may affect different components or be used
in several ways against the infrastructure. The vulnerabilities presented were se-
lected to ensure the representativeness of the experiments and directly contain
11 CWEs of the top 25 of 2023 [CWE, 2023]. The weaknesses in our dataset also
represent the same CWE class as another 6 CWEs present in the top 25, which
means that our vulnerabilities represent directly and indirectly represent 17 of the
weakness categories in the most dangerous software weaknesses of 2023 [CWE,
2023]. This selection is also based on the tactics and techniques of the Mitre
Att&ck Framework, as described in Section 7.2.1. Table 7.3 presents vulnerabili-
ties covering reconnaissance (CVE-2016-6816), code execution (CVE-2017-12617),
privilege escalation (CVE-2016-5195), obtaining credentials (CVE-2016-1240), and
container breakout (CVE-2019-5736; CVE-2022-0492).
To exploit the presented vulnerabilities, we have collected proof-of-concept code
(i.e., exploits) that demonstrate their presence and activate them. This proof-of-
concept code is publicly available and was collected from different sources, such
as exploit-db5, 0day.today6, and GitHub repositories. After a preliminary anal-
ysis and testing of the found candidates, we selected an exploit for each vulner-
ability and prepared the setup to be used. The services attacked in each testbed
are: TeaStore, WebUI; SockShop, front-end; and SocialNetwork, nginx-thrift.
5ExploitDB is a CVE compliant database that stores and provides public exploits for known

60day.today is a database of vulnerabilities and exploits that makes exploit code available to
security researchers (0day.today).
143

Chapter 7
7.4.2 Execution Phase
To generate the datasets, we devised a methodology with microservice applica-
tions deployed on a Kubernetes cluster and workloads to exercise the target ap-
plication. We collected system call data using µDetector, which uses sysdig [Sys-
dig, Inc, 2019]. The methodology considers a set of 17 different exploits that
target the same number of real-world known vulnerabilities in three layers of
the service attacked through an attack injection procedure. The fact that we use
a version of the service with known vulnerabilities increases the representative-
ness and confidence of the experiments and the results obtained.
Figure 7.8 provides a graphical overview of the methodology depicting the mul-
tiple phases of the experiments performed to collect information from the mi-
croservices, for attack and validation data purposes. It starts with 10 minutes
to launch and start the applications, populating and initiating databases. This is
followed by a 10-minute warm-up period with a light-intensity workload to ex-
ercise the testbed. Data collection lasts for 36 minutes, starting one minute before
the end of the warmup phase and ending five minutes after the main workload,
which runs for 30 minutes. The validation data does not include the attack injec-
tion step depicted in Figure 7.8.
Launch & Startup: 10m Warmup: 10m Workload: 30m
Data Collection: 36m
Total Duration: 60m
Data Enrichment
 Attack Injection
Figure 7.8: Attack data generation methodology followed. Validation data also
follows this approach, but the attack injection step is not performed.
During the experimental campaign, the monitored system is exercised with only
benign workloads to collect training traces. The detection (or test) traces are col-
lected when an attack is injected while the benign workload is running, provid-
ing a mixed data set that can be used to test the detection models. The process is
repeated for multiple deployment configurations of each system. This diverges
from common datasets where the only varying factor is the attack used in each
scenario. The information on the timespan of the attack is collected and stored, so
it is possible to define the ground-truth for each testing dataset, allowing metrics
of algorithm effectiveness to be computed.
Experimental Setup
We used four physical machines: one to serve as the load generator, we used
Locust7, which interacts with the application and making the requests; and three
that composed the cluster, a master and two worker nodes (see Figure 7.9). The
load generator and master node machines had an Intel Core i5 with 32 GB of
RAM running Ubuntu 18.04, while the worker node machines had an Intel Core
i5 with 64 GB of RAM running Ubuntu 16.04.
7Locust is a load testing framework that simulates user interacting with applications based on
scripted endpoint calls (locust.io).

Preparation and Execution of the Campaign
Data collection, attacks, and data enrichment were automated for repeatability.
Deployments were scripted using YAML scripts from public repositories. To ac-
commodate the vulnerabilities elicited (see Table 7.3), we installed Docker ver-
sion 18.06.3, used Tomcat version 7.0.57, NodeJS version 10.1.0, and Kernel 4.4.0
with Ubuntu 16.04. Although these versions are a bit old, the systems are still in
use today and are representative of technologies commonly found in production
software. The information we collect (system calls) has a stable interface across
different versions of this software stack, and its stability ensures that our dataset
does not suffer from reduced representativeness.
7.5 Characterization and Comparison of the Datasets
Each collection experiment produces a set of files. The main file contains sys-
tem calls collected from all microservices, including their replicas, forming the
monitored application. This file has multiple features per line: event timestamp,
container name, thread ID, system call direction (‘ >’ for entry, ‘ <’ for exit), sys-
tem call category (according to sysdig), system call name, and list of arguments.
This data was split into files of up to 100GB each before compression, organized
by worker node. Additional files detail the execution intervals of data enrich-
ment commands (identifying set and container), and attacks, if any. For training
data, timestamps are provided for scaling events and write probability increase
timestamps.
From the client side, different files are collected: a log file of the load genera-
tor operation, exception logs for errors, details of failed requests, chronological
request details with URLs and statistics, and transaction data. These files offer
throughput, response time, percentiles, and performance insight. Data was col-
lected separately for the monitoring and warm-up phases.
Table 7.4 outlines file specifics containing system call data across applications
and data file types. Training datasets consist of 52 files, gathered over eight days
per microservice application, incorporating data enrichment commands, scaling

enrichment commands. This results in mixed-data datasets, consisting of benign
and malicious events, suitable for algorithm evaluation. The test data comprises
3.9TB (230GB compressed) over 372 runs. Benign validation data, similar to the at-
tack methodology but without attacks, is 157GB (12GB compressed) of system call
events from 24 runs. This division of data is a suggestion for the data scientist
to use, but they can still perform different divisions using the training datasets
available. The key reasoning is that in the current separation, the users have
different data enrichment activities performed in training and validation data,
which allows the user to tune their approaches in terms of generalization ability,
before using the test data, which, as recommended in the machine learning best
practices, should never be used for tuning and only for the final results. The val-
ues reported here refer to the current state of the datasets. They are still being
expanded to more scenarios and testbeds as well as other workloads and attacks.
Below we analyze some characteristics of a subset of the datasets in more de-
tail, including the number of system calls collected over the training collection
and their distribution across the categories assigned by sysdig. This analysis is
provided as an example and is not intended to be exhaustive of the complete
datasets. All plots shown are the result of applying a moving average to the raw
data of the datasets.

System Calls Count
The training data collection time is 192 hours per testbed. Figure 7.10 illus-
trates the data collected for the TeaStore deployment node and the two SockShop
nodes. Notably, system call counts align with workload intensity patterns (see
Figure 7.6), illustrating the impact of the training workload on the data collected
from the monitored services. Scale-up events increase the number of system calls,
while scale-down events decrease them. At its peak, the number of system calls
reaches up to 1.2 million, with TeaStore generating more than SockShop.
Figure 7.11 represents the number of system calls generated in each node over the
collection period for the test datasets. We present a sample of the vulnerabilities
used in the infrastructure against which the attacks are injected. For each vul-
nerability chart, we have added the time of attack injection, represented by the
red line. We can observe that for the majority of the sample presented, the sim-
ple analysis of the number of system calls invoked is not enough to identify the
presence of malicious activity. However, one of the attacks (the one that targets
CVE-2016-0728) causes a significant change in the state of the microservice, which
starts to produce ten times more system calls than before the attack. The baseline
for each node remains stable at around 1 million system calls per minute during
the execution of the experiments; other cases not presented in this example may
exhibit behavior similar to CVE-2016-0728.
Figure 7.11: TeaStore and SockShop system calls collected for testing traces.
Categories
Sysdig assigns a category to each system call it collects. To understand the dis-
tribution of the categories assigned to system calls, we analyzed and plotted this
data. TeaStore’s categories (see Figure 7.12) mirror intensity training workload
patterns, with IPC system calls prevailing. Thus, the services from this testbed
frequently perform process coordination and communication tasks. SockShop
(see Figure 7.13) exhibits a significant frequency of the sleep category, while the
file category is the second most frequent category. Excluding the sleep category,
other categories rarely exceed 100,000 system calls per minute.

Learning Phase Comparison
A comparative experiment was conducted using LID-DS and our dataset to deter-
mine the impact of the learning phase. We trained STIDE and BoSC algorithms
on both datasets, using a similar amount of data from each dataset (close to 27
million records from each dataset). STIDE and BoSC use a sliding window ap-
proach over the system call trace to extract and store patterns of system call se-
quences. For this experiment, we used two window sizes: three and four, to
illustrate a possible use of the algorithms. These patterns are used during the
detection phase to identify deviations from baseline behavior. For LID-DS, we
used all the training data files from the Juice Shop scenario. Preliminary analy-
sis indicates that our dataset provided broader learning for the algorithms (see
Figure 7.14). The shorter LID-DS runs (approximately 45 seconds for each file)
resulted in less variable recording behavior, which reduced pattern learning. In
addition, our dataset structure allows for comprehensive testing against all vul-
nerabilities due to consistent target systems across attacks, eliminating the need
for repeated training. For LID-DS, each vulnerability/attack is a different sce-
nario, requiring a new training phase.

7.6 Benchmarking Intrusion Detection Algorithms
for Microservices
The objective of the implementation of the benchmarking approach is to demon-
strate it and validate its process. At the same time, we can evaluate different
intrusion detection algorithms in the context of different microservice environ-
ment scenarios and compare their effectiveness. The benchmarking results and
algorithm ranking are performed in three case studies that focus on different mi-
croservice application operating environments, as described in Section 7.3.
7.6.1 Stable Deployment Case Study
This case study describes the base case of intrusion detection evaluations, where
an algorithm is trained in the same environment in which it is deployed to de-
tect intrusions. That is, the configurations of the environment are the same and
it does not face modifications when trying to identify malicious actions. In this
case study, the profiled microservice version (on whose data the detection mod-
els are trained) is the same as the one used to evaluate the algorithms. We use
deployment scenario #1, which considers only one service replica in operation.
The results are shown on Table 7.5. We can observe that, across the three testbeds,
the ML algorithms (HMM, IF, OCSVM, and LOF) generally perform better for the
low risk tolerance scenario. These detection models detect the attacks performed
with higher frequency, but sometimes at the expense of precision. They generally
perform worse than STIDE/BoSC for moderate and high tolerance scenarios, ex-
cept in the case of the SocialNetwork testbed, where the ML algorithms still out-
perform STIDE/BoSC. This may be due to the fact that the SocialNetwork service
produces less data that needs to be processed by the algorithms, and therefore the
ML algorithms may be able to produce clearer detection models for this case. In
general, all detection models perform better with less data to process, which may
indicate that data processing techniques would help to obtain better profiles.
The results also allow us to evaluate the performance of different configurations
of the same algorithms. The performance of BoSC decreases as the window size

7.6.2 Scalability Case Study
This case study describes a dynamic case of intrusion detection evaluation, where
a model is trained in a deployment configuration that is different from the config-
uration in which it is used to detect intrusions. That is, the configurations of the
environment are dynamic due to scalability and elasticity. The detection model is
subject to changes as it attempts to identify malicious behavior. In this case study,
the profiled microservice remains the same, in terms of behavior, but there may
be a varying number of replicas to increase the capacity to respond to user re-
quests. During testing, the deployment scenarios for each testbed are #3, #5, and
#7, which consider different numbers of replicas for the profiled microservice.
A general overview of the results obtained in this case study are presented on Ta-
ble 7.6. Here, we can observe similar trends to the stable deployment case study.
The ML algorithms tend to rank better in the low risk tolerance scenarios but
are outperformed by STIDE/BoSC in the moderate and high tolerance scenarios.
Still, for SocialNetwork, the top ranking detection models still come from ML
algorithms. OCSVM and LOF rank better for the three risk tolerance scenarios
in this testbed. For SockShop and TeaStore, STIDE/BoSC perform better in the
moderate and high risk tolerance scenarios, notably with smaller window sizes.

A detailed analysis of the SocialNetwork testbed results is presented in Table 7.7.
The results obtained are detailed across each testing deployment configuration.
This makes it possible to understand how the detection models perform with a
higher or lower number of service active replicas. The general trend remains,
and ML algorithms generally perform better across the three risk tolerance sce-
narios. However, it is possible to observe that the HMM detection models out-
perform the others as the number of active service replicas increases. OCSVM
is the most impacted, and its performance decreases as the number of replicas
increases. From the top of the ranking in three risk tolerance scenarios for de-
ployment #3, it drops to third and fourth for deployment #7. The STIDE/BoSC
algorithms are also impacted by the increase in active replicas, with lower preci-
sion and a slight decrease in recall.
7.6.3 Continuous Delivery Case Study
This case study focuses on a Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery
(CI/CD) environment where a detection model is trained on an older version of
a microservice and used to detect intrusions in newer versions. This means that
the codebase of the microservice changes during the lifetime of the model. As the
microservice changes, so does its behavior, and detection models must be able
to handle such changes. For training, we use data from the version v1.3.0 of
TeaStore. During testing, we use two versions of the service: v1.3.0 and v1.3.8;
both in deployment scenario #1.

For this case study, we compute value of the MRI metric (described in Sec-

Table 7.8 presents the results of the MRI calculation. This metric allows users
to gain insight into the reusability of intrusion detection models. It provides
the likelihood that detection models will be reused in different versions of the
microservice. According to the results obtained, the models trained on v1.3.0
that are more likely to be reused in v1.3.8 are those produced by STIDE/BoSC,
