presented in Table 7.9. After calculating the metrics, we calculated the variation
of each metric for each detection model. Based on the metric variation, we create
the ranking for each risk tolerance scenario, and the average ranking position for
each detection model to compare it with the MRI ranking position (see Table 7.9).
This allows us to validate the MRI results since the metric does not take into

7.6.4 Properties Discussion
Here, we discuss how the instantiation of the benchmarking approach demon-
strates the benchmark and workload properties defined in Section 2.6.1.
The instantiation and successful use of the benchmarking approach demonstrates
its relevance. There is currently no benchmark that addresses the challenges of
microservices for intrusion detection. This benchmark allows its users to evaluate
and compare how different IDSs perform when faced with dynamic microservice
environments. It also provides evidence of the benchmark’sportability, which was
shown during the experimentation, as it can be easily applied to different types of
algorithms and configurations, from ML algorithms to statistical approaches and
pattern-based techniques, which use different measurement units and techniques
to process the data.
The benchmark uses well-known state-of-the-art metrics, whose results are easy
153

Chapter 7
to interpret and analyze. This guarantees thesimplicity of the benchmark and con-
tributes to the credibility of the results obtained. In addition, the execution of the
benchmark is simple and economical, it has only two time-consuming steps: train-
ing the models, only once per configuration, and testing them in each scenario
to obtain the measures and compute the results. Multiple runs of the benchmark
will yield statistically similar results because the workload has been collected and
is not changed between runs, which contributes to making it a repeatable and re-
producible benchmark.
The different case studies also validate the properties of the workload (dataset).
The workload is composed of three testbed applications with state-of-the-art tech-
nologies and operating modes that follow the architectural pattern under focus
to ensure its representativeness, relevance, broad coverage, and comprehensiveness. It
also provides labeled attacks to compare the IDSs results with aground truth, and
calculate the measures. As demonstrated by the different case studies, the work-
load is configurable, with multiple scenarios, and scalable, with different levels of
complexity (e.g., scalability and continuous integration) that the user can choose.
7.7 Threats to Validity
The experiments conducted to demonstrate the benchmarking approach demon-
strate its flexibility, applicability, and usefulness for evaluating and comparing
multiple intrusion detection algorithms for microservices. The results show that
ML algorithms generally perform better on testbeds that produce less data to pro-
cess. The benchmark instantiation benefited from a 900-hour dataset generated
using state-of-the-art microservice technology. Still, it is worth mentioning that
some validity threats were identified during the execution of the experiments.
In terms of internal validity, our experiments were carefully designed and con-
ducted in a controlled laboratory environment. We ensured that no external fac-
tors could influence the results. However, it is important to acknowledge the
presence of certain threats to external validity.
The effectiveness of detection models depends on the configurations selected.
The results of the benchmark are valid for the configurations selected during the
experiments. The lessons learned from the evaluation allow us to have a general
indication of the detection effectiveness of the detection models produced, but
there may be slight variations with different algorithm configurations. The use
of either approach to intrusion detection must be carefully studied, evaluated,
and tested for the scenarios of application, and multiple configuration parameters
should be used to ensure a broad and representative analysis of effectiveness.
Reduced number of intrusion detection algorithms. In the benchmark instanti-
ation, we evaluated and compared six different intrusion detection approaches.
This number is reduced in order to provide a broad and complete analysis of the
state of the art in intrusion detection techniques and algorithms. However, the
main purpose of the evaluation was to demonstrate the applicability and scope
of the benchmarking approach. We are confident that the experimental campaign
154

Evaluating Intrusion Detection for Microservice Applications
could be expanded to include more algorithms and more complex approaches to
provide a more comprehensive understanding of the current state of practice.
7.8 Conclusion
In this section, we summarize the contributions and lessons learned from the
benchmarking approach and dataset generation methodologies proposed as well
as from their application to generate a 900-hour dataset and the evaluation and
comparison of six state-of-the-art intrusion detection algorithms.
A benchmarking approach for representative and meaningful evaluation of
microservice IDSs. We propose a general benchmarking approach for represen-
tative and meaningful evaluation of microservice IDSs in diverse environments,
considering three complexity scenarios, three risk tolerance scenarios, and using
multiple evaluation metrics. The approach specifies how to implement a bench-
mark for evaluating and comparing intrusion detection systems based on i) rep-
resentative workloads for different complexity scenarios, and ii) specific metrics
for different risk tolerance levels.
A methodology for generating representative microservice intrusion detection
datasets. We propose a general methodology for generating datasets in the con-
text of microservices that applies the characteristics of these environments and
allows developers to overcome guidelines limitations and generate representa-
tive and useful data. The methodology considers representative workloads based
on state-of-the-art technologies for microservice applications and environments,
with a wide range of representative application testbeds, dynamic scenarios with
scalable behaviors, and common attacks in such environments.
An instantiation of the benchmark evaluating six different IDSs in three case
studies. We present an instantiation of the proposed benchmark approach for
host-based intrusion detection in microservice applications. The instantiation of
the benchmark uses measurement-based techniques where the selected IDSs are
executed to detect security intrusions on the workload (dataset) containing dif-
ferent types of attacks to obtain the measures and calculate the metrics to rank
the tools.
The benchmark results indicate ML algorithms perform better with less data.
The results show that ML algorithms generally perform better on testbeds that
produce less data to process, while STIDE/BoSC can handle more information,
process it effectively, and still achieve a high detection rate. The detection models
produced by STIDE/BoSC are easier to reuse across microservice releases, but
need to be improved to ensure satisfactory detection rates.
900-hour microservice intrusion detection dataset to train, validate, and test
IDSs. We generate representative datasets with 900 hours of recorded data to
overcome the limitations of currently available datasets that do not have the con-
cerns and characteristics of microservices in their genesis. The dataset was gen-
erated in microservice applications using state-of-the-art technologies with three
155

Chapter 7
different microservice applications collecting three different types of data: for
training, validation, and testing. Our datasets also consider a data enrichment
process to help reduce overfitting when training the detection models.
The work presented in this chapter allows researchers to overcome the limita-
tion of a common ground to evaluate and compare novel techniques to the state-
of-the-art and understand how their work improves the current state of intru-
sion detection for microservices. The utilization of an accepted and independent
benchmark along with a well-defined dataset also contributes to the validity of
the findings in research work and reduces validity threats such as the in-house
generation of data and evaluation procedure.
In our roadmap to improve the security of microservices, we leverage the tech-
niques developed and the lessons learned from preliminary studies to design
and develop a framework for continuous and adaptive intrusion detection for
microservice applications, which use CI/CD pipelines. Chapter 8 details our
µSherlock framework, which addresses the practical application of intrusion de-
tection to microservice applications.
156

CHAPTER 8
µSherlock: Continuous Intrusion Detection for Dynamic
Microservices
While the adoption of microservices-based systems offers many benefits, it also
exacerbates concerns about system reliability and security, which are exacerbated
by attacks and the lack of adaptive and autonomous tools that continuously mon-
itor the environment. Solutions that can collect, monitor, and adapt in real-world
deployments are paramount.
In this chapter, we present µSherlock: a continuous monitoring and intrusion
detection framework for dynamic microservice applications. It applies and lever-
ages the research work on techniques and methodologies for intrusion detection,
and integrates them into a practical solution for automating their operation in a
DevSecOps microservices pipeline. We implement the conceptual methodology
into a tool for end-to-end continuous monitoring of microservices in Kubernetes.
The user defines the configuration and integrates the tool into a Continuous In-
tegration and Continuous Delivery (CI/CD) pipeline, so it deploys monitoring
units to collect and process data to identify malicious activity and act accordingly.
µSherlock proactively computes metrics to understand if detection models are
outdated and should be retrained. Evaluation results demonstrate the ability to
perform efficiently and detect intrusions effectively while operating correctly to
ensure the security level. The source code is online (see Appendix A).
This covers research and implementation challenges (Section 8.1), the µSherlock
framework (Section 8.2), the detection and resilience mechanism µSherlock
applies (Section 8.3), the design and implementation of the µSherlock tool (Sec-
tion 8.4), and the evaluation and validation conducted on µSherlock (Section 8.5).
It closes with threats to validity (Section 8.6) and a conclusion (Section 8.7).
157

Chapter 8
8.1 Research and Implementation Challenges
Scalabe and Elastic Environment. The dynamicity in microservice applications
environments demand that intrusion detection approaches be capable of adjust-
ing to their scalability and elasticity characteristics, which allow them to manage
multiple operational profiles. The security level provided by the Intrusion De-
tection System (IDS) needs to accommodate the variations in the operational de-
ployment of the application. This accommodation is already difficult for scalable
behavior but, when the elastic operation is also considered it becomes even more
challenging. Currently, the techniques that learn the behavior of a given oper-
ational deployment are not capable to be effectively applied to variations. Our
research shows, that a possible approach to overcome this challenge is the uti-
lization of data processing techniques to create generalizable detection models.
Fast Delivery and Continuous Changes. The development of microservice ap-
plications leverages CI/CD pipelines, which have contributed immensely to al-
low releasing new features and correcting defects in an easy and fast manner.
Nevertheless, these pipelines incur challenges to security mechanisms that are
based on the runtime behavior of the microservice released [Soldani et al., 2018].
With the continuous modification of services, detection models acquired knowl-
edge may become obsolete resulting in reduced effectiveness in the detection of
malicious activity. At the moment, mechanisms to deal with the impact of these
changes on existing detection models are limited.
Isolate Intrusion Entry Point.After an intrusion is detected, there is still a need to
pinpoint the source of the reported problem. For simple deployments, this step is
straightforward but in microservice applications that have services running with
multiple replicas it may be a cumbersome task. For example, reporting on the
affected service without specifying the replica where the problems are occurring
would lead to a reduced reaction effectiveness, or even lead to enforcement of
very vast actions. Therefore, to deliver an effective mitigation measure against
an intrusion, pinpointing the compromised replica is crucial, even when alarms
are reported at different system components.
8.2 µSherlock: Continuous Intrusion Detection
It is critical to have a well-defined end-to-end framework that continuously mon-
itors and adapts the intrusion detection mechanisms that identify malicious activ-
ity in a microservice application. To address this,µSherlock allows administrators
to automate the monitoring, detection, and response to malicious agents attempt-
ing to infiltrate very large and complex, often business-critical, applications.
Figure 8.1 provides an overview of the µSherlock framework. µSherlock ingests
information from various sources and uses it to continuously monitor the mi-
croservice application and take action based on the circumstances to maintain an
appropriate level of security. µSherlock processes three types of data:

Figure 8.1: Overview of µSherlock framework.
Mentions the µSherlock data sources which are used to conduct intrusion detection,
application monitoring, and adaptation. Also refers the available consumers and the
operations the framework can take.
i) user data, which is provided by the user as the configuration of the mon-
itoring approach, the actions µSherlock should take when an intrusion is
reported, and the cluster location;
ii) deployment data, such as running nodes, running services and their repli-
cas, system call data collected to perform host-based intrusion detection;
iii) source code data and releases of the microservice.
µSherlock continuously monitors applications according to user configurations.
It deploys monitoring units to collect the required information and processes it
according to the needs of the detection models so that alarms can be reported.
For detection, the framework manages the training of detection models based
on the configurations and ensures that the most appropriate detection model is
used for a given time and operational deployment. Over its operation, µSherlock
monitors the execution of the detection models and the operational deployment
of the microservice application to ensure that the models are still up-to-date with
the versions of the microservices or if there has been a significant change in the
behavior of the microservice.
µSherlock ’s actuation component becomes operational when either an alarm is
raised or the monitoring metrics fall below the acceptable threshold. After an
alarm, µSherlock can provide more active (e.g., restarting a service) or more pas-
sive (e.g., intrusion notification) actions, depending on the configuration. If the
monitoring metrics fall below the threshold, µSherlock initiates retraining of the
corresponding detection models. The operation can be monitored using the front-
end UI, the command line application, or a user script using the REST API.
8.2.1 Microservice Information Sources
As a deployment monitoring framework, µSherlock continuously monitors and
ingests deployment data from the configured application. The main source of in-
formation is the system calls that the microservices issue during their execution.
159

Chapter 8
These events are collected along with their timestamps, parameters, thread IDs,
and other information. The framework uses the source code data to understand
the possible impact of the changes introduced in the available detection mod-
els. µSherlock receives information about new microservice versions and decides
whether or not to retrain the existing detection models.
µSherlock also requires data from the user to customize its operation according
to its definition and intended purpose. This can include the configuration of the
cluster, the algorithms to be used, the microservices to be profiled, and the re-
sponse actions to be enabled.
8.2.2 Monitoring and Detection
µSherlock deploys a monitoring unit to collect information from the cluster’s mi-
croservices, and allow it to perform its multiple tasks.
Training and Detection Phases. Training is critical to building effective detec-
tion models. It is paramount to obtain a comprehensive snapshot of the service’s
operational profile. This phase accomplishes two objectives:
i) feed the algorithm with prepared and processed information
ii) define the normal behavior of the microservice
For the first goal, the data is processed according to the processing techniques
and passed to the algorithm. For the second, the algorithm ingests the data, orga-
nizes and stores patterns that make up the service’s operational profile. During
this phase, it is crucial that there are no disturbances from malicious actions, as
these would poison the profiles and lead to false learning, so the data for the
training phase must come from a controlled environment where the application
is exercised with representative loads.
The detection component applies a host-based intrusion detection approach us-
ing system call data and the trained detection models. The system events are
processed accordingly and leverage previous research to increase the usefulness
of the detection models produced.
Processing Techniques. We present three processing techniques to enable the
application of intrusion detection in scalable and elastic microservice applications
(see Section 6.2 for details). The techniques enable knowledge generalization and
allow detection models to be used in multiple operational deployments, helping
to address the challenge of scalability and elasticity. In addition, we present a
set of approaches for processing ingested data to obtain the core behavior of the
microservice (see Section 6.3 for details). These approaches allow the detection
models trained in one version of a microservice to be used for newer versions,
thus contributing to fewer model retrains and IDS resource efficiency.
Reporting Methodology. The reporting approach follows a methodology to re-
duce the number of false alarms raised [Flora et al., 2020, 2023a]. First, an epoch-
based analysis divides the output of the detection models into epochs that are
160

µSherlock: Continuous Intrusion Detection for Dynamic Microservices
classified based on the percentage of anomalies reported. Second, an alarm is
triggered for a configurable time interval if there is an anomalous epoch in a
given interval. The response actions occur after the alarm. This approach avoids
overloading response mechanisms while still providing timely notification.
8.2.3 Adaptation and Actuation
µSherlock performs two main types of adaptation:
i) proactive, based on the Model Reusability Index (MRI)
ii) reactive, based on the alerts of the detection models
These measures contribute to the resilience mechanisms that µSherlock provides
to the microservice applications it monitors. Details in Section 8.3.2.
Proactive Actuation. After calculating and comparing MRI values, the detection
model is retrained for the new microservice version if a certain threshold is not
met. This proactive operation allows µSherlock to keep detection models up to
date with released microservice versions and ensure an appropriate level of secu-
rity. It also allows the system to perform only the training phases that are actually
required, and not waste time and resources on phases that would not result in an
increase in security.
Reactive Actuation. Based on the alerts generated by the detection models,
µSherlock can simply notify administrators of possible malicious actions or take
active measures to increase resilience. For now, µSherlock supports restarting the
service where the malicious activity was identified as a mitigation measure that
terminates the malicious access to the running service. Also, the ability to apply
a moving target defense approach by moving the service to another cluster node
when available [Torkura et al., 2018; Ouffoué et al., 2019].
8.2.4 Consumers
The command line application allows users to perform simple and direct actions
such as configuration, listing, management. For extensibility, a REST API is also
available to allow users to customize and automate some operations through the
use of scripting languages or integration with CI/CD pipelines.
The µSherlock front-end UI provides several views: i) a cluster information page
that allows the user to observe the resources available in the configured cluster;
ii) a detection model management page where the user can analyze the detection
models available in µSherlock ; iii) a traces page that allows the user to list and
manage the collected logs; iv) a real-time analysis page that displays the detection
models used, alarms, monitoring metrics, etc.
161

Chapter 8
8.3 Detection and Resilience Mechanisms
µSherlock processes information from multiple sources to establish baselines for
service behavior and to detect malicious activity. To make detection models more
effective in such dynamic environments, we proposed three data processing tech-
niques that fall into the detection component of µSherlock (see light blue in Fig-
ure 8.1). µSherlock provides proactive and reactive measures based on Model
Reusability Index (MRI) and reported alarms, respectively, to increase the re-
silience of microservice applications. The MRI is detailed below, along with the
actions µSherlock takes based on user configuration when alarms are raised. These
mechanisms allow the framework to increase the resilience of the monitored ap-
plication by adapting and responding appropriately (matching the light green
components shown in Figure 8.1).
8.3.1 Data Processing Techniques
In Section 6.2, we proposed a set of data processing techniques to make detec-
tion models more effective in scalable and elastic environments. The techniques
group events, generalize knowledge, and handle large amounts of data, allowing
algorithms to capture reusable profiles of replicated services and maintain secu-
rity levels as scaling events occur. We assume that each service is deployed in its
own container, allowing for non-intrusive black-box monitoring. The focus is on
host-based intrusion detection by analyzing system call data from each service.
In Section 6.3, we propose a group of techniques that allow intrusion detection
models to be reused across multiple microservice releases. The techniques ex-
tract the core behavior of a microservice using source code and runtime data col-
lected from the microservice. This allows the µSherlock to reduce the number of
detection model retraining and increase the adequate protection even when new
versions of the service are released.
These techniques are lightweight and do not add overhead to the detection
pipeline. For this reason, we have integrated them into µSherlock so that the
detection models created within the tool can be reused for longer and reduce the
overhead of the training phase that would otherwise be necessary.
8.3.2 Resilience Countermeasures
The countermeasures applied by µSherlock increase the resilience of the mon-
itored microservice application by using a custom metric (MRI) developed to
proactively understand when a detection model needs to be retrained for new
microservice releases. µSherlock takes into account techniques for responding to
the detection of attacks. These response techniques are configured by the opera-
tors and can be passive, simply notifying administrators or other systems that a
breach has been detected, or active, modifying the microservice operation either
by restarting the service or moving it to another node in the cluster.
162

µSherlock: Continuous Intrusion Detection for Dynamic Microservices
Model Reusability Index (MRI).
MRI was developed to provide insight into the likelihood of a detection model to
be reused for newer versions of a microservice. Hence, it can be used when a de-
ployment occurs to understand whether detection model retraining should take
place. We implemented MRI into µSherlock as a way to reduce model switches
and training phases. Below MRI is presented, for more details on the metric see
Section 6.4.
MRI =
n
∑
i=1
ωiCi = ω1CD + ω2Al + ω3RI (8.1)
Malicious Activity Notification.
The µSherlock framework can notify other systems or system administrators of
potentially malicious behavior when alarms are raised. Once an intrusion is iden-
tified, the notification is delivered to the configured endpoint to allow for exter-
nal mitigation or further analysis. In this way, µSherlock can be integrated and
extended with third-party methodologies or tools to provide a more comprehen-
sive operation.
Modify Microservice Operation.
µSherlock provides resiliency guarantees for the microservice application being
monitored. Restarting a service can be effective in dealing with non-persistent
threats or other performance issues [Zheng and Trivedi, 2020]. Therefore, after an
alert is raised and the replica causing the problems is identified, the microservice
instance is restarted / replaced with a fresh instance, allowing a possible non-
persistent malicious compromise to be retired.
Another approach provided byµSherlock is to change the target, i.e., the microser-
vice application to be monitored. If restarting the service has already been at-
tempted and the alarms are still being reported on a given microservice, it is
likely that stronger measures are required. For these cases, µSherlock applies a
moving target defense approach based on migrating microservices with a higher
alarm ratio and taking reactive measures against them. By migrating the deploy-
ment to other available nodes in the application cluster, the attack surface can be
modified and the attacker’s effectiveness reduced while other intrusion tolerance
measures, such as diversity, are prepared.
8.4 Design and Implementation
This section focuses on the details of the system design and implementation. It
provides the rationale for the design choices and challenges addressed, as well as
the technologies and components used to implement theµSherlock tool. Figure 8.2
shows the architecture of the µSherlock implementation.

Figure 8.2: Architecture of µSherlock implementation.
µSherlock is designed to use a microservices architecture that automatically deploys a
collector on each worker node of the microservice application cluster, collects data, and
conducts detection of intrusions.
8.4.1 System Design
An implementation of the µSherlock framework needs to respond timely and ef-
fectively to changes in the application(s) it monitors. The tool needs to scale ef-
ficiently and effectively to accompany the microservice application replicas and
services to be monitored. It also needs to store and process large amounts of data,
namely system call data, coming from the different nodes of the application clus-
ter. µSherlock should be able to detect and report intruders in near real time and,
if necessary, respond to them as quickly as possible. So we chose a microservice-
based design that allows for orchestration and easier scalability, along with mes-
sage queues that provide a reliable, scalable, and fault-tolerant means of trans-
mitting information.
µSherlock is designed around core components that operate to provide the in-
tended service. The intrusion detection module that operates the detection mod-
els (training and detection), the processing modules (data storage, application of
data processing approaches, etc.), a monitoring and adaptation module that uses
the custom metrics and alarm data to apply the resilience measures, and the in-
terface modules, the API gateway, the web application, and the interaction with
the cluster via the Kubernetes daemon. The components communicate mainly
over HTTP/REST, which provides lightweight and flexible communication while
strengthening the interoperability and independence between the components.
Some communication channels are based on the publish/subscribe model, which
provides various relevant guarantees in data transfer, such as multiple receivers
for one source of information.
In addition, various services store information using databases, either relational
or non-relational depending on the data types, to persist user and system in-
formation. The implementation can be updated to account for the use of a dis-
tributed file system and the replication of detection models and trace files.
164

µSherlock: Continuous Intrusion Detection for Dynamic Microservices
8.4.2 Implementation
The implementation of µSherlock was mainly done using the FastAPI1 framework
with Python. Each service exposes a REST API to allow communication with
other services. The consumers, a web application and a command line interface,
were developed using Next.js2 and Typer3, respectively. The internals of the tool
are adapted to the different tasks the tool performs (e.g., intrusion detection, data
processing, etc.), which allows to compartmentalize the different system opera-
tions.
The tool uses two message queues using Redis 4: one to distribute the collected
system calls from the cluster and the other to deliver them after processing
by the data processing techniques. To collect the system call data from the
monitored microservice application, µSherlock uses the Kubernetes Python client
(github.com/kubernetes-client/python) to interact with the cluster and deploy a
daemonset from a custom µSherlock collector. A Kubernetes daemonset ensures
that a pod is deployed for each operational node to collect system call data using
a sysdig probe. The daemonset collects the data and forwards it to the Redis
queue.
Some services (e.g., model management engine, detection model manager, metrics en-
gine) use a MongoDB database to store metadata and information relevant to the
operation of the system, such as metric values or intrusion alerts, while the mon-
itor agent service uses a MariaDB database to store metadata and information
about the files stored with the system calls collected from the system. These files
are stored zipped in the file system for further analysis or forensics.
8.5 Evaluation
8.5.1 Experimental Setup
Testbed Setup. We used five machines, as shown in Figure 8.3. µSherlock runs on
an Intel Xeon with 28GB of RAM running Ubuntu 20.04. A load generator (which
interacts with the application and makes the requests) and a master node, both
running Ubuntu 18.04 on an Intel Core i5 with 32GB of RAM. Two worker nodes
running Ubuntu 16.04 and Kernel v4.4.0 on an Intel Core i5 with 64GB of RAM.
We are running Kubernetes v1.23.5 (kubeadm, kubelet, and kubectl) with Docker
v18.06.3.
Microservice Applications. We chose TeaStore [von Kistowski et al., 2018] (v1.3.0
and v1.3.8) and DeathStarBench’s SocialNetwork [Gan et al., 2019]. We used Tom-
cat v7.0.57 with TeaStore. TeaStore simulates an e-commerce store selling tea with
five microservices developed in Java that communicate via REST, while Social-
1FastAPI is a web framework for building Python service APIs (fastapi.tiangolo.com).
2Next.js is a web development framework to create React-based web applications (nextjs.org).
3Typer is a library for building command line applications using Python (typer.tiangolo.com).
4Redis is an in-memory storage, used as a distributed, in-memory database (redis.io).

Figure 8.3: Overview of the evaluation setup.
Network consists of a social network system with unidirectional relationships
that communicate via Thrift RPCs ( thrift.apache.org). For more details on each
testbed see Section 2.2.3
Deployment Scenarios. For evaluation purposes, we defined three deployment
configurations for each application, with different numbers of active service repli-
cas per microservice. Based on the deployment scenarios, the active service repli-
cas vary from 7 to 19 for TeaStore and from 27 to 49 for SocialNetwork. Table 8.1
details the scenarios used for both applications. These deployment scenarios pro-
vide a dynamic and scalable environment, common in microservices, with which
µSherlock must deal effectively.
Workloads. For representativeness, we used the recommended workload profiles
for each application available in TeaStore (github.com/DescartesResearch/TeaStore)
and DeathStarBench (github.com/delimitrou/DeathStarBench) repositories and
adapted them for use with Locust 5. The user behavior profiles consist of
different requests that express e-commerce and social networking activities. The
load intensity associated with the behavior profiles follows various trends such
as constant, quadratic, sinusoidal, cubic, and reciprocal. This allowed us to build
complex and diverse load intensity curves.
Threat Model. To ensure a representative assessment of detection effectiveness,
we adapted the microservices threat model (see Section 3.1) and defined attacker
tactics based on the Mitre Att&ck framework [Strom et al., 2018]. Because we
operate in a container-based environment that uses kernel security features, a
malicious agent might try to target the host with the goal of compromising other
services or tenants within the shared infrastructure. The attacks have three main
targets: i) application service; ii) the virtualization engine; and iii) the host.
5Locust is a load testing framework that simulates user interacting with applications based on
scripted endpoint calls (locust.io).

Vulnerabilities and Attacks. For representativeness, we elicited six vulnerabil-
ities (see Table 8.2) that cover technologies present in different layers of the de-
ployment environment. The vulnerabilities were reported over the years, having
been assigned Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) IDs, from 2017 to
2022, and cover several types: from memory-related issues to improper authen-
tication or command injection. To exploit these vulnerabilities, we collected for
each a Proof of Concept (PoC) from public sources (e.g., ExploitDB, 0day.today).
The services attacked are:WebUI in TeaStore; andnginx-thrift in SocialNetwork.
Metrics. To evaluate the detection effectiveness ofµSherlock, we apply the typical
metrics. Based on the ground truth known from the execution of the experiments,
we define which alarms are true or false negatives or positives, and compute
the following metrics: recall, precision (prec.), f-measure (F-M), and markedness
(Marked.).

8.5.2 Runtime Performance
We designed three 2-hour experiments to evaluate the performance of the
µSherlock implementation and its impact on the performance of the monitored
applications. During each run, the applications adapted to the workload inten-
sity with the following sequence of scenarios 1→2→3→2→1. Scenarios 2 and
3 lasted 30 minutes, and scenario 1 lasted 15 minutes. One run was performed
to establish the performance baseline, and the remaining two were performed
to understand the performance of µSherlock and its impact on the applications,
one for each main mode of operation (training and detection). For each run, we
collected information from the applications on resource usage (CPU, memory)
and executed transactions based on user requests. During the runs withµSherlock
running, we also collected information about the system calls gathered by the
tool and its resource usage (CPU, memory).

We also collected information about resource usage and user request transactions
for each testbed. Figure 8.5 shows the results for each testbed: SocialNetwork
in the top row and TeaStore in the bottom row; the left column shows CPU and
memory usage, while the right column shows successful and failed transactions.
For SocialNetwork, the resource usage analysis shows a slight variation in mem-
ory usage when comparing the data from the recognition phase with the other
two lines (training and no µSherlock). However, this variation is very small and
may not be related to the use of µSherlock, but rather to the fact that the recog-
nition phase runs immediately after the training phase, without a restart in be-
tween. Additionally, this variation is not seen for CPU usage, where the trend
of usage remains the same without significant differences (see Figure 8.5a). Re-
garding the transactions performed by the application, there is no difference or
observable impact of running µSherlock to monitor the application. There are no
failed transactions in any of the runs and the number of successful transactions is
the same across all runs (see Figure 8.5b).
For TeaStore, we also observe no µSherlock impact on resource usage. There are
small differences in the values, but the trend remains the same for memory and
169

Chapter 8
CPU across all runs (see Figure 8.5c). For transactions, we can also see similar
trends across the different phases. TeaStore has some failed transactions when the
scenario changes, but is still able to successfully deliver an average of 40 transac-
tions over the experiments (see Figure 8.5d). µSherlock has no negative impact on
the normal operation or resource usage of the testbeds used.
8.5.3 Detection Effectiveness
During the effectiveness evaluation, we focus on the SocialNetwork testbed and
configure µSherlock to apply the trace events in total order ( τ) data processing
technique as a demonstration of its use. To evaluate the effectiveness of the mod-
els, we designed a two-step detection phase: first, a 10-minute warm-up is per-
formed on the testbed; second, the detection phase (with µSherlock running) runs
for 30 minutes, with an attack injected at the 15-minute mark.
We tested several configurations for the detection models, using two algorithms
(STIDE and BoSC) with two different configurations for each window size (3 and
4). Table 8.3 shows the obtained effectiveness results. The displayed algorithm
label contains the name of the algorithm and the window size used for this con-
figuration. Each detection phase was run twice for each combination of scaling
scenario (Table 8.1) and attack performed (Table 8.2).
The results obtained show that the detection effectiveness of µSherlock can vary
depending on the configuration, which is to be expected, but remains rather high
across the different scenarios. The detection models obtain a very high preci-
sion (median value of 0.958) without compromising the recall (median value of
0.647), which leads to an increased F-Measure (median value of 0.703) for the
presented experiments. Nevertheless, with the increase of the number of active
replicas of the service, we observe that the recall tends to suffer a slight decrease,
as observed in previous work (see Section 6.6). The detection effectiveness of
µSherlock detection models requires a careful analysis of the configuration and
algorithms used to ensure good performance on the system where it is applied.

µSherlock: Continuous Intrusion Detection for Dynamic Microservices
8.5.4 Adaptation and Actuation Effectiveness
µSherlock provides an adaptation measure that allows to understand whether an
existing detection model can be reused for a recently released service version.
Through the use of MRI (see Section 8.3.2), it can understand the reuse potential
of the detection model and decide if changes are required. To demonstrate this
feature, we designed a half-hour non-attack experiment where a new microser-
vice version is released (at 10 minutes) and µSherlock decides how to proceed.
For this experiment, the weights applied in the calculation of MRI are
ω = {0.15, 0.25, 0.60}. Thus, the source code similarity component con-
tributes 15% to the MRI, while the system call alphabet and algorithm patterns
contribute 25% and 60%, respectively. The MRI threshold, below whichµSherlock
decides to retrain the model for the new version, is set at 0.80. This selection
comes from prior work and experimentation (see Section 6.8).
Figure 8.6 depicts the evolution of the percentage of alarms raised by two algo-
rithm configurations over the duration of the experiment. We consider the Bags
of System Calls (BoSC) algorithm configured with window sizes of 3 and 10. Af-
ter the new version is released, µSherlock computes MRI with bosc-3 achieving
0.861 and bosc-10 scoring 0.698.

Figure 8.6: MRI-based decision on the reuse of detection models.
In this scenario, µSherlock decides to keep the bosc-3 model for the new version
and replace the bosc-10 model. However, we also plot the alert rate of the old
model on the chart if µSherlock had not replaced it. We can see that, despite the
false alarms generated by the new model, the old model would have generated
even more false alarms. MRI does not guarantee effective detection rates, but
rather allows the framework to understand that the existing model still provides
a good idea of the behavior of the new version. On the detection effectiveness
side, a size 3 window is clearly a better configuration than a size 10 window,
since it does not even produce false positives.
171

Chapter 8
8.6 Threats to Validity
The experiments conducted to validate and evaluate µSherlock allowed us to bet-
ter understand how the research conducted to improve the effectiveness of intru-
sion detection approaches affects the security of microservices in practice. The
experimental results show no performance impact on the microservices testbeds
used. The implementation of µSherlock also uses few resources to accomplish the
task at hand, while ensuring high detection effectiveness, with different configu-
rations for the STIDE and BoSC algorithms. However, it is worth mentioning that
some validity threats were identified during the execution of the experiments.
In terms of internal validity, our experiments were carefully designed and con-
ducted in a controlled laboratory environment. We ensured that no external fac-
tors could influence the results. However, it is important to acknowledge the
presence of certain threats to external validity.
Detection models effectiveness is influenced by configurations applied. The
effectiveness of the detection models is affected by the quality of the data col-
lected and the behavior of the system being monitored. The configurations that
produce good results in our experiments may not be the best for other types of
systems. However, this does not invalidate the proposed approach or the ability
of µSherlock to protect microservice systems. However, if used, careful configura-
tion tuning and preliminary experimentation is recommended to ensure that the
best configuration is used for the case at hand.
Attacks used to demonstrate practical application may be skewed towards Ker-
nel vulnerabilities. The attacks considered in the experiments may appear to be
biased towards kernel-level vulnerabilities. However, the set of attacks targets
real-world vulnerabilities found in widely used systems, with different layers of
the infrastructure having multiple classes of vulnerabilities, providing guaran-
tees of representativeness. In addition, the experiments could be performed on
a wider range of testbed applications to increase generalizability. Nevertheless,
we conducted the experiments on two well-known microservice applications that
are widely used and representative of different types of applications. This gives
us a level of generalization that is substantial for the work presented.
8.7 Conclusion
In this section, we summarize the contributions and lessons learned from design-
ing and implementing a continuous intrusion detection approach for complex
and dynamic microservice applications. µSherlock leverages previous research on
techniques, metrics, and methodologies to improve intrusion detection in scal-
able, elastic, and ever-changing microservice environments.
A framework for continuous intrusion detection for dynamic microservice en-
vironments. The framework provides continuous, autonomous, and adaptive
intrusion detection for dynamic microservice applications. It provides improved
172

µSherlock: Continuous Intrusion Detection for Dynamic Microservices
effectiveness of intrusion detection models according to the active operational
use of the application.
Application of techniques and metrics from the research work conducted. We
apply the previous research techniques to non-intrusively process data collected
from scalable and elastic microservice applications, and use custom metrics and
tolerance mechanisms to identify malicious actions. It integrates them into a prac-
tical solution to automate their operation in a DevSecOps microservices pipelines.
The application of proactive and reactive measures for increased security of mi-
croservice applications. The application of adaptive measures allows the frame-
work to provide and enforce increased effectiveness not only in detecting, but
also in responding to attacks perpetrated against microservice applications. To
support this operation, we use a custom metric that allows us to decide whether
a detection model should be retrained given the changes introduced to each mi-
croservice deployment.
µSherlock: A scalable tool for continuous intrusion detection and practical
demonstration. We implement a µSherlock tool to demonstrate the practical use
of the framework and apply it in various deployment scenarios using two mi-
croservice testbeds that have known vulnerabilities, scalable and elastic behav-
ior, and integrate source code changes over time. The tool can be integrated into
a CI/CD pipeline to receive the changes made to the microservices and provide
the intended level of security regardless of the operational changes.
Chapter 9 summarizes the lessons learned and the main contributions of the work
presented in this document, along with possible research paths to further explore.
173

CHAPTER 9
Conclusions and Future Work
The importance of security mechanisms is increasing, and organizations need to
adapt their security posture to reduce the risk of exposure. During this thesis,
we contributed to increase the security level of microservice applications, which
have a growing trend of adoption empowered by developers and cloud services.
This chapter summarizes the lessons learned and suggest possible research and
development directions for the coming years.
9.1 Conclusions
The work presented in this thesis advances the state-of-the-art on the security of
microservice applications by providing techniques and methodologies to evalu-
ate and compare intrusion detection models, improve the detection capacity of
existing detection algorithms, and propose a framework to ensure continuous in-
trusion detection for Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery (CI/CD)
pipelines. This thesis started with an introduction of the security challenges of
microservice applications. The identified challenges arise from a review and
study of the state of the art, which allowed us to find existing gaps where work
is required to increase the security level of microservice applications. The chal-
lenges and security weaknesses of distributed applications followed the defini-
tion of the threat model considered during the experimental work conducted to
validate and evaluate the studies conducted, and the techniques proposed to in-
crease the security of microservice applications.
To determine the current state of security and fault tolerance capabilities designed
and currently deployed in microservice environments, we conducted a study that
uncovered the problems with fault tolerance mechanisms in a very popular mi-
croservice orchestrator, Kubernetes. The study showed that Kubernetes Probes
175

Chapter 9
are not only unable to detect the aging effects on microservices that our study
shows occur, but also have shortcomings in detecting and responding to different
types of failures in a microservice application. Thus, work is required to improve
current approaches for detecting and responding to failures in microservices.
To understand the feasibility of host-based intrusion detection for microservices,
we conducted an applicability and effectiveness study. This study first evalu-
ated the capacity to produce usable detection models based on system calls col-
lected from a container - the common deployment of microservices - and showed
promising results, with several algorithms and configurations able to converge
to usable detection models. Further work in the same study evaluated the ef-
fectiveness of detection models built with the same configuration across different
deployment methods and showed that Docker containers allow detection models
to deliver higher detection rates with fewer false positives. This is an interesting
finding since Docker is the most popular containerization platform for deploying
microservice applications [Carter, 2021].
We pursued the lead in intrusion detection by proposing techniques and metrics
that allow detection models to achieve higher detection effectiveness while ad-
dressing several of the previously identified security challenges. Specifically, we
proposed a set of data processing approaches that allow detection models built
to increase their effectiveness in scalable and elastic microservice environments.
These techniques provide detection models with the ability to generalize knowl-
edge and detect attacks in different operational deployments. We also proposed
a set of techniques that ensure that detection models capture the core behavior
of a microservice and simultaneously address two objectives. They allow detec-
tion models to focus on core behavior patterns without losing detection effective-
ness, while increasing the likelihood of model reusability across different releases
of the same microservice. To allow developers and researchers to understand
whether a detection model is more or less likely to be reused, we proposed a met-
ric (Model Reusability Index (MRI)) to measure this factor. MRI uses source code
and microservice runtime data to provide an objective score of the likelihood that
a detection model will be reused for a new version of the service.
To address the shortcomings of methodologies for evaluation of intrusion detec-
tion approaches in microservice environments, we developed a benchmarking
approach that takes into account their characteristics. The proposed benchmark
takes into account dynamic, scalable, and elastic environments by leveraging a
900-hour dataset with three types of data (training, testing, validation) gener-
ated during the work of this thesis following a generation methodology we also
proposed. The dataset considers several representative microservice applications
and attacks that are exercised over long hours with representative and varied
workloads. We applied this benchmarking approach and provided a comprehen-
sive evaluation of state-of-the-art algorithms across three different case studies.
All the lessons learned during the execution of the work presented in this thesis
have contributed to the µSherlock framework. This framework achieves the goal
of continuous and adaptive intrusion detection that aims to evolve with microser-
vice applications and provide a constant level of security regardless of changes
and updates. The framework was implemented in a tool that is validated and
176

Conclusions and Future Work
tested in an operational microservice environment, where it continuously and
autonomously adapts to the changes in the application by applying the appropri-
ate detection model and deciding whether to retrain the existing models.
Over the execution of the work, we have made available online several resources,
such as the dataset, the source code of the tools developed, and experimental
campaign results, the links to which we have compiled in Appendix A.
9.2 Future Work
Based on the results and lessons learned from the work conducted, we propose
several research directions for the future:
Expand and maintain dataset: The dataset needs to be maintained and updated
to include different testbeds and emerging attacks observed in microservices. It
is important to ensure that the dataset continues to evolve to provide up-to-date
evaluations and fair comparisons of target technologies.
Develop a metric to characterize the health of microservices: Create a novel
approach for quantifying the ability of systems to tolerate intrusions needs to
be explored. A quantitative and meaningful metric that provides the health of
a microservice based on several factors, such as the age of a system (systems
become more susceptible to software failures due to aging [Avizienis et al., 2004]),
its known vulnerabilities, data from health checks (e.g., Kubernetes Probes), and
attacks or intrusion attempts. The metric could be continuously calculated and
monitored, allowing the application of proactive measures to increase its level.
Explore improvements of detection models for multi-target attacks:Research is
needed to take advantage of fusion techniques to improve detection effectiveness
by incorporating the behavior of different services during the training phase, or
by achieving consensus among multiple detection instances for different services.
These techniques can counter multi-target attacks that exploit interdependent mi-
croservices to simultaneously target multiple services within a system, advancing
the state of the art.
Develop better data representations to improve ML-based intrusion detection:
Research is needed to enable the use of ML in intrusion detection for microser-
vices by processing the information produced in a way to leverage possibly hid-
den connections, which are not easily identified. For instance, to apply ML-based
techniques for host-based intrusion detection in microservices, it is imperative
that relationships between commonly executed system calls be represented in on-
tologies or similar. This representation should reflect similarities between system
calls that manipulate the same information or perform similar operations (e.g.,
network or file related). Only in this way will it be possible to take full advan-
tage of distance-based ML algorithms to achieve better results and make efforts
to increase the effectiveness of complex detection models.
Expand and improve automation of µSherlock: The µSherlock implementation
is in its early stages. The tool and framework need to evolve and be updated to
177

Chapter 9
improve its fault tolerance capacity and to extend the support for algorithms and
configurations to provide more customizable and diverse detection models.
Research and develop Intrusion Detection System (IDS) protection measures
to improve their resiliency: Research is needed to evaluate, analyze, and pro-
pose intrusion-tolerance techniques that can be introduced in the design of IDSs
(e.g., redundancy, multiple information sources) that allow making an IDS more
resistant to attacks while still producing satisfactory results. Explore the possibil-
ity of protecting IDS execution by using Trusted Execution Environments (TEE),
which has been used in other domains with promising results.
Wider integration of intrusion tolerance/reaction approaches in µSherlock: In-
vestigate the application of intrusion tolerance to microservices to provide them
with the ability to guarantee reliable and trustworthy services even in the pres-
ence of security breaches. Explore proactive reinstantiation measures for services
without reducing system availability, as well as geographic replication for redi-
recting service traffic and overcoming security breaches without compromising
service security attributes (confidentiality, integrity, availability).
Model the decision process that applies intrusion reaction mechanisms: Cre-
ate a composite approach to model the application of reaction mechanisms. The
model should take into account the microservice’s criticality, health, and moni-
toring scores to define the most appropriate reaction to identified attacks or in-
trusions. In this way, the system responsible for reacting to security incidents
would be able to consider the many characteristics of a service (e.g., relevance to
business objective, criticality of data) along with its historical information (e.g.,
previous intrusion attempts, failures) to apply the most effective reaction to miti-
gate possible propagation of the security breach.

APPENDIX A
Resources Available Online
The work presented in this thesis is publicly available in several online reposito-
ries. This appendix collects them for easier access.
Intrusion Detection Dataset (Section 7.4)
https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.10655126
A dataset for intrusion detection in microservice applications generated using
state-of-the-art technologies used in three different microservice applications
with three different types of data: train, validation, and testing, with a total of
900 hours of recorded data.
The source code for the tools developed in this work are available at:
µSherlock (Chapter 8)
https://github.com/jeflora/sherlock
A tool that applies the µSherlock framework and demonstrates its practical ap-
plication. The tool can integrate into a CI/CD pipeline to receive the changes
made to the microservices and deliver the intended security level regardless of
the operational modifications.
µDetector (Flora et al. [2023b])
https://github.com/micro-sec/detector
µDetector is an intrusion detection tool for microservice-based applications, writ-
ten in Python. This tool uses intrusion detection techniques from previous re-
search and automates them for Kubernetes and KubeEdge deployments.
This tool was developed in the context of Miguel Teixeira’s master thesis, whose
work was co-advised by the candidate. The tool applied the initial techniques
and intrusion detection approaches developed in the context of this Ph.D. work.
195

Appendix A
Some experimental results obtained are available at:
Evaluation of Intrusion Detection in Containers (Section 5.3)
https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.c.5084945
These experiments allowed us to evaluate and compare different intrusion detec-
tion algorithms.
Detection in Scalable Environments (Section 6.6)
https://figshare.com/s/08dbdd5bac7798beb8d9
The evaluation demonstrates the performance improvements resulting from the
utilization of our proposed data processing techniques in scalable microservice
environments.
